Setup is not a stage execution of its own: it runs as the first step of the activity it prepares, in the same sandbox instance with the same home and target directory, under `setup.timeout_secs`. A setup failure fails that activity with category `infrastructure`.
- **WS-5.** Run directories are removed 24 hours after a terminal state *(tunable)*; `target/` and exports are removed earlier (9.3).
- **WS-6.** The workspace tree MUST derive from the previous candidate. After a base update (12.4) the host discards `workspace/` and re-creates it from the update commit, so a later round can never revert what landed upstream.
- **WS-7.** The host resolves the toolchain that `rust-toolchain.toml` names at the base commit to a directory on the host, and puts that directory's `bin` first on the worker `PATH` (D-42). rustup and mise shims are never on the worker `PATH`: run in a directory that holds a `rust-toolchain.toml`, rustup 1.29.3 downloaded and installed the toolchain that the file named, for a command that only asked rustup's version (P-39). A missing toolchain, component or target is a blocking onboarding failure, and after onboarding it stops a claim as a missing check tool does (WS-7a).
  - **The file.** It is the file of that exact name at the root of the tree, read as data from the object database (INV-2). The file `rust-toolchain`, which rustup also reads, is not read. The host reads the table `[toolchain]` and three keys of it: `channel`, `components` and `targets`. A fourth, `profile`, may stand there and is tested for nothing: it names what rustup would install, and the host installs nothing. `channel` is a full version, three numbers written in digits with a dot between them, as `1.100.0`. The host compares no version: it looks for a directory of that name. A component's name is made of lowercase letters, digits and hyphens and begins with a letter; a target's name may hold a dot and an underscore as well. The host cannot resolve a file that is not valid TOML, that has no `[toolchain]` table or no `channel` in it, that holds another table or key, a `path` among them, or that holds a channel, a component or a target of another form. A tree without the file names no toolchain. Each of these counts as a missing toolchain, and the host says what is wrong with the file where it would name the toolchain. rustup itself takes a file without a `channel`, by its manual (P-39).
  - **Why a full version.** `stable`, `1.100` and a nightly are channels to rustup. It gave `1.100` and `stable` a directory each, beside that of `1.100.0`, and on that day all three held the compiler of 1.100.0 (P-39). What the first two hold changes when the operator next updates them, with no change in any repository. A dated channel, `nightly-2026-11-20` for one, names one build as a full version does. It was not taken with this decision, and no such directory was observed.
  - **The directory.** Operator configuration names one toolchains directory for the `rust` ecosystem, `[ecosystem.rust] toolchains_dir` (16.4). It has no default (CFG-4), and the factory's user needs to read it and nothing more. The host looks for the toolchain at `<toolchains directory>/<channel>-<host triple>`. The host triple is `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` or `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu`, by the architecture that `sf` was built for (4.4). That is the name rustup gives a toolchain's directory, below the directory `toolchains` of its home (P-39); its manual gives the name of a toolchain and not where one lies. `RUSTUP_HOME=<dir> rustup toolchain install <channel> --profile minimal`, with `--component` for each component and `--target` for each target, therefore leaves the layout, and the key names `<dir>/toolchains`. The host runs neither rustup nor a program of the toolchain to find it: the version is what the path says, as for a check tool. A key that names a rustup home was not taken, since the host reads one directory of such a home. A layout of the factory's own, `<channel>` alone as WS-7a has `<name>/<version>`, was not taken either: no tool leaves it.
  - **What is present.** The toolchain is present when its directory holds `bin/rustc` and `bin/cargo`, each a regular file that the factory's user may execute, and the file `lib/rustlib/components` in it holds the line `rust-std-<host triple>`. The toolchains directory is the operator's, so the host follows a symbolic link there, at the toolchain's directory or below it, and the sandbox shows the directory that the link leads to. That file is rustup's record of what a toolchain holds, one name to a line. A component is present when the file holds its name or its name with `-preview` after it, each with `-<host triple>` at the end or without: `clippy` stood there as `clippy-preview-aarch64-apple-darwin` and `rust-src` as `rust-src` (P-39). A target is present when the file holds `rust-std-<target>`. `rustup component remove` took a component's line and its programs away together, and `rustup target remove` the line and the libraries (P-39). A table in the code that names a file for each component was not taken, since a component that the table lacks could not be resolved. Testing the directory alone was not taken either: a missing component would then show as a failed verify round and not as a refused claim. The pages of rustup's manual that were read do not mention the file (P-39). Where a later rustup writes it otherwise, a component reads as missing, and the claim stops.
  - **What a worker is shown.** The sandbox shows the directory of that one toolchain read-only (SEC-6) and no other part of the toolchains directory. With the `bin` of a copy of a toolchain first on a cleared `PATH`, a crate was built, tested, linted and checked by rustfmt: the copy lay under another path and name, with no write permission on any entry, there was no rustup, and `HOME` and `CARGO_HOME` were elsewhere (P-39). `cargo` is then the toolchain's own program. It took no notice of a `rust-toolchain.toml` beside the crate that named another version, and it refused `cargo +1.97.1` as an unknown command. A candidate that changes the file is therefore built with the toolchain of its base commit all the same, and the change is flagged (11.3). The file that the next claim after the merge reads is the new one.
  - **Where the code lies.** What the file names is a pure function of the `rust` implementation of `Ecosystem` (9.5): from the bytes of the file and the host triple to the name of the directory and the list of what it has to hold, or to what is wrong with the file. `factory-daemon` looks on disk (19.1). M0 builds both, since `sf sandbox exec` resolves a toolchain (CLI-6).
  - **Not observed.** P-39 ran on macOS, with rustup 1.29.3 and the toolchain 1.100.0. No toolchain was looked for on Linux, and none was shown to a sandbox. The cold build of M0's exit check is the first (BOOT-3).
- **WS-7a.** A repository's setup commands and checks run programs that are no part of its toolchain: `cargo-nextest`, `cargo-deny` or `typos`, for example. Each is a check tool, and it reaches a worker as the toolchain does (D-27).
  - **The policy names them.** `[tools]` in the policy (16.2) holds one key per check tool, its name, with one exact version as the value. There is no range and no "latest". A name is made of lowercase letters, digits and hyphens and begins with a letter. A version is made of letters, digits, dots, hyphens and `+` and begins with a letter or a digit. A policy with any other name or version is invalid (16.3), because both become directory names on the host.
  - **The operator installs them.** The host installs nothing: it neither downloads nor builds a check tool. Operator configuration names one tools directory, `tools_dir` (16.4). It has no default, since no home directory is built in (CFG-4), and the factory's user needs to read it and nothing more. The host looks for a check tool at `<tools directory>/<name>/<version>/bin`, and the tool is present when that `bin` directory exists and holds at least one executable file. For a tool that is a crate, `cargo install --locked --version <version> --root <tools directory>/<name>/<version> <crate>` leaves that layout. The host does not run a tool to ask its version: the version is what the path says. `factory-daemon` makes the test, as it makes that of the toolchain (WS-7, 19.1).
  - **One set, wherever the toolchain goes.** A sandbox that is given the toolchain is given the check tools. The host puts each `bin` on the worker `PATH` after the toolchain's, in order of name, and the sandbox shows the directory `<name>/<version>` of each named tool read-only (SEC-6) and no other part of the tools directory. From M2 the guest image holds them too (SEC-9). There is one set and no list per role: the implementer has the check tools that verify has. A repository whose implementer is to run more checks than its verify list names more check tools.
  - **A missing one stops the claim.** A check tool that the policy names and the host lacks is a blocking onboarding failure (20.1), and `sf doctor` reports it (CLI-1). A policy merged after onboarding can name a version that the host does not hold, as a change to `rust-toolchain.toml` can name a toolchain. Step 1 of the claim therefore resolves the toolchain and the check tools of the commit it reads (8.2). Where one is missing the factory claims no issue on that repository, and it names each missing tool with its version, or the toolchain or component, when it refuses. A run takes up a newer policy or base commit at a reset or a base update (16.3). An activity of that run whose sandbox cannot be given the toolchain or a check tool fails as a Deterministic failure and escalates with `infrastructure` (8.6).
- **WS-10.** `host.git` is created at claim as a bare, single-branch clone of the mirror's default branch (`--no-local`), with its remote removed. Before every test-merge, base update or rebuild the host fetches the default branch (and, on rebuild, this run's work branch) from the mirror into it. It holds only objects reachable from the default branch and from this run's candidates. A workspace made from a commit that is not on the default branch (WS-6, 12.4, 8.4) is cloned from `host.git` at a temporary ref on that commit in the same way as WS-1; the temporary ref is deleted afterwards.

Linked worktrees are not used: they share refs, config and hooks with the hub repository, which conflicts with INV-2.

### 9.3 Build output

- `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` is outside the workspace. Implementer stage executions of one attempt share `runs/<run_id>/target`. `plan`, `author_tests`, `verify` and `review` each get a new, empty target directory on every execution, so a build there is cold each time. That cost is accepted; CI is the second clean-environment gate.
- `target/`, per-execution target directories and exports are deleted when the run enters `human_review` or a terminal state.
- No cache writable by a worker is shared between runs, or between the implementer and any other stage. `sccache` is not supported in v1. Seeding target directories from a host-built copy-on-write clone MAY be added after probe P-13 measures it.

### 9.4 Capture: building the candidate

Capture runs after the implementer's sandbox instance is destroyed and the process sweep (AG-18) is empty.

1. **Scan (host, `lstat` only, never following symlinks).** Fail if any entry other than the root `.git` has a name that git would treat as `.git` (case-insensitive, including filesystem-equivalent spellings), at any depth; or if any entry is not a regular file, directory or symlink. What git treats as `.git` is its rule for that one name and not the fold of VAL-5d, which knows no spelling of HFS+ or NTFS (P-29). The root `.git` is not entered. The scan covers ignored directories, and it records the type and size of every entry. It runs before any git command, because git leaves some of these entries out of its listings without a message (an untracked FIFO or socket, a `.git` entry it does not take for a repository, anything in an ignored directory), and git blocks when `.gitignore` is a FIFO (P-7).
2. **Stage (sandboxed git).** `quarantine/` is a repository the host creates anew for each capture: an empty object directory with `host.git`'s objects as an alternate, and a host-written configuration (hooks, filters, fsmonitor, system and global config all disabled; no in-tree attributes, WS-11). The host's git binary runs with `quarantine/` as its repository and the workspace as its work tree, in new sandbox instances with no network and no credentials, under the filesystem policy of SEC-6 for capture. Everything the commands of this step share is in `quarantine/`. The staging git builds a fresh index from the tree the workspace was last created or captured from (the previous candidate's tree, `base_sha` on the first round, or W's tree in a merge-conflict round, 12.4), so upstream content is never counted as the round's change set. The index file lives in `quarantine/`; nothing in `workspace/.git` is read (WS-3). It then lists the change set: changed and deleted tracked paths with `git diff-files`, submodules ignored, and untracked paths with `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard`. The ignore rules are therefore the `.gitignore` files in the workspace as they stand at capture, the agent's edits included, and nothing else: `quarantine/` has no `info/exclude`, no global excludes file is configured, and the workspace's own `info/exclude` is not read. An ignore rule never drops a tracked path. The host checks the listing against the inventory of step 1, without touching the workspace again. It rejects any listed path that is neither a regular file nor a symlink (git lists a nested repository as its directory) or exceeds 5 MiB, and any change set over the total-bytes cap. If nothing is rejected, the staging git removes the deleted paths from the index (`git update-index --force-remove`), adds the others (`git update-index --add`) and lists the change set again. A path still listed was not staged, and that fails the capture: `update-index` skips a path it refuses and still exits 0. The staging git then writes a tree and emits the objects it wrote as one pack on its standard output (`git pack-objects --local`). A rejected path fails the capture; the host never builds a candidate that silently omits one.
3. **Ingest (host, bare `host.git`).** The host takes two things from the sandbox, the name of the tree and the pack, and opens nothing in `quarantine/`. It indexes the pack into `ingest/`, a new object directory with `host.git`'s objects as an alternate, using `git index-pack --strict` with `--max-input-size` set from the total-bytes cap. That recomputes the name of every object, runs the `fsck` checks, and fails if the pack refers to an object that is neither in the pack nor in `host.git`. The host then walks the new tree and rejects: any entry whose mode is not `100644`, `100755` or `120000`, so submodule entries cannot enter a candidate; two paths that have the same fold, unless the base tree holds both (WS-12); a path in which the new tree differs from the base tree and which lies below a directory with the layout of a git repository (WS-13); and, by the sizes of the blobs that differ from the previous tree, any file or change set over the capture limits. Only then does the pack move into `host.git`. After a rejection `ingest/` is deleted and `host.git` is unchanged.
4. **Commit.** `git commit-tree` with the previous published head (or `base_sha`) as parent. From a conflicting base update until the next push, the parents are instead W's two parents (previous published head, new default-branch head), never W itself, so the default-branch head stays an ancestor of the work branch. The commit carries the factory identity, journalled dates, the validated subject and the host-written body and trailers. Journal the candidate and tree SHA.

**Capture limits** *(tunable)*: 5 MiB per file, 50 MiB of new or changed bytes per candidate, and a 300 s timeout for the sandboxed staging step. They are keys of operator configuration, `[limits.capture]`, and these values are the defaults (16.4). A policy of version 1 has no key for them and lowers none (CFG-5). A capture timeout is an abandoned activity (8.6, Poison).

**Subject validation.** The subject is a single line of at most 72 characters without control characters. It is rejected, returning to `implement`, if it contains (case-insensitively) `[skip ci]`, `[ci skip]`, `[no ci]`, `[skip actions]`, `[actions skip]` or `skip-checks`, or anything GH-5a forbids. The forms of a reference are those of the encoder's module in `factory-core`, which M1a builds with capture (GH-5a, D-33). The pull request title and the squash title are generated by the host from the issue title, after the sanitisation of SEC-4, with every reference (GH-5a) removed (skip tokens are already excluded by the lint, 6.4); the squash message is host-written (5.6).

**Export.** `verify` and `review` use a clean checkout of the candidate made from `host.git`: `git --git-dir=host.git read-tree <candidate>` into a temporary index, then `git --git-dir=host.git --work-tree=runs/<run_id>/exec/<seq>/export checkout-index -a` into that new, empty, host-created directory (bare `host.git` has no work tree of its own, and `checkout-index` needs one), with no filter configured and no attribute applied (WS-11; left to itself, `checkout-index` applies the candidate's own `.gitattributes`). `git archive` MUST NOT be used, because it honours `export-ignore` from the candidate's own `.gitattributes`. The export is writable for `verify` and read-only for `review`. It belongs to one stage execution, and no export is used a second time (WS-14).

**Publish.** Push the journalled candidate to `refs/heads/factory/issue-<n>` over HTTPS. The installation token reaches `git push` through a credential helper built into `sf` that receives it over an inherited pipe; it never appears in arguments, environment or a file (INV-1). Then create the draft pull request if no open one exists for the head branch, otherwise update its body, and report the three factory checks for the head SHA.

- **WS-8.** All git mutations run through the `git` CLI in one wrapper crate that parses only porcelain or NUL-delimited output. `gix` MAY be used for read-only fast paths. `git2` is not used. Fetches into a mirror are serialised per repository. Every git command of the host names its repository, with `--git-dir` or with the path that `init` and `clone` take, and none finds a repository from its working directory. The host's commands therefore work on a bare repository under the setting of WS-15.
- **WS-9.** The capture, ingest and export design MUST be prototyped in M0 against a hostile-tree suite before the pipeline depends on it: nested repositories (directory, gitfile, `.GIT` case variant) carrying `core.fsmonitor`; hooks and config; symlinked directories; FIFOs and hard links; `.gitattributes` with filters, `export-ignore` and `-diff`; an agent-edited `.gitignore`; LFS pointers; case collisions, which are two paths with one fold (WS-12); a directory with the layout of a git repository under a name other than `.git` (WS-13); executable bits; large files; an object from another run's branch must not be readable in a fresh workspace. Probe P-7 did this for the git commands of this section, without the factory's sandbox, and added cases: a `.git` entry that names no repository; a special file or a nested repository in an ignored directory; a FIFO named `.gitignore`; a tracked directory replaced by a symlink; attributes that change bytes (`eol`, `ident`, `working-tree-encoding`), in the base commit and in the round; and a hostile pack or tree name in place of the staging git's output. It left two cases open, which D-43 decided: its prototype rejected two names that differ only in case with a check that this section did not hold, and it admitted a directory with the layout of a bare repository. The suite asserts WS-12 and WS-13 for the two, with the trees and the layouts of probe P-40: a pair that a round adds is rejected and a pair that the base tree holds is not, and a path that a round changes below a layout is rejected whether the round or the base tree brought the layout in.
- **WS-11.** No in-tree attribute is applied when content moves between a tree and a directory. The workspace checkout (WS-1), staging and the export run with the attribute source set to the empty tree, as the diffs of VAL-5a do. Each therefore moves bytes unchanged: an untouched workspace is an empty change set, and every path of a candidate has in the export the bytes it had in the workspace. The source is set with the `--attr-source` option and not with the `GIT_ATTR_SOURCE` variable alone, because a git too old to know the option rejects it but ignores the variable and applies the attributes. The cost is that `text`, `eol`, `ident` and `working-tree-encoding` do nothing in a workspace, a candidate or an export: a file the agent writes with CRLF line endings is committed with them (P-7).
- **WS-12.** Two paths of a tree that have the same fold are rejected, unless the base tree holds both (D-43). The paths are those of every entry of the tree, at any depth and of any kind: a file, a symbolic link or a directory. The fold is that of VAL-5d. The base tree is the tree of the run's `base_sha`, the default-branch commit that the workspace derives from (8.2), which a reset and a base update replace (7.6, 12.4). In the round that resolves the conflicts of a base update it is already the tree of the new default-branch head (AG-11a). Ingest makes the test on the tree of every capture (step 3), and the host makes it on the tree of every test-merge and of every base update (12.3, 12.4). A rejection at ingest returns to `implement` as any rejected path does (7.3), with both paths in the feedback.
  - **Why.** git has no such rule. It staged two such names (P-7), and `fsck --strict` and `index-pack --strict` accepted each of seven such trees (P-40). On Linux an export held both paths of each. On a file system that is not case-sensitive it cannot. On the operator's Mac the export of `README.md` beside `readme.md` held one file, and `checkout-index` exited with 1; the directories `src` and `SRC` became one directory that held the files of both, with exit status 0; and a clone of such a branch left `git status` reporting a change in a work tree that nobody had touched (P-40). What verify and CI build on Linux is then not what a checkout on such a machine holds. VAL-5d already takes the two for one path.
  - **Which pairs.** The export on the Mac held one file as well where only full case folding or normalisation makes two names one: `straße.txt` beside `STRASSE.txt`, `é.md` as one code point beside the same as two, and `AGENTS.md` beside that name with a long s (P-40). APFS gave the answer of the fold in each of the 21 pairs of P-29. A comparison that folds the letters A to Z alone, as git's does (P-29), was not taken. Admitting what git admits was not taken either.
  - **Paths, and not the names of one directory.** Where a tree holds the directories `src` and `SRC`, the files `src/a.rs` and `SRC/a.rs` are one file on the Mac, whose export held one `a.rs` (P-40). A rule that compared the names inside each directory would admit the second file wherever the base tree holds the two directories.
  - **What the base tree holds.** A pair is admitted when the base tree has an entry at each of its two paths, whatever the run did to either. A repository whose default branch holds such a pair therefore fails no capture, and a round may change both files. A rule over the whole tree was not taken: claim and onboarding would then have to refuse such a repository, as they refuse one with a submodule entry (8.2, 20.1). The readiness report of onboarding lists each such pair of the tree at the default-branch head as an advisory item (20.1).
  - **The base tree, and not the previous tree.** The operator was asked with the previous tree of step 2 in this place. The two give the same answer but in two cases. A pair of the base tree of which an earlier round removed one path may come back. And after a base update that conflicted, the previous tree is that of W (12.4), which holds a pair that the merge made of one path of the run and one of the default branch. Against the previous tree that pair would be admitted in the round that follows. The base tree is by then the tree of the new default-branch head, which does not hold it.
  - **A name that is not valid UTF-8** is folded as VAL-5d folds such a path, with U+FFFD in the place of each ill-formed sequence. Two paths that differ only in such bytes are rejected as one, which is a rejection too many.
  - **Where the rule lies.** The fold is the function of VAL-5d, and the walk of a tree is capture's, in `factory-git` (19.1).
- **WS-13.** A run brings no git repository into a candidate under a name other than `.git`, and it changes none that the base tree holds (D-43). A directory has the layout of a repository when it holds an entry named `HEAD` together with entries named `objects` and `refs`, or together with an entry named `commondir`. An entry counts whatever it is: a file, a directory or a symbolic link. Paths are compared by the fold of VAL-5d, so the entries of two directories count as those of one where the paths of the two have the same fold. The host rejects every path in which a tree differs from the base tree (WS-12), whether the path was added, changed or removed, that lies below a directory which has the layout in that tree, or which has it in the base tree and still holds an entry in that tree. The root of a tree is such a directory like any other. Ingest makes the test on the tree of every capture (step 3), and the host makes it on the tree of every test-merge and of every base update (12.3, 12.4). The host reads the paths from the two trees (VAL-5a), and not from the listing of step 2. A rejection at ingest returns to `implement` as any rejected path does (7.3).
  - **Why.** git that is started in such a directory of a checkout, or anywhere below it, takes the directory for a repository and reads its configuration. In each layout that it took and that had a `config`, git ran an alias that the `config` named, and in each but the one with `commondir` it ran the fsmonitor of that `config` on `git status`: in the work tree and in a clone of it, on git 2.56.1 and on git 2.47.3 (P-40). The scan of step 1 refuses a name that git treats as `.git` and no layout, and git staged the files of such a directory like any others (P-7). A subverted implementer could so add what looks like the fixture of a test. Once merged, the command runs as whoever next starts git in that directory, on a checkout that holds that person's credentials.
  - **Why these names.** The rule is git's test for a git directory as it was observed. git took a directory for a repository where it held `HEAD` with `objects` and `refs`, also where those two were symbolic links or regular files of mode `100755`, and where it held `HEAD` with a file `commondir` that named a directory holding the two. It needed no `config` for that. It took none where `refs` was missing, where `HEAD` held another word, or where `objects` and `refs` were files of mode `100644` (P-40). The rule reads names, and neither the content of `HEAD` nor a mode, so it rejects the last two as well: a rejection too many. It reads a tree, and git tracks no empty directory. A directory of the base tree that lacks an entry of the layout, and gains it when a test or a person creates an empty `refs`, is no layout to this rule, and a run may change its `config`.
  - **Why the fold.** The Mac's file system took `head`, `Objects` and `REFS` for the three names. A tree with `fix/HEAD` in one directory and `FIX/config`, `FIX/objects` and `FIX/refs` in another became one directory in an export on the Mac, and git ran the alias of its `config` there (P-40). On Linux neither is a repository, and the candidate is checked out on the Mac all the same.
  - **The root.** In a checkout git finds the `.git` of the root before it tests the root itself, and it took the outer repository there. A copy of a tree without its `.git` has no such cover, and an export is such a copy. In one that was made with `cp`, git took the root for a repository and ran the alias, also from a subdirectory (P-40). The root therefore counts. A layout that a run brings to it is rejected with every path in which the tree differs from the base tree. Where the base tree has one at its root, no run could change any path, so such a repository is not worked on: the item is a blocking one at onboarding (20.1), and the claim refuses such a base tree as it refuses one with a submodule entry (8.2). A run that meets one at a base update passes no round, and ends in an escalation by its caps (7.3).
  - **What the base tree holds.** A layout of the base tree fails no capture, and a run changes no path below it: not its `config`, and not one of the entries that make the layout, which a later commit could put back beside a `config` that the run wrote. A run may remove the directory whole. The readiness report of onboarding lists each directory below the root of the tree at the default-branch head that has the layout as an advisory item (20.1). A blocking item was not taken for those: a repository whose tests keep such a fixture would not be onboarded at all.
  - **What a layout reads from outside its directory.** git read a `config` that was a symbolic link to a file outside the directory, a file that the `config` named in `include.path`, and the `config` of the directory that a `commondir` named, and it ran the alias of each (P-40). Where a layout of the base tree reads such a file, the file lies below no layout and a run may change it. The operator sees the layout in the listing of onboarding and adds what it reads to `protected_extra`. A layout that a run brings in is rejected whatever it reads.
  - **Not taken.** A `flag` in 11.3 in the place of the rejection: where a policy protects most of the code nearly every pull request carries the flag (VAL-3), and what is merged runs on the next checkout. Admitting the layout and leaving it to the setting of WS-15 was not taken either. 23.3 names what the rule does not cover.
- **WS-14.** An export belongs to one stage execution (D-43). The host makes it at the start of every `verify` and every `review`, in `exec/<seq>/export/`, a directory that it creates for that execution and that is empty. No export is used a second time: not by a later execution of the same stage on the same candidate, and not by `review` after `verify`. A stage that starts again, after an interruption or after a restart of the daemon (8.4), is a new stage execution with a new `<seq>`, and it has a new export. An export is none of the journalled sub-steps that a recovery replays.
  - **Why.** Verify may write to its export, and it runs the candidate's own tests. Until D-43 the export was named by the candidate's SHA, and nothing said what a second use of that name starts from. A test of a subverted candidate could rewrite files of its export and then hang. The verify that started after the interruption would have run on what the first one left, and `factory/verify` would have been reported for a tree that never passed (INV-11, VAL-2).
  - **What was observed.** The two commands of the export were run a second time on a directory that a stand-in for verify had written to. `checkout-index -a` left each changed file as it was, printed `already exists, no checkout` for it and exited with 1. With `-f` it wrote the candidate's files again and left a file that the candidate does not hold. Only a new directory held the candidate's bytes and nothing else (P-40). The name by SHA, with a rule that the host deletes the directory before each use, was not taken: a directory that is used once needs no deletion to be right, and `verify` and `review` of one candidate never meet in it.
  - **How long it stays.** An export is removed when 9.3 removes it, and not when its stage execution ends. After a failed verify the operator can look at what the commands left. A run with several verify rounds holds an export for each until then, inside the quota of its run directory (8.3). Removing an export with its stage execution was not taken.
- **WS-15.** `safe.bareRepository = explicit` is in force for git where a person works on a checkout (D-43). The operator sets it in the global configuration of their own account on the Mac and on the server, with `git config --global safe.bareRepository explicit`, and the development VM has it in its system configuration (DEV-3). Nothing checks it.
  - **Why.** WS-13 covers what the factory brings in. It does not cover a layout that a human commits, one that a base tree holds, or one in a repository that the factory never worked on. With the setting in the global configuration, in the system configuration or on the command line, git refused each layout that it had taken, with `cannot use bare repository`, also where the directory's own `config` set the value back. It did not read the setting from the configuration of the repository around the directory (P-40), so no file of a checkout can set it and none can take it away.
  - **What it costs.** Under the setting an ordinary repository, a linked worktree, the inside of a `.git` directory, and a bare repository that is a remote by its path behaved as before. git that is started inside a bare repository needs `--git-dir` or `GIT_DIR` (P-40), which the host gives on every command (WS-8).
  - **Not taken.** A step of `cargo xtask check` that fails where the setting is not in force: it would test the machine and not the repository. Leaving the setting out of this specification was not taken either.
  - **Not observed.** What a program does that reads a repository without the `git` CLI, with the setting or without it.

### 9.5 Ecosystems

Target repositories are Rust first, with other languages to follow (decision D-10). Everything specific to a language's toolchain therefore sits behind one interface, `Ecosystem`, in `factory-core`, with `rust` as the only implementation in v1:

- toolchain resolution and the worker `PATH` (WS-7); the check tools join that `PATH` by one rule for every ecosystem (WS-7a);
- the environment for build output and caches (9.3);
- the default `setup` and `verify` commands offered at onboarding, with the check tools those commands need (11.2, WS-7a);
- integrity rules for dependencies and build scripts, with the paths that name a manifest, a lockfile and a build script (VAL-5e) and what in the first two is a new dependency or a changed source (VAL-5g), lockfile and generated-file defaults, and the built-in deny list (11.3);
- the advisory database, its source (VAL-1) and where a worker is shown it (VAL-1a), and the mutation-testing command (11.7);
- the dependency-bump class predicate (13.6);
- ecosystem-specific readiness checks at onboarding (20.1).

Policy names the ecosystem (`ecosystem = "rust"`). The pipeline, sandbox, GitHub model and gates contain no Cargo-specific logic outside this interface, and a structural test enforces that. Where this specification names `cargo` commands or Cargo files elsewhere, it describes the `rust` implementation. Adding a language means adding an implementation, not changing the pipeline. Repositories that mix ecosystems are out of scope for v1.

`Ecosystem` does no I/O, as nothing in `factory-core` does (19.1, D-42). Each of its functions is pure. It is given bytes that another crate read, those of `rust-toolchain.toml`, of a manifest or of a lockfile, and it returns data. Where the answer lies on the host's disk, as for the toolchain, it returns what to look for, and `factory-daemon` looks (WS-7). Where the answer needs a command, as for a readiness check of onboarding, it returns the command line, and `factory-daemon` runs it in a sandbox as it runs a verify command (11.2). What is specific to an ecosystem in operator configuration is a table of its own, `[ecosystem.rust]` (16.4).

---

## 10. Agent execution

### 10.1 Driver interface

```rust
trait AgentDriver {
    fn capabilities(&self) -> Capabilities;        // resume, interrupt, structured_output, budget_cap, reports_cost
    async fn start(&self, spec: SessionSpec, sandbox: &dyn SandboxInstance) -> Result<SessionHandle>;
}

struct SessionSpec {
    session_id: Uuid,            // generated and journalled by the engine
    role: Role,                  // Planner | TestAuthor | Implementer | Reviewer
    cwd: PathBuf,                // workspace (planner, implementer) or candidate export (reviewer)
    system_prompt: String,       // byte-stable per stage and policy version
    prompt: String,
    result_schema: serde_json::Value,
    tools: ToolPolicy,           // allowlist
    model: ModelSpec,            // id, effort
    limits: Limits,              // turns, usd (min of stage cap and remaining issue budget; through M1 the stage cap, BUD-7), wall clock, idle, tool call
    env: BTreeMap<String, String>,
}

// SessionHandle: events() -> Stream<AgentEvent>; interrupt(); kill(); wait() -> SessionOutcome
```

`AgentEvent` is a normalised enum: `Init`, `AssistantText`, `ToolCall`, `ToolResult`, `Usage`, `RateLimited`, `Result`, `Unknown(raw)`. Unknown message types and fields are preserved, never fatal.

v1 ships one driver, for Claude Code. A Codex driver (which can likewise reuse a saved ChatGPT login) and a generic Agent Client Protocol driver are later additions behind the same trait; the ACP crate has had four major versions in five months, so it is not a v1 dependency.

### 10.2 Roles

| Role | File access | Context given |
|---|---|---|
| Planner | Workspace mounted read-only | Snapshot, repository instructions |
| Test author (M3) | Workspace, test paths only | Snapshot, plan |
| Implementer | Workspace read-write | Snapshot, plan, `progress.md`, feedback from failed gates |
| Reviewer | Candidate export mounted read-only | Snapshot, plan, the diff `base_sha..head`. Never the implementer's transcript, `progress.md` or feedback. |

Read-only roles are enforced by the sandbox mount mode, not by the tool allowlist alone. Sessions run one at a time within a run. The reviewer SHOULD be configurable to a different model than the implementer.

### 10.3 Claude Code driver

The driver runs the CLI as a subprocess and speaks its stream-JSON protocol. No community SDK crate is used; there is no official Rust SDK.

- **AG-1.** Every invocation is headless, and bare except in `subscription` mode (AG-3a): `claude -p --bare --setting-sources user --output-format stream-json --verbose --input-format stream-json`, with an explicit `--session-id`, `--model`, `--permission-mode`, `--permission-prompts none`, tool allowlist (`--tools` / `--allowedTools`), `--settings <file>`, `--mcp-config <file> --strict-mcp-config`, `--json-schema <inline schema>`, `--max-turns`, `--max-budget-usd`, `--append-system-prompt-file <file>`, `--include-partial-messages` (BUD-4) and `--forward-subagent-text` (BUD-10). Flag names are those of Claude Code 2.1.311; the last was observed on 2.1.323 (probe P-26). The driver owns the mapping. `sf doctor` verifies each flag by invoking the CLI with it against a stub model endpoint and checking for an unknown-option error; it does not parse `--help`, which omits some supported flags. The stub is a listener of `factory-net` that answers for the model host, beside the model gateway (19.1), and `factory-daemon` joins it to the driver's flag list. The check arrives with the driver, in M1a (CLI-1, D-39).
- **AG-2.** Bare mode skips the repository's `CLAUDE.md`, hooks, skills, plugins and `.mcp.json`, but project settings files can still apply an `env` block and helper commands. `--setting-sources user` is therefore mandatory, so no project or local settings file is read. This is intended (INV-2, INV-10).
- **AG-2a.** Repository instructions are computed by the host at the policy commit: `AGENTS.md` and the files that its instruction references name, to at most 2 levels, within a size cap for a file and one for the set, only where a pattern of policy's `[instructions] directories` matches them (default `/docs/**`, matched as VAL-5b says), resolved only to regular blobs read from the object database. Symlink entries and paths containing `..` are rejected; HTML comments are stripped. AG-2b says what an instruction reference is, how the set is built from them and what the caps are. The implementer and planner receive them through the system prompt file. The reviewer's system prompt contains only the factory's role prompt; repository instructions reach it in a delimited data block. Two things about the patterns were decided as D-26. The default is anchored to the repository root, because a match admits a file to what every session is given as instructions, and a pattern that matches too much loosens that control (VAL-5b). And a pattern may name a single file, as `/GLOSSARY.md` does in the factory repository's policy (BOOT-6). The instruction set is what a session is given unasked, not all that a worker can read: the planner and the implementer have the whole tree in the workspace, and the reviewer has it in the export (10.2). A document too large for the set stays out of it, and a worker reads it in the workspace or the export.
- **AG-2b.** How the instruction set is built (D-34). The host starts from `AGENTS.md`, the file of that exact name at the repository root, and reads the instruction references of each file that it takes into the set.
  - **An instruction reference** is a link destination or a code span that names a file of the tree at the policy commit: the path of an entry that is a regular file or a symbolic link. A symbolic link is never admitted, as the tests below say. A file is read as CommonMark, without extensions. A link is an inline link or a reference-style link; an image and an autolink are not links here. A code span counts by its whole text. A path that stands in the prose in neither form is no instruction reference, and nothing inside a code block, fenced or indented, is one. The factory repository's `AGENTS.md` names every file in a code span and holds no link, so links alone would have left its set at `AGENTS.md` (BOOT-6). A bare path was not taken, because nothing marks where it ends: a full stop after it may be part of it. The cost of the code span is that a file named in passing, in "do not edit `docs/x.md`", is an instruction reference too, and only a pattern keeps it out of the set.
  - **What a path is relative to.** The text of a code span is a path from the repository root, whatever directory the file that holds it lies in, with or without a leading `/`. GitHub makes no link of a code span, so this follows how such files are written: `docs/agents/domain.md` of the factory repository names `GLOSSARY.md` so. A link destination is relative to the directory of the file that holds it, and it is a path from the repository root where it begins with `/`. That is how GitHub reads a relative link in a rendered file, by its documentation: such a link is "relative to the current file", and one that starts with `/` is "relative to the repository root". A destination is read in this order. One with a scheme, or one that begins with `//`, is no instruction reference. It then loses its fragment and its query, which is everything from the first `#` or `?`, and a destination of which nothing is left is none. Its percent-escapes are decoded after that, and only then is it split into components at each `/`. In either form a `.` component is dropped, and a name with a `..` component is not looked up (AG-2a). A file below the root therefore names a file above its own directory from the root: as `/GLOSSARY.md` in a link, or in a code span. Reading a link from the root as well was not taken. In `docs/a.md` a link to `b.md` means `docs/b.md`, and from the root it would name nothing, or a different `b.md`.
  - **One file.** A name is looked up in the tree by its exact bytes, without the fold of VAL-5d. A name that is the path of no entry, of a directory or of a submodule is no instruction reference. A name that leads through a symbolic link is the path of no entry, since the host follows no link. In the factory repository's `AGENTS.md`, `docs/research/NN-*.md` and `docs/adr/` are none, and neither is expanded into the files it covers. Patterns are written in `[instructions] directories`, and a change there is a change under `.factory/**` (CFG-3). An expanded name would let a file that is added later join the set with no change to a file of the set or to policy.
  - **Admission.** A file that an instruction reference names joins the set when it passes five tests, tried in this order: a pattern of `[instructions] directories` matches its path (VAL-5b); it is a regular file and not a symbolic link; it is no larger than 32 KiB *(tunable)*; its bytes are valid UTF-8; and it fits into what is left of 64 KiB *(tunable)* for the whole set. The first test that fails is the reason given for leaving the file out, and a file that fails one of the first three is not read. What is left of the 64 KiB is that number less the sizes of the files that are in the set by then, `AGENTS.md` among them. A file that was left out takes nothing from it. Both numbers are those of the snapshot (SEC-4), and neither is a key of policy or of operator configuration. A size is that of the blob as stored, before the HTML comments are stripped. A file that does not fit is left out whole. No file is cut: half of a file can be half of a rule.
  - **Order and levels.** `AGENTS.md` comes first. The files that its instruction references name are level 1, and they are tried for admission in the order in which the references first appear in its text. Only after all of them are the level 1 files of the set read, in the order in which they joined it. The files that each of them names are level 2, tried in the order of first appearance within it. The instruction references of a level 2 file are not read, and neither are those of a file that was left out, so nothing is followed from either. A file is tried once, at the first place it is named. An instruction reference to a file that is already in the set, `AGENTS.md` included, adds nothing, so a cycle ends by itself. The walk goes on after a file was left out, and a smaller file that is named later can still join.
  - **`AGENTS.md` itself.** The policy is invalid (16.3) where, at the policy commit, the tree holds no `AGENTS.md`, or it is not a regular file, or its bytes are not valid UTF-8, or it is over the cap for a file. Each of these is a blocking item at onboarding too (20.1). The host follows no link, so an `AGENTS.md` that is a symbolic link to `CLAUDE.md`, which is a common layout, is one of these cases. No session runs without repository instructions, and none runs on a cut `AGENTS.md`. A merge that takes `AGENTS.md` over the cap therefore pauses new dispatch on the repository until a later merge brings it back under.
  - **HTML comments.** A comment is here what SEC-4 calls an HTML comment, and one function of `factory-core` finds it in a file of the set as in a snapshot (D-35): CommonMark says where the raw HTML lies, read with the table extension on as SEC-4 has it, and the HTML standard says what a comment in it is. A `<!--` in a code span or in a code block is text and stays, and so does one inside a paragraph that nothing closes. The host strips each comment from the text that a session is given. The instruction references are read from the text that is left, as CommonMark without extensions, so no name inside a comment is one, and the table extension plays no part in what an instruction reference is. A comment that nothing closes within its HTML block takes what follows it with it, as far as SEC-4 says, and it draws a warning (below). A reading by the characters alone, from every `<!--` to the next `-->`, was not taken. A file that writes `<!--` in a code span, as a text about GH-5a does, would lose everything from there to the next `-->`, with nothing to show for it. Until D-35 a comment here was what CommonMark alone reads as one: an HTML block that begins with `<!--`, taken whole, and inline HTML from `<!--` to `-->`. In an issue body GitHub also hides a comment that stands on a line inside another HTML block, a `<div>` for one, and that reading left such a comment in the text (P-32). A file of a repository was rendered as an issue body is, on every text of that probe.
  - **What is reported.** Apart from the cases of `AGENTS.md` itself, nothing in this item is an error, and one thing draws a warning: a comment that nothing closes within its HTML block (SEC-4), in a file of the set. The warning names the file, and the policy stays valid (CLI-5). A stray `<!--` at the start of a line takes the rest of a file's instructions away, and a list that is only information gives nobody a reason to read it. An invalid policy was not taken, because the author may have meant the comment. A link destination or a code span that names no file is not reported: a code span that names no file cannot be told from a command or a label, and most code spans are one of these. An instruction reference whose file was left out is listed with the reason, which is the first test of admission that failed: no pattern admits it, it is a symbolic link, it is over the cap for a file, it is not valid UTF-8, or it does not fit the set. A name with a `..` component is listed too. That a file names a document which the set leaves out is the intended state: the factory repository's `AGENTS.md` names `SPEC.md` and its policy admits `GLOSSARY.md` alone. Whenever a run takes a policy commit (16.3), the journal records the set, each file with its path and blob id, and the list of what was left out. `sf policy check` prints both (CLI-5), and the body of an onboarding pull request holds them (20.1).
  - **Where the code lies.** Reading the instruction references out of a text and building the set are pure functions of `factory-core` (19.1). The first uses `pulldown-cmark` (19.2), since a code span, a link and a comment are whatever CommonMark says they are: a run of backticks of any length, a link whose definition stands elsewhere in the file. Probe P-31 records what the crate reported for each form that this item names. The second is given the tree as a lookup from a path to an entry, which `factory-git` answers from the object database. A scanner of the factory's own for the two forms was not taken. M1a builds both (22).
- **AG-3.** Authentication follows the credential mode (13.5): `subscription` (default) passes a long-lived token from `claude setup-token` as `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`; `gateway` points the CLI at the model gateway; `direct_key` passes an API key or `apiKeyHelper`.
- **AG-3a.** Bare mode does not read the subscription token, so in `subscription` mode the CLI runs without `--bare` and the isolation bare mode gave is rebuilt from other controls: `--setting-sources user`, `--strict-mcp-config` with a host-written MCP file, and the per-execution config directory of AG-4 as the only user-level configuration. On Claude Code 2.1.311 these kept out of the session everything that probe P-21 planted in the working directory and that took effect without them: `CLAUDE.md`, `CLAUDE.local.md`, `.claude/rules/` and `AGENTS.md`; skills, commands and subagents; the hooks and `env` block of `.claude/settings.json` and of `.claude/settings.local.json`, and the permission rules of the second; `.mcp.json`; a plugin folder under `.claude/skills/`; and what the CLI otherwise picks up after the start, from a subdirectory or from a file written during the session. The flags are those of every role (AG-1), so the planner and the implementer load none of these files from the workspace either, and repository instructions reach a session only through AG-2a (INV-10). This was observed on one CLI version, on macOS and outside the sandbox. Until the contract test (19.4) has passed on the server, the host still removes `CLAUDE.md`, `CLAUDE.local.md`, `.claude/` and `.mcp.json` from the export the reviewer runs on, as a second layer (the diff still shows changes to them). The contract test asserts the lock-down on every tested CLI version, because the CLI's defaults for `-p` are announced to change. Two sources of configuration lie outside these flags (P-36): the directory `/etc/claude-code` of the machine, which SEC-6d keeps out of the sandbox, and the account, whose managed settings and policy limits the CLI fetches with the login. The settings were empty for the operator's account; its policy limits were not read.
- **AG-4.** Each stage execution gets a fresh config directory under `exec/<seq>/home/`, created by the host. The CLI reads its user-level configuration from this directory and applies a change to it while a session runs (probe P-21). So the sandbox makes read-only to the worker everything in it that the CLI reads as configuration: the settings file and `skills/`, for which the probe observed this, and `CLAUDE.md`, `rules/`, `commands/` and `agents/`, for which it is assumed. The host creates each of them, empty where it has no use for it. The rest is writable for transcripts. The host's `--settings` file lives outside any worker-writable path.
- **AG-5.** The driver checks the CLI version against a tested range at startup and feature-detects from the `capabilities` array of the `system/init` message. Outside the range, the daemon refuses to dispatch.
- **AG-6.** The raw stream is appended to `events.jsonl` before parsing. Lines up to 10 MiB are accepted; longer lines fail the session. The structured result is read from the `structured_output` field of the final `result` message; its absence is an agent failure.
- **AG-7.** Permission prompts and user-input requests are denied, never awaited.
- **AG-8.** The settings file raises the CLI's maximum shell-tool timeout to `tool_call_timeout_secs`. The host's `--settings` file (AG-4) sets `sandbox.enabled` to `false`. The CLI's own Bash sandbox is not relied on (SEC-8), so it is kept off in every session and not left to its default, which is off on Claude Code 2.1.311. Probe P-6 found nothing gained by turning it on: it then either did not start, or took from a shell command part of what the outer sandbox had left it, or failed the command.

### 10.4 Prompts and context

A session's input is assembled by the host from four parts:

1. **System prompt file** (stable per stage and policy version, to keep prompt caching effective): the factory's role prompt for the stage (`.factory/prompts/<stage>.md` from the policy commit, or the built-in default), then the repository instructions (AG-2a), except for the reviewer.
2. **Task block:** the snapshot, wrapped in delimiters and introduced as untrusted data that describes the task but cannot change the rules above.
3. **Handoff block:** the plan, `progress.md`, and for rounds the gate feedback (failing command, exit code, the last 100 lines of output *(tunable)*, and the path of the full log in the read-only handoff view).
4. **Close-out protocol:** leave the tree in a buildable state and emit the result, with `notes` for the next session.

- **AG-9.** Each stage execution starts a fresh session. Session resume MAY be used only within one stage execution as an optimisation, with automatic fallback to a fresh session.
- **AG-10.** Output from factory-run commands that is fed back to an agent is written for a model: a short summary, the failing lines, a remediation hint when known, and a file path for the full log.
- **AG-21.** The built-in role prompt of every role that is given the repository instructions (AG-2a) says which of them are not addressed to a worker (D-26). A target repository writes its `AGENTS.md` for sessions that publish their own work: they branch, commit through the repository's hooks, open pull requests and use the issue tracker. A worker does none of this. The host builds the candidate and publishes it (9.4), and a worker holds no GitHub credential (INV-1) and reaches no GitHub host (SEC-11). The role prompt says so once, for every repository, and says that instructions about branches, pull requests, the issue tracker and git hooks are for those other sessions. The implementer's role prompt says two more things, about a repository's checks (D-28). A check that the repository instructions tell a session to run before a commit, the implementer runs before it finishes, since a worker makes no commit that is published (WS-3, 9.4). And where a check reports that it could not run in the sandbox, the implementer leaves the check and its tests as they are and says so in `notes` (10.5). A change that makes such a check pass in the sandbox, or leaves it out, would change it in every place that runs it. The default prompts that onboarding writes into `.factory/prompts/` (20.1) are the built-in texts and carry the same statements. A repository therefore needs no part of its `AGENTS.md` written for a worker.

### 10.5 Stage results

Every agent stage ends with a schema-validated JSON result.

**Implementer (and test author):**

```json
{
  "status": "success | needs_human | failed",
  "summary": "one paragraph",
  "commit_subject": "≤ 72 characters",
  "criteria": [{"id": "AC-1", "state": "met | not_met", "evidence": "test name, file:line, or explanation"}],
  "questions": ["only with needs_human"],
  "follow_ups": [{"title": "…", "body": "…"}],
  "notes": "handoff for the next session; the host writes it to progress.md"
}
```

A result with `status: success` and any non-`manual` criterion `not_met` is schema-invalid. An inapplicable criterion is a `needs_human` outcome.

**Planner:**

```json
{
  "status": "success | needs_human | failed",
  "summary": "one paragraph",
  "plan_markdown": "≤ 32 KiB",
  "questions": [],
  "issues": [{"key": "S1", "title": "…", "body": "…", "risk": "low | medium | high",
              "blocked_by": ["S0"], "spec_delta": false}]
}
```

`issues` is required for tier 2 and forbidden otherwise. The host writes `handoff/plan.md` from `plan_markdown`; the planner writes no file.

The reviewer's schema is in 11.5. An agent's `success` is a claim. It moves the run to the next gate and nothing more.

### 10.6 Process supervision

- **AG-11.** Every sandboxed command (agent session, setup, capture, verify) is spawned as the leader of its own process group inside a sandbox instance, with a cleared environment plus an allowlist: `PATH` (WS-7, WS-7a), `HOME`, `TMPDIR`, `CARGO_HOME`, `CARGO_TARGET_DIR`, locale, `TRACEPARENT`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY` and `NO_PROXY` with the same three names in lower case (SEC-10b), `SF_SANDBOX` and, in a workspace, `SF_BASE_SHA` (AG-11a), and, for agent sessions only, `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC=1` (SEC-11), `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_BACKGROUND_TASKS=1` (BUD-10) and the model credential for the mode (13.5): the subscription token, or the model gateway address and per-run token. At level `process` it is the first process that starts the command as the leader of its group (SEC-10a), and `TMPDIR` holds `/tmp` (SEC-6c).
- **AG-11a.** Two variables of that environment tell a program of the repository's own tree, its check command for example, where it runs (D-28).
  - **`SF_SANDBOX`** holds the sandbox level of the instance (13.3): `none`, `process` or `hardened`. Every sandboxed command has it. A program that reads `process` or `hardened` there runs in a worker's sandbox, where no user namespace can be created (SEC-6b).
  - **`SF_BASE_SHA`** holds the default-branch commit that the workspace's tree derives from: the run's `base_sha` (WS-1), which a reset (7.6) and a base update (12.4) replace. In the round that resolves the conflicts of a base update it is already the new default-branch head. Every sandboxed command that runs in a workspace has it. A command on an export does not, since an export holds no repository. A workspace has no remote (WS-1) and the agent may commit in it (WS-3), so neither a remote-tracking branch nor `HEAD` says what the run changed.
  - **Neither is a control.** A candidate can change the program that reads them, as it can change its own tests. On the factory repository both lie under protected paths, so such a change carries a flag (BOOT-4). The host takes no base from a worker: the integrity check diffs from the run's own `base_sha` (11.3, 12.4). Neither variable is set in CI or in an interactive session.
- **AG-12.** Stop sequence: send the protocol interrupt or SIGINT and wait up to 10 s for the result message; close stdin; SIGTERM to the group; after 5 s, SIGKILL to the group and destroy the sandbox instance. After SIGTERM the supervisor does not wait for a result message, because the CLI does not emit one. At level `process` the group is inside the sandbox's PID namespace, and the supervisor sends each signal of the sequence to the first process, which passes SIGINT and SIGTERM on to the group (SEC-10a).
- **AG-13.** Stdout is read through a length-capped line codec; stderr is drained concurrently to a log file. Pipes are used, not a PTY. After the leader exits, output is drained for at most 2 s and the pipe handles are then closed, so a grandchild holding a pipe cannot hang the supervisor.
- **AG-14.** The agent's own `--max-turns` and `--max-budget-usd` are set equal to the factory's session limits, so the control plane's threshold (BUD-1) fires first, except for what subagents spend (BUD-10); a CLI-side cap result is handled as Budget. With stream-JSON input the CLI stays alive after such a result and waits for input (BUD-6), so the supervisor ends the session then, by the stop sequence of AG-12.
- **AG-15.** The sandbox record, pid, process group id and process start time are journalled before the first exec.
- **AG-16.** Every sandboxed command is started through a supervisor shim (`sf __supervise`, same binary) that holds a pipe from the daemon and kills its whole tree when that pipe closes, so workers do not outlive a killed daemon. The sandbox also uses a PID namespace with die-with-parent (level `process`) or a VM (level `hardened`), so no worker process outlives its instance. The shim is on the host's side of the sandbox. At level `process` it starts `bwrap`, `bwrap` starts the first process, and the first process starts the command (SEC-10a).
- **AG-17.** `sf` ships a systemd unit whose stop timeout (45 s) exceeds the daemon's shutdown budget (19.3).
- **AG-18.** After every sandboxed command the supervisor sweeps for survivors: any process whose process group or session equals the journalled one, or whose working directory or executable is under `runs/<run_id>/`. They are killed, and the next stage MUST NOT start until the sweep is empty. The same sweep is the orphan rule of 8.4. With a PID namespace or a VM per instance, the sweep is a second check and not the primary control.
- **AG-19.** The egress proxy and the model gateway admit connections for a run only while one of its activities is live. This closes the spend and exfiltration path for a process that escaped the sweep. The gateway accepts a per-run token only on connections that arrive through that run's own proxy endpoint (SEC-10); a token presented anywhere else is refused and logged with both run ids.
- **AG-20.** An agent session may start subagents (D-22). BUD-6 and BUD-10 say how they run and how their calls count toward the session's limits.

---

## 11. Validation

Passing tests is not enough evidence that a change is correct, and agents weaken tests they are allowed to edit (report 03). The pipeline is therefore layered, and the implementer controls none of the layers: every gate after `implement` runs on the candidate commit, outside the implementer's environment (INV-11).

### 11.1 Layers

| # | Gate | Decides | Since |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `integrity` — diff policy on the host | Did the change touch what it must not, or weaken tests? | M1: path, structural and secret rules. M3: tampering heuristics. |
| 2 | `verify` — repository commands on a clean export, in a sandbox | Does the committed tree build, lint and pass tests? | M1 |
| 3 | `ci` — GitHub Actions on the pull request | Does it pass in a second clean environment? | M1 |
| 4 | `review` — clean-context reviewer agent | Does the diff satisfy each criterion, and is it sound? | M2 |
| 5 | Human review | Is this acceptable to merge? | M1 |
| 6 | Locked acceptance tests from `author_tests` | Were the criteria tested by someone other than the builder? | M3 |
| 7 | Diff-scoped mutation testing in CI | Do the tests detect changed behaviour? | M3 |
| 8 | Holdout scenarios in a separate private repository | Does it work on checks the builder never saw? | M6, for classes that require it (13.6) |

### 11.2 Verify

Policy lists the commands, for a Rust repository typically:

```toml
[verify]
commands = [
  "cargo fmt --all --check",
  "cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --locked -- -D warnings",
  "cargo nextest run --workspace --locked",
  "cargo test --doc --workspace --locked",
]
timeout_secs = 1800
```

`cargo nextest` is no part of the toolchain. A command of this list finds such a program only if the policy names it as a check tool (WS-7a).

- **VAL-1.** Commands run on the candidate export in a new sandbox instance with a fresh home and target directory, the same sandbox level as the agent, and no credentials (INV-3, INV-11). `setup` commands run first. A change that adds or alters dependencies makes `cargo build` arbitrary code execution; the sandbox, not the command list, is the control. The host fetches the RustSec advisory database daily (never a worker) and shows it to verify and to an implementer session (VAL-1a); `cargo deny` in a worker runs without fetching.
- **VAL-1a.** How a worker is shown the advisory database (D-41). The host keeps one clone of it in the data directory (9.1), which it fetches with its `git` (19.2), under the directory name that `cargo deny` gives the database's address: `advisory-db-3157b0e258782691` for `https://github.com/RustSec/advisory-db`. `cargo deny` 0.22.1 took a clone that `git clone --depth=1` had made under that name (P-38). A sandbox instance of an implementer session or of verify shows that directory read-only at `$CARGO_HOME/advisory-dbs/advisory-db-3157b0e258782691`, which is where `cargo deny` looks when its configuration names no `db-path`. The directory `advisory-dbs` above it is the instance's own and stays writable. `cargo deny` opens a file `db.lock` there for writing in every run, to take a lock on it, and exits with 1 where it cannot, as it did with that directory on a file system mounted read-only. With git's clone mounted read-only in its place below a writable directory, the step passed (P-38). An implementer session is shown the database because the repository's check command may run `cargo deny` there (DEV-7), and a worker can fetch nothing (SEC-11). The database is public data that the host fetched. `cargo deny` fails in a worker, as anywhere, when the last fetch is older than the limit of the repository's `deny.toml`, 90 days where it names none. It reads that time from the file `FETCH_HEAD` of the clone, and where there is none it takes the later of the time of the file `HEAD` and the date of the commit. A repository whose `deny.toml` names another `db-path`, or a database that the host does not fetch, is not served by this. Where its verify runs `cargo deny`, it does not pass the blocking item of 20.1 that names the tool, and where its check command alone does, the step fails in an implementer's session. The host's fetch keeps the time of the last fetch as the task of DEV-14 does when a fetch fails. What else the host does then is left to the change that builds the fetch, in M1b (22).
- **VAL-2.** `factory/verify` records the tree SHA that was verified and is reported only on a head whose tree equals it (GH-9).

### 11.3 Integrity check

A deterministic check on `base_sha..candidate`, run by the host on every candidate. Each rule yields `pass`, `flag` or `fail`.

| Rule | Outcome |
|---|---|
| Change to a **locked path**: a file of the tests that `author_tests` wrote for this run (VAL-5e) | `fail` |
| Change under the `.github/workflows` directory at the repository root: a path that `/.github/workflows/**` matches (VAL-5e) | `fail`. For a `type:ci` issue the run escalates with `policy-denial` and attaches the proposed workflow as a patch for a human to commit, because the App cannot push it (GH-11). A `state:ready` resume from this escalation is handled as a reset (7.6): the new attempt starts from the default-branch head that then contains the committed workflow. If nothing else remains to do, the human closes the issue instead. The escalation comment states both options. |
| Change under a **protected path**: `.factory/**`, `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/**`, `.mcp.json`, the instruction directories of AG-2a and any file in the computed instruction set (VAL-5e), `.github/**`, `CODEOWNERS`, `.gitattributes`, `.gitignore`, `docs/specs/**` (outside a `type:spec-delta` issue), `deny.toml`, `clippy.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml`, `.cargo/**`, plus policy additions. Every built-in pattern matches at any depth (VAL-5b) | `flag` |
| New or changed build script: a `build.rs` at any depth, or a file that a `Cargo.toml` names as its build script; a change of the key that names it. New dependency or changed dependency source in a `Cargo.toml` or a `Cargo.lock` at any depth (VAL-5e); VAL-5g says what either is | `flag` |
| A path that is a **symbolic link** at the base or in the candidate: a link added, removed or pointed elsewhere, a link where a file was, a file where a link was (VAL-5f) | `flag` |
| A changed path carries an attribute that hides or alters its diff (`-diff`, `binary`, `diff=`, `merge`, `filter`, `working-tree-encoding`, `linguist-generated`, `linguist-vendored`) | `flag`, listed in the pull request body as "hidden or collapsed in the GitHub diff"; `fail` if the attribute was added or changed in the same diff |
| A binary change to a path that was text at base | `flag` |
| Diff larger than the size budget (default 600 changed lines excluding lockfiles and generated files *(tunable)*) | First time: `fail` with a request to split (`size_strikes` +1). Second time: `flag`. |
| An added path matching the built-in deny list (`target/**`) | `fail` |
| Secret pattern in added content | `fail` |
| M3 heuristics: deleted or newly ignored tests, falling test count, new `#[allow]` or `#[ignore]`, changed snapshots, hand-written `PartialEq` on types used in assertions | `flag` |

Structural rules (file type, file size, nested repositories, submodule entries, two paths with one fold, the layout of a repository under another name) are enforced earlier, in capture (9.4, WS-12, WS-13). A symbolic link is not refused there: capture admits one, and VAL-5f flags it.

- **VAL-3.** A `flag` adds the label `factory:protected-change` to the pull request, lists the flagged rules at the top of its body, and makes it ineligible for auto-merge. The listing names, for each flagged rule, the paths that matched it, for a protected path the pattern each path matched, for a symbolic link its target (VAL-5f), and for the dependency rule the reason each file was flagged for (VAL-5g). A path is written as GH-5c says. For one flagged rule the listing names at most 50 paths *(tunable)*, the first in the byte order of the paths, and then says, outside the fence, how many more there are; the journal holds them all (D-33). The label alone does not say what was touched: where a repository's policy protects most of its code, as BOOT-4 requires of the factory repository's, nearly every pull request carries it. The flag lives in the journal and the `factory/integrity` check; removing the label does not clear it (SEC-1b). In `enforced` mode `CODEOWNERS` additionally requires code-owner review for protected paths (VAL-3a).
- **VAL-3a.** The `CODEOWNERS` file of a target repository holds an entry for every protected path, with the operator as its owner (D-26): one for each pattern of the built-in list, of the instruction directories and of `protected_extra`. It is written in the onboarding pull request (20.1). With one human the file adds nothing to the approval that 20.3 requires on every pull request. It matters where code-owner review decides which pull requests need a human, as in one layout of probe P-18: a file that names fewer paths than the integrity check has GitHub enforce less than the factory reports. So the patterns of `protected_extra` have entries too, although on the factory repository they cover nearly every file (BOOT-4). One entry `*` is not used in their place, because it would own what a class of 13.6 is meant to merge without review. Every entry names the same owner, so the order among them decides nothing.
  - **Where the entries lie (D-37).** They go into the file that GitHub reads. GitHub looks under `.github/`, then at the root, then under `docs/`, and uses the first file it finds; it also found one under `.GitHub/` and one named `codeowners` (P-28). A repository that has such a file keeps it, and the entries are added to it, so that no entry it already has falls silent behind a new file in an earlier place. A repository without one gets `.github/CODEOWNERS`. A change that adds a file in any of these places is flagged: the built-in pattern `CODEOWNERS` matches each of these names (VAL-5b, VAL-5d).
  - **Last in the file (D-37).** A later line of the file decides the owner of a path that it matches. A line without an owner, or one whose only owner GitHub does not know, leaves that path with none (P-28). The entries of this item are therefore the last lines of the file, and no other entry follows them.
  - **Translation.** GitHub does not read a pattern as VAL-5b does, so an entry is a translation of its pattern. A pattern without a leading `/` is written with `**/` before it. Copied as it stands, a pattern that holds a `/` between two components and does not begin with `**/` is read from the repository root alone: `crates/**` owned nothing under `vendor/crates/` (P-28). A pattern with a leading `/` is written as it is. A backslash is written before each space, each tab and each `#` of the pattern. Without it a space ends the pattern, and a `#` begins a comment wherever it stands: `**/#hash.md` was read as the entry `**/` without an owner, which matches every file and takes the owner from every path that a line above it gave one, and GitHub reported no error for it. A tab was tried with the backslash only. Everything else is written as this section or the policy writes the pattern, `?` and the letter case included. The grammar of VAL-5c has no character class and no brace, so no pattern needs a form that the file lacks: a class drew the error `Invalid pattern`. A pattern that holds a line feed or a carriage return has no entry, and no other control character was tried. The comparison below reports such a pattern as a protected path without an entry.
  - **What an entry owns beyond its pattern.** Where the last component of an entry matches the name of a directory, the entry owns every file below that directory, as a rule of `.gitignore` would have it and VAL-5c does not: `**/Cargo.toml` owned what lies in a directory named `Cargo.toml`, and `**/*.snap` what lies in one named `x.snap` (P-28). A last component that is `*` alone owns the files of its directory and nothing below them. Such an entry owns more than its pattern protects, which asks for a review too many and never one too few.
  - **What a pattern matches and its entry does not own.** GitHub compares an entry with a path byte for byte, and VAL-5d folds case and normalises. A path that differs from a pattern only in a spelling that VAL-5d folds is therefore flagged by the integrity check and has no owner on GitHub: `**/SPEC.md` did not own `spec.md`, nor `**/AGENTS.md` that name with a long s (P-28). `?` stands for one byte in the file and for one code point in VAL-5c, so a path with a character outside ASCII in the place of a `?` has no owner either. Writing `*` for `?` would close the second gap and was not taken (D-37): the first is open to every path that an agent chooses, so closing the second alone protects nothing more. Both gaps are accepted.
  - **What rests on observation.** Probe P-28 observed each form above on the scratch repository, for files of the default branch. No API names the owner of a path, so the owner was read from the endpoint that the page of a file takes it from. Before it the rule rested on GitHub's documentation, which says nothing of a `#` or a space inside a pattern, of `?`, or of what an entry owns below a directory. Which file a pull request is held to was not observed; by that documentation it is the file of the base branch. That question, and whether a required code-owner review reads an entry as the page of a file does, are part of probe P-18.
  - **Kept in step.** A change to `protected_extra` or to the instruction directories changes `CODEOWNERS` in the same pull request. The check compares text: the lines that end the file are exactly the entries that the rule of translation gives for the patterns of the three lists. On the factory repository the structural test makes that comparison (DEV-2). On any other repository `sf repo check` makes it, from the milestone in which `sf repo add` writes the file (M5).
  - **The errors endpoint (D-37).** `GET /repos/{o}/{r}/codeowners/errors` is read as a second check, and never in place of the comparison. It names an owner that GitHub does not know, with the advice to make sure that the login exists and has write access, and from the day the operator's login is such an owner every entry leaves its paths without one. It names a character class in a pattern. It names nothing for a `#` that cut an entry short, nor for an entry without an owner, so an empty list does not say that an entry owns anything (P-28). `sf repo check` reads it with the comparison and reports every error it lists (M5). The probe read it with the operator's token; whether an installation token may read it is probe P-33. At the onboarding of the factory repository a session reads it by hand (BOOT-6).
- **VAL-4.** A `fail` returns to `implement` with the rule and the offending paths, and counts as a verify round.
- **VAL-5.** The built-in protected-path list cannot be shortened by repository policy (16.1). For the size budget, "generated" and "lockfile" are defined only by path patterns in default-branch policy, never by attributes. VAL-5b says how a path pattern matches, VAL-5c what it may hold and VAL-5d which spellings of a path are one name. VAL-5e says how the rules of the table are read that name a path outside those lists, and VAL-5f what a symbolic link draws, which no pattern says. VAL-5g says what the dependency rule flags inside a manifest and a lockfile.
- **VAL-5a.** Every diff the host computes (integrity, size budget, secret scan, reviewer input, no-progress hash) runs against bare `host.git` with no work tree, with external diff and textconv disabled and with no in-tree attributes applied (attribute source set to the empty tree). A file is treated as binary only by content detection.
  - **No rename is detected (D-43).** Rename detection and copy detection are off in every such diff, by an option on the command and not by what the command does when it is given none. A file that moved is then a deletion at its old path and an addition at its new one, and both are paths of the diff.
