With detection, `clippy.toml` moved to `notes/lints.txt` was one entry, a rename, and so was a workflow that moved out of `.github/workflows` and a `build.rs` that moved and gained a line (P-40). A rule that read the path such an entry ends at would match none of the three.
  - `git diff-tree` detected no rename when it was given no option, also with `diff.renames` set in the configuration of the repository, and `git diff` detected them on the same two trees (P-40). The option is given all the same, so that the rule does not rest on which command the host runs.
  - There is one form of diff for every use. Where capture or a base update reads the paths in which two trees differ, it reads them in the same way (WS-13). A second form with detection, for the size budget and the reviewer's input, was not taken. A file that moved therefore counts in the size budget with the lines of its deletion and those of its addition, where a diff with detection counted only the lines that changed in it, and the reviewer reads it as both.
- **VAL-5b.** Every path pattern is a glob in the grammar of VAL-5c, and all of them are matched in one way (D-25, D-29): the built-in protected-path list and deny list of this section, the lists of repository policy (`[paths]` and `[instructions]`, 16.2) and the path allowlist of a class predicate (13.6). A pattern is matched against a path relative to the repository root, with `/` between its components. Case folding and Unicode normalisation apply to the pattern and to the path before the match, as VAL-5d says. The rules of this section that name a path without being one of these lists are in VAL-5e.
  - Any depth is the default. A pattern matches a path when it matches the whole path, or the part of the path that follows any `/`. So `Cargo.toml` matches `crates/factory-core/Cargo.toml`, `crates/**` matches `vendor/crates/a.rs`, and `SPEC.md` matches `docs/specs/billing/spec.md`.
  - A leading `/` anchors a pattern to the repository root. What follows the `/` then has to match the whole path: `/SPEC.md` matches `SPEC.md` or `spec.md` at the root, and nothing below the root. The leading `/` is the only anchor. A `/` inside a pattern separates two components and anchors nothing, which is not how `.gitignore` reads one: there `crates/**` does not match `vendor/crates/a.rs`.
  - The built-in protected-path list names its own patterns unanchored, and policy cannot change them. A path is protected when a pattern of that list or of `protected_extra` matches it, so an anchored pattern in policy narrows nothing that the list names (CFG-1). The instruction directories, which the list protects too, are patterns of policy and match as policy wrote them. The list's own patterns stay at any depth without a claim about where each tool looks for its file: an entry that acts only at the root adds a needless flag at worst, and anchoring it would rest on such a claim.
  - A pattern that matches more than its author meant is safe where a match adds a flag, as it does for a protected path. It is not safe in a list that loosens a control for what it matches: `generated` and `lockfiles` exempt it from the size budget, `[instructions] directories` admits it to the instruction set (AG-2a), and a predicate's allowlist admits it to a class. `lockfiles = ["Cargo.lock"]` exempts a file of that name in every directory, and `/Cargo.lock` exempts the one at the root.
- **VAL-5c.** The grammar of a path pattern (D-29). A pattern is an optional leading `/`, the anchor of VAL-5b, and then one or more components with a `/` between them. It names files: a diff holds the paths of files, and git tracks no directory.
  - There are three wildcards. Inside a component, `*` stands for any run of characters, the empty run included, and `?` for exactly one character. Neither stands for a `/`. A component that is `**` and nothing else stands for whole components: for one or more where it is the last component of the pattern, and for none or more anywhere else. Every other character stands for itself. A character is a code point of the folded form (VAL-5d). A leading `.` in a name is not special.

    | Pattern | Path | Match |
    |---|---|---|
    | `*.snap` | `x/a.snap` | Yes: any depth (VAL-5b) |
    | `/*.snap` | `x/a.snap` | No: `*` stops at a `/` |
    | `**/*.snap` | `a.snap` | Yes: `**` stands for no component here |
    | `a/**/b` | `a/b`, `a/x/y/b` | Yes |
    | `crates/**` | `crates/a/b.rs` | Yes |
    | `crates/**` | `crates`, a file | No: a last `**` stands for at least one component |
    | `a?b` | `a/b`, `ab` | No |
    | `*` | `.hidden` | Yes |

  - No pattern names a directory. `migrations` matches a file of that name and nothing below a directory of that name. Everything below the directory is `migrations/**`, and that is its only spelling: a pattern that ends in `/` is unreadable, and the error for it names the form with `/**`. The reading of `.gitignore`, in which a name also covers what lies below it, was not taken. In a list that loosens a control (VAL-5b) it would let `generated = ["**/*.snap"]` exempt every file that an agent puts into a directory named `x.snap`.
  - There are no character classes, no braces and no escape character. The characters `[`, `]`, `{`, `}` and `\` are reserved: a pattern that holds one is unreadable, so that each can be given a meaning later without changing what a valid pattern matches. A path that holds one of them is named with `?` in its place: `app/?id?/*.tsx` matches `app/[id]/page.tsx`.
  - A pattern is unreadable when it is empty or a lone `/`; when it ends in `/`; when a component is empty, as in `a//b`, or is `.` or `..`; when it holds a reserved character; or when `**` is part of a component, as in `a**b`. An unreadable pattern makes the policy that holds it invalid (16.3), and in a class predicate's allowlist it is an error of the operator configuration (16.1). Nothing is guessed and no pattern is skipped: a pattern that was skipped in `protected_extra` would be a protection that is missing without a sign. The error names the list, the pattern and what is wrong with it.
  - Two patterns are valid and are probably not what their author meant: one without a wildcard that matches the path of a directory in the tree at the policy commit, which is how `.gitignore` would name that tree, and one that matches no path of that tree. Each draws a warning (CLI-5, 20.1).
  - The matcher is a pure function of `factory-core` (19.1), written for this grammar. A path is chosen by an agent, so the matcher's work is bounded for every pattern and path: by the product of their lengths at most, with no search that grows faster. It is covered by the property tests of 19.4 and by the mutation testing of DEV-2. No glob crate is used (19.2). `globset` 0.4.21, which was tried, matches `?` against one byte, lets a negated class match a `/`, and folds the case of ASCII letters only (P-29).
- **VAL-5d.** Which spellings of a path are one name (D-29). Before the match, the pattern and the path are each replaced by toNFC(toCasefold(toNFD(x))): canonical decomposition, then full case folding by the default mappings, which know no rule of a single language, then canonical composition. Two strings get the same result exactly when one is a canonical caseless match for the other, as the Unicode Standard defines it (D145 in its section 3.13). The composition at the end changes none of those answers. It is there for `?`, which then stands for `é` whether the path holds that letter as one code point or as two.
  - The aim is to treat as one name what a case-insensitive file system does, and not what git does. A path that such a file system takes for a protected path is that file wherever the candidate is checked out onto it. On the operator's Mac, APFS took `AGENTſ.md`, written with the long s (U+017F), for `AGENTS.md`, and `workflows` with the Kelvin sign (U+212A) in place of its `k` for `workflows`. It gave the answer of this rule in each of 21 pairs that were tried. git 2.56.1 folds the letters A to Z in a case-insensitive pathspec and compares every other byte as it is (P-29).

    | Pattern | Path | Match |
    |---|---|---|
    | `AGENTS.md` | `agents.MD`, `AGENTſ.md` | Yes |
    | `.github/**` | `.GitHub/workflows/x.yml` | Yes |
    | `straße` | `STRASSE` | Yes: full case folding |
    | `é.md` | `E` and U+0301, then `.md` | Yes |
    | `fi` | `ﬁ` (U+FB01) | Yes: full case folding |
    | `stra?e` | `straße` | No: the folded form is `strasse`, which `stra??e` matches |
    | `a` | `ａ` (U+FF41, fullwidth) | No: a compatibility form |
    | `i` | `İ` (U+0130) | No: the default mappings have no Turkic rule |

  - The tables are those of the crates `icu_casemap` and `icu_normalizer`, pinned exactly (19.2). At that pin they are the tables of Unicode 18.0.0. A change of the pin can change what a pattern matches. Unicode's stability policy keeps the normalisation of a string of assigned characters the same in every later version. Its promise for case folding covers only strings that are in the form NFKC.
  - The fold applies in every list alike. In a list that loosens a control (VAL-5b) it therefore loosens as much: `lockfiles = ["/Cargo.lock"]` also exempts a root `cargo.lock`.
  - A path that is not valid UTF-8 is folded with each ill-formed sequence replaced by U+FFFD, and is then matched as any other path.
  - Three kinds of spelling are not folded, and a path that differs from a protected path only by one of them is not flagged. Compatibility forms, such as fullwidth letters: APFS did not take them for the same name. The code points that HFS+ ignores in a name, such as U+200C, and the spellings that NTFS takes for the same name, such as a trailing dot or a short name `AGENTS~1.MD`: neither is the file system of the operator's Mac, which is APFS, or of a Linux host (D-3). git refuses both kinds for `.git`, each under a setting of its own, and accepted `AGENTS.md` followed by U+200C (P-29). The scan of 9.4 follows git for that one name.
  - Capture reads the fold too, for single names: it says which two paths of a tree are one path (WS-12), and which entries make the layout of a repository (WS-13). The fold is one function of `factory-core` for the matcher and for capture, and M0 builds it (22).
- **VAL-5e.** The rules of the table that name a path and are not one of the lists of VAL-5b (D-30): the workflow rule, the build script rule, the dependency rule and the locked paths. None of them compares a path in a way of its own. Each names its paths as path patterns, matched as VAL-5b to VAL-5d say, or as exact paths. Nobody wrote an exact path as a pattern, so no character of it is a wildcard and none is reserved. It equals a path of the diff when the two have the same fold (VAL-5d). The files of the computed instruction set (AG-2a), which the protected-path rule names beside its patterns, are exact paths too.

    | Rule | Its paths |
    |---|---|
    | Workflow | The pattern `/.github/workflows/**` |
    | Build script | The pattern `build.rs`, and the exact path of each file that a `Cargo.toml` names as its build script |
    | Dependency | The patterns `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock` |
    | Locked path | The exact paths that the run recorded (11.7) |

    | Path in the diff | Outcome |
    |---|---|
    | `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.GitHub/workflows/ci.yml` | `fail`: the workflow rule |
    | `sub/.github/workflows/ci.yml` | `flag`: the protected path `.github/**`, and not the workflow rule |
    | `crates/a/build.rs`, `src/build.rs` | `flag` |
    | `crates/a/gen.rs`, where `crates/a/Cargo.toml` holds `build = "gen.rs"` | `flag` |
    | `fuzz/Cargo.lock`, with a changed dependency source | `flag` |
    | `tests/Acceptance.rs`, where `tests/acceptance.rs` is locked | `fail` |

  - **The workflow rule is anchored to the root**, which no pattern of the built-in lists is. GitHub's documentation says: "You must store workflow files in the `.github/workflows` directory of your repository." A push that adds or changes a file in that directory is what GitHub refuses the App (GH-11). So `sub/.github/workflows/ci.yml` does not fail. It is the workflow of a template or of a test fixture, and the protected path `.github/**` flags it at any depth, with its pattern in the listing (VAL-3). A `fail` there would cost a verify round (VAL-4), and in a `type:ci` issue it would hand a human a patch to commit. On the scratch repository GitHub took no file under `sub/.github/workflows` for a workflow, and it accepted the App's push of one (P-5).
  - The fold applies to the workflow rule as to every pattern, so `.GitHub/workflows/ci.yml` at the root fails. On the scratch repository GitHub took no file under `.GitHub/workflows` or under `.github/Workflows` for a workflow, and it accepted the App's push of one (P-5). The path fails all the same. On a case-insensitive file system it is the same directory (VAL-5d), the rule has no second way of matching (D-30), and a `fail` too many costs one verify round.
  - A last `**` stands for at least one component (VAL-5c). The rule therefore covers what lies below the directory, and not a file or a symbolic link whose own path is `.github/workflows`; the protected path `.github/**` flags that one. A symbolic link whose path is `.github` matches no pattern of the built-in list, and the same holds for every pattern that names a tree, `.cargo/**` for example. The link rule flags each of those links, and neither fails (VAL-5f).
  - **A build script has two names.** Cargo takes `build.rs` in the root of a package for its build script, and the key `package.build` of the manifest names another file or, as `false`, switches that off. The pattern `build.rs` is unanchored and matches at any depth, with no look at whether a manifest lies beside the file. A module `src/build.rs` is flagged too, which is a flag too many at worst (VAL-5b). For the other name the host reads `package.build` in every file that the pattern `Cargo.toml` matches, in the base tree and in the candidate's, as data from the object database, the way WS-7 reads `rust-toolchain.toml`. A string there names a file relative to the manifest's directory, and that file's exact path joins the rule. The name is split at each `/`: a component `.` is dropped, a component `..` takes away the one before it, and a `\` stands for itself, as it does on the Linux host (D-3). A value that is not a string names no file, and neither does a name that begins with `/` or that leaves the repository.
  - A change of `package.build` is flagged by itself, whatever the old and the new value: the key added, removed or given another value, and the key present in a manifest that is new. So a candidate that makes an unchanged file the build script is flagged, and so is one that switches a build script off.
  - The rule does not follow a build script into the files it pulls in with `mod` or `include!`; a change to one of those is not flagged. The sandbox is the control for what a build runs (VAL-1), and the flag is an aid to the reviewer.
  - **The dependency rule reads every manifest and lockfile of the tree**: each file that `Cargo.toml` or `Cargo.lock` matches, at any depth. The host resolves no workspace. Which packages are its members is written in manifests that the candidate can change, and a manifest outside the workspace, of a fuzz target or of a fixture, is built by whoever runs a command in its directory. A manifest that nothing builds draws a flag too many at worst. What a new dependency or a changed source is inside those files is VAL-5g. The list `lockfiles` of policy plays no part in this rule: it exempts a file from the size budget (VAL-5b) and says nothing about dependencies. Nor does this rule say which lockfile the dependency-bump predicate of 13.6 admits. That predicate loosens a control (VAL-5b), and it is written with the classes (M4, D-14).
  - A file that one of these two patterns matches and that the host cannot read as a manifest or as a lockfile is flagged when it is new or changed. It names no build script. VAL-5g says which files the dependency rule cannot read.
  - **A locked path is an exact path.** The host records the path of each file that the commit it captured from `author_tests` adds or changes (11.7), as the tree spells it. The rule reads the diff from that commit to the candidate, and not `base_sha..candidate`, which holds the tests themselves. A path of that diff with the same fold as a locked path is a change to it, so `tests/Acceptance.rs` beside a locked `tests/acceptance.rs` fails: on a case-insensitive file system the two are one file. No directory is locked, and the implementer may add a file beside a locked one.
  - The manifest, the lockfile and the build script are named by the `rust` implementation of `Ecosystem` (9.5). Another implementation names its own, as path patterns and exact paths that are read in the same way. The workflow rule and the locked paths are the same in every ecosystem.
- **VAL-5f.** The link rule (D-31). A path of `base_sha..candidate` is flagged when its entry is a symbolic link, mode `120000`, in the base tree or in the candidate's. That is a link the diff adds, removes or points elsewhere, a link where a file was, and a file where a link was. The rule reads the two modes of an entry and no path, so it holds at any depth and under any name, and it is the same in every ecosystem (9.5). It is a rule of its own: a link whose path another rule matches draws that rule's outcome as well. Where a link takes the place of a tracked directory, the files that were in it are deletions under their own paths (P-30), and a pattern that names the tree matches those. The link itself, and a link at a name that held nothing, is what only this rule sees.

    | Entry of the diff | Outcome |
    |---|---|
    | `.cargo`, a new link to `tools/cfg` | `flag`: this rule alone, since no pattern matches the path |
    | `docs`, a new link, in a tree that held no `docs` | `flag`: this rule alone, though `docs/specs/**` is protected |
    | `clippy.toml`, a link that now points at another file | `flag`: this rule and the protected path `clippy.toml` |
    | `notes.md`, a file where a link was | `flag`: this rule |
    | `.github` or `.github/workflows` at the root, a new link | `flag`, the second through `.github/**` too; neither fails |
    | `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, a new link | `fail`: the workflow rule (VAL-5e) |
    | `target`, a new link | `flag`: the deny list does not match it |
    | `tools/cfg/config.toml`, changed, where `.cargo` is a link to `tools/cfg` at the base | Nothing from this rule: the diff holds no link |
    | `cfg2`, a link that the base tree holds as `cfg` | `flag` for both paths: a link removed and a link added, since no diff of the host detects a rename (VAL-5a, P-40) |

  - **Why a rule and not a pattern.** A pattern names files (VAL-5c), and a link is one entry of a tree under its own path, with its target as its content. So `.cargo/**` does not match a link whose path is `.cargo`, and a file behind that link is in the diff under the path it has in the tree, `tools/cfg/config.toml`, which no built-in pattern matches. Capture admits the link and the export writes it (9.4), and cargo reads its configuration through it: with `.cargo` a link to `tools/cfg`, cargo 1.100.0 took an alias from `tools/cfg/config.toml` (P-30). The same holds for every pattern that names a tree: `.github/**`, `.claude/**`, `.factory/**` and `docs/specs/**` in the built-in list, and the trees of `protected_extra`.
  - Two narrower rules were not taken: the bare name of each protected directory in the built-in list, and a capture that refuses a link at such a name. Both name the places where a link may not stand, and a link one directory up stands at none of them: `docs/specs/**` is protected, and a link whose path is `docs` stands where that whole tree would be. Policy would also have to write each tree of `protected_extra` twice.
  - The host follows no link, here as everywhere else (9.4, AG-2a). The rule compares modes, which every kind of change to a link leaves in the diff (P-30), and a target is a string that the host lists and never resolves.
  - A link that the diff removes is flagged like one it adds. What a tool read through it is gone, as a file deleted below a protected tree is.
  - **A link at `.github` or `.github/workflows` is flagged and does not fail.** On the scratch repository GitHub took nothing behind either link for a workflow: the push of a branch whose `.github/workflows` was a link to a directory with a workflow in it started no run and registered no workflow, and neither did one whose `.github` was a link, while the same workflow as a regular file on a branch beside them ran (P-5). On that evidence such a link is no change to a workflow. A link that is itself a file of that directory, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, is a path the workflow rule matches, and it fails (VAL-5e); GitHub registered one as a workflow, and its run failed before any job. The same held for a push to the default branch and for a pull request whose head held such a link. Those pushes were made with the operator's token. GitHub accepted the App's push of a link at either name as well, and started no run for it; the App's push of a link that is itself a file of the directory it refused, as it refuses a regular file there (P-5). In the pushes that were tried, then, publish met no refusal for a link that this rule flags.
  - **A link whose path is `target` is flagged and does not fail.** The deny list is there for build output that was committed. A link is none, and cargo writes nothing through it, because build output goes to the directory that the host names in `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` (9.3).
  - **The listing names a link's target beside its path** (VAL-3): the target the candidate holds, and for a link the candidate no longer holds, the target at the base. A target is text that an agent chose, so it is written to the pull request body in the form of GH-5c and through the encoder of GH-5a.
  - **A link that is in the base tree is not followed either.** The rule sees a link when a diff changes it, and not what a link that is already there stands for. Where `clippy.toml` is a link to `cfg/clippy.toml` at the default-branch head, a change to `cfg/clippy.toml` is in the diff under that path alone (P-30), matches no built-in pattern and is not flagged. The integrity check does not resolve the links of the base tree to protect their targets: that would put the reading of relative targets, of chains and of loops into the gate. The readiness report of onboarding lists every symbolic link of the tree at the default-branch head with its target, as an advisory item (20.1), and the operator adds to `protected_extra` each target that needs it. The listing of a flagged link shows its target for the same use, before the pull request is merged. A blocking item was not taken: a `CLAUDE.md` that is a link to `AGENTS.md` is common, and its target is protected.
  - So every link is listed at onboarding or flagged when the factory brings it in. A link that a human commits to the default branch after onboarding is seen by neither, and that gap is accepted.
  - **No list that loosens a control reads a link in a way of its own** (VAL-5b), and a link widens none of them. A link is matched by its own path, and a file behind it by the path it has in the tree. A link that `generated` or `lockfiles` matches therefore exempts the lines of its own diff from the size budget, which are its old and its new target, and nothing behind it. The instruction set admits regular blobs only (AG-2a), so nothing behind a link enters it; that holds for a link at a directory too, since a path below it names no entry of the tree. A diff that changes a link carries this rule's flag, and a head with a flag does not merge at A3 (13.6), whatever the allowlist and the file modes of a class predicate admit.
  - A `Cargo.toml` or a `Cargo.lock` that is a link is read neither through the link nor as the text of its target. It counts as a file the host cannot read as a manifest or as a lockfile, which VAL-5e flags when it is new or changed, and it names no build script.
- **VAL-5g.** What the dependency rule flags in the files that VAL-5e names (D-32). The rule flags a change through which a build can take code from a package, or from a place, that the base did not name. Which version of a package comes from the same place is not this rule's matter: `cargo deny` checks the versions, in verify or, where the verify list leaves it out as on the factory repository, in CI (VAL-1, BOOT-6), and a change of version is what the dependency-bump predicate of 13.6 admits. Where the host cannot tell, it flags. Each file is compared with the file at the same path in the base tree, and with nothing where the base tree has none, so a manifest or a lockfile that moved is new at its new path. The two are compared as TOML data: the order of keys, spacing, comments and the way a string is quoted decide nothing.

    | Change in a `Cargo.toml` | Outcome |
    |---|---|
    | `cfg-if = "1"` added to `[dev-dependencies]` | `flag`: `new dependency` |
    | `cfg-if = "1"` becomes `cfg-if = "1.0.5"`, or `{ version = "1", features = ["x"], optional = true }` | Nothing |
    | `cfg-if = "1"` becomes `{ version = "1", git = "…" }`, or `{ version = "1", package = "other" }` | `flag`: `changed source` |
    | An entry moved from `[dev-dependencies]` to `[dependencies]`, or given another key | `flag`: `new dependency` |
    | `core = { path = "../core" }` added, for a crate of the same repository | `flag`: `new dependency` |
    | An entry removed | Nothing |
    | An entry of `[patch.crates-io]` added, removed or given another `version` | `flag`: `patch or replace changed` |

    | Change in a `Cargo.lock` | Outcome |
    |---|---|
    | A `[[package]]` under a name that the file did not hold, whether or not a manifest names it | `flag`: `new dependency` |
    | The `version` and the `checksum` of a package changed, its `source` the same | Nothing |
    | The commit after `#` in the `source` of a git package changed | `flag`: `changed source` |
    | The `source` of a package gone, as a `[patch]` by `path` leaves it | `flag`: `changed source` |
    | The `checksum` of a package changed, its `name`, `version` and `source` the same | `flag`: `checksum changed` |
    | The file removed | `flag`: `lockfile removed` |

  - **The dependency tables of a manifest** are `dependencies`, `dev-dependencies` and `build-dependencies`, the same three in each table under `target`, and `dependencies` under `workspace`. `dev_dependencies` and `build_dependencies`, at the top level and under `target`, are dependency tables too: cargo 1.100.0 read them in a package of edition 2021, with a warning from a build, and refused them in one of edition 2024, and the Cargo reference names neither (P-34). All of them count alike. A development dependency is built and run by the tests, and a build dependency by the build.
  - **A new dependency in a manifest** is an entry whose key the same table of the base file does not hold. No rule says which entry is the same dependency under another key or in another table, so each of these is new: every entry of a manifest that the base tree does not hold; an entry that moved from one table to another, the move from `dev_dependencies` to `dev-dependencies` included; and an entry given another key. Each is a flag too many at worst. An entry with a `path` is new like any other, also where the path stays inside the repository. An entry of `workspace.dependencies` is new whether or not a member inherits it: cargo resolved none that no member inherited (P-34), and the host resolves no workspace (VAL-5e). A manifest that the base tree does not hold is not flagged for being new. It is flagged for an entry of a dependency table, and for a `[patch]` or a `[replace]`.
  - **A changed source in a manifest** is a change to an entry that the base file holds, in any key but these: `version`, `features`, `default-features`, `default_features`, `optional` and `public`. A key added, removed or given another value is a change. An entry that is a string is read as a table with that string as its `version`. The rule names the keys that do not draw the flag, so that a key it does not know draws it. The flag therefore follows `git`, `branch`, `tag`, `rev`, `path`, `registry`, `package`, `workspace` and `base`, the unstable keys `artifact`, `lib` and `target`, and also `registry-index`, which the Cargo reference does not name and which cargo 1.100.0 followed to a registry of its own (P-34).
  - **A change to one of those keys is not flagged**, and neither is the removal of an entry. A changed `version` is what a dependency bump is. A changed `features` or `default-features` can bring in a package that no manifest names, and that package is then new in the lockfile, where it is flagged: `features = ["extra"]` on one entry added a package to the lockfile (P-34). A dependency that is no longer `optional` was in the manifest already, and in the lockfile, which holds an optional dependency whether or not a feature turns it on (P-34). `public` belongs to an unstable feature of cargo, and by the Cargo reference it marks a dependency as public; it names no package and no place. The tables `[features]`, `[profile]` and `[lints]`, and `[workspace]` apart from its `dependencies`, name no package to fetch, and this rule does not read them.
  - **`[patch]` and `[replace]`.** Any change to either table is flagged: an entry added, removed or changed in any key, `version` included, and the table itself added or removed. They exist to change where a package comes from, and removing an entry changes it back. The rule reads them in every manifest. cargo reads them in the manifest at the root of a workspace alone and warned of either in a member (P-34); the host cannot tell a member from a root (VAL-5e).
  - **The packages of a lockfile** are the tables of `package` and of `patch.unused`. cargo read the package of a patch that nothing used: the lockfile held it with the version that only that package's own manifest names (P-34). A lockfile is flagged for each pair of `name` and `source` that it holds and the base file does not. The `source` is compared as the whole string, and a package without one has none for its value. A pair whose `name` the base file does not hold is listed as a new dependency, and any other as a changed source. That one comparison covers:
    - a package under a new name, also one that no manifest of the tree names: the dependency of a dependency, or what a feature brought in;
    - a name that now comes from another registry or from git;
    - a git package at another commit, whether or not its version changed, since the string ends in `#` and the commit (P-34);
    - a package whose `source` is gone, which is how a `[patch]` by `path` leaves it (P-34);
    - a package of the tree itself, which never has a `source`. A new crate of a workspace is therefore flagged here, as its `path` entry is in a manifest.
  - **Another `version` of a pair that the base file holds is not flagged**, and neither is a second version beside the first. That is a dependency bump, and it is what a lockfile can do by itself. With no manifest changed, cargo built the version and the git commit that the lockfile named. It dropped, or under `--locked` refused, a package that no manifest asked for and a package from another place than its manifest asked for (P-34). The arrays `dependencies`, the table `[metadata]` and the key `replace` are not compared; a package that `[replace]` put in the place of another is a pair of its own (P-34).
  - **A changed checksum.** Where a package has the `name`, the `version` and the `source` of a package of the base file, a `checksum` that was added, removed or changed is flagged. cargo refused a lockfile with a changed checksum, and under `--locked` one with the checksum taken out (P-34), so the flag marks a file that does not build, or a source whose content changed.
  - **A lockfile that the candidate removes is flagged.** With it go the versions that every later build of that directory would have used, and no later diff shows what a build then resolves. A removed manifest is not flagged. A lockfile that the base tree does not hold is flagged for each package in it. Each lockfile is read, also one in the directory of a workspace member, which cargo did not open (P-34).
  - **A file that the host cannot read** is flagged when it is new or changed (VAL-5e), and so is one that it can read on one side of the diff alone. A manifest cannot be read when it is not TOML in UTF-8, or when a part that this rule reads has another shape: a dependency table, `target`, a table under it, `workspace`, `patch`, a table under it or `replace` that is no table, or an entry that is neither a string nor a table. cargo loaded no manifest of such a shape (P-34). A manifest or a lockfile that is a symbolic link is one that the host cannot read either (VAL-5f). A lockfile cannot be read when it is not TOML in UTF-8; when its `version` is not 3 or 4; when `package` or `patch.unused` is not an array of tables; or when a package lacks a `name` or a `version`, or holds a `name`, a `version`, a `source` or a `checksum` that is no string. 3 and 4 are the formats that cargo has written for a new lockfile since its version 1.53, by its source. An older lockfile has no `version` line and may keep its checksums in `[metadata]`; none was made for P-34. A repository with one is flagged at each change to that file until cargo has written it again.
  - **A lockfile that is behind its manifests.** The lockfile shows what a feature or a version requirement brings in only where the candidate holds the lockfile that its manifests ask for, and cargo holds a build to the lockfile under `--locked` alone. Where a manifest had gained a feature and the lockfile was left as it was, `cargo build --locked` stopped with an error. `cargo build` fetched and built the package that the feature brought in, and wrote it into the lockfile of its work tree, which is in no diff (P-34). The verify commands of 11.2 carry `--locked`, and so do those of the factory repository (BOOT-6). There verify fails on such a candidate until it holds the lockfile, and the new package is flagged in that file. A repository whose verify commands leave `--locked` out has a gap: such a change to a manifest draws no flag, and a build takes a package that no diff names. The gap is accepted.
  - **A repository without a lockfile.** Where a tree holds no `Cargo.lock`, no diff shows a package that no manifest names, and each build resolves its versions anew. This rule then sees the manifests alone. The readiness report of onboarding names such a tree as an advisory item (20.1). It is not a blocking one: a library may leave its lockfile out on purpose. The report goes by the two patterns alone (VAL-5e), so a repository with a lockfile for one workspace and none for a second is not named.
  - **Outside the two files.** By the Cargo reference, cargo also takes a `[patch]`, a replaced source, the override `paths` and the address of a registry from a configuration file under `.cargo/`; P-34 ran none of them. A change to such a file is a protected path of this section. cargo takes the address of a registry from an environment variable too, and a sandboxed command has the environment that the host gives it (AG-11).
  - **The listing of a flag** names the files of this rule under the reason that each was flagged for (VAL-3). A reason is one of six, in words of the host's own that stand outside the fence, as the name of a rule does: `new dependency`, `changed source`, `patch or replace changed`, `checksum changed`, `lockfile removed` and `cannot be read`. A file that was flagged for two reasons is named under both. Under the first four, the number of entries or packages that drew the reason follows the path in parentheses (GH-5c). The names of the packages and their addresses are text that an agent chose, and one change can bring in many. They go to the journal, and the reviewer has the diff.
  - **Where the rule lives.** It is a pure function of `factory-core`, in the `rust` implementation of `Ecosystem` (9.5): the bytes of one file at the base and in the candidate go in, and the findings come out. It reads TOML with the crate `toml` (19.2), on which cargo's own types for a manifest and a lockfile rest (P-34). No crate joins the stack.

### 11.4 Continuous integration

- **VAL-6.** The operator installs the workflow template supplied by `sf repo add` (20.1). It runs on `pull_request`, `merge_group` and `push` to the default branch and on nothing else, with top-level `permissions: contents: read`, no secrets, GitHub-hosted runners, and actions pinned to full commit SHAs. It SHOULD fetch dependencies first and then build and test in a container without network access, because CI otherwise gives agent-written code an open network.
- **VAL-6a. Workflow audit.** At startup, on every default-branch head change and in `sf repo add`, the factory parses every workflow at the default-branch head. Required from M2. Dispatch in the repository pauses (as for an invalid policy, 16.3) unless:
  - every workflow that runs in the context of a factory ref (`push` matching `factory/**`, `pull_request`, `merge_group`) declares top-level `permissions` with no write scope, references no `secrets.*`, uses no `secrets: inherit`, uses GitHub-hosted runners and pins actions to SHAs;
  - `pull_request_target` and `workflow_run` workflows exclude factory branches;
  - every base-context workflow (`issues`, `issue_comment`, `pull_request_review`, `check_*`, `status`) is acknowledged in operator configuration by blob SHA, checks out no pull request head and interpolates no event text into `run:`; a changed blob pauses dispatch until re-acknowledged.

  Anything the parser cannot resolve (a reusable workflow, an unknown expression in `on:` or `permissions:`) fails closed.
- **VAL-7.** Factory pull requests come from a branch in the same repository, so they do not get the fork sandbox. Release and deployment workflows MUST live behind protected environments and MUST NOT share caches with pull request workflows. DEV-12 says how the factory repository's own release workflow does.
- **VAL-8.** The required CI checks are `required_checks = [{ name, app_id }]` in operator configuration per repository, which policy may extend but not shorten; the list is non-empty for any repository the daemon dispatches on. `name` is the check-run name as the API returns it (the job name). In `enforced` mode the factory cross-checks the list against the ruleset. The `ci` stage passes when every listed check exists on the head SHA with conclusion `success`, `neutral` or `skipped` from the listed App; it fails on any other conclusion (CI round +1). A check still missing at `ci.timeout_secs` is not a CI round: the run escalates with category `infrastructure` and does not return to `implement`.
- **VAL-9.** On a red check the factory fetches the failing job's log through the API, extracts the failing step's tail, and feeds it back as gate feedback.
- **VAL-10.** Pushes are made with the App installation token, which triggers workflows normally: a push and a ref of the scratch App's each started the workflow that the pushed commit held (P-5).

### 11.5 Reviewer

The reviewer is a read-only session with a fresh context on the candidate export. It returns:

```json
{
  "verdict": "approve | request_changes",
  "criteria": [{"id": "AC-1", "satisfied": true, "evidence": "…", "confidence": "high | medium | low"}],
  "findings": [{"severity": "blocking | major | minor", "path": "…", "line": 0, "problem": "…", "suggestion": "…"}],
  "scope_creep": ["changes not required by the issue"]
}
```

- **VAL-11.** The control plane, not the reviewer, computes the gate: it fails if any non-`manual` criterion has `satisfied: false`, any finding is `blocking`, or `scope_creep` is non-empty and policy forbids it. `major` and `minor` findings are listed in the pull request for the human.
- **VAL-12.** The reviewer never approves the pull request on GitHub (INV-5) and is never the only gate before merge.
- **VAL-13.** `review` may be disabled by repository policy for tier 0 only; for other tiers only by operator configuration. The reviewer's precision is measured against human decisions (15.2); a reviewer stage that neither blocks bad changes nor agrees with humans is a candidate for removal.

### 11.6 Evidence manifest

**Criterion evaluation** is computed by the control plane on the head SHA. A criterion whose method is `manual` is excluded from the gates and shown as "manual, for the human reviewer". Any other criterion passes when the implementer's state is `met` and, if the `review` stage ran for this head, the reviewer's `satisfied` is true.

The pull request body is generated by the host from a fixed template, with agent text passed through the outbound encoder:

1. Outcome (from the issue) and `Closes #n`.
2. Enforcement mode, risk, tier, and any integrity flags.
3. A table: each acceptance criterion → result → evidence → verification method.
4. Gate results: integrity, verify, CI, review, with round counts.
5. Where to look first (from the reviewer's findings and the largest hunks), and files hidden or collapsed in the GitHub diff.
6. What was left out, and follow-up issues filed.
7. Cost, models and factory version; run id; audit-log digest.

- **VAL-14.** A pull request is marked ready only when every non-`manual` criterion passes and the three factory checks and the required CI checks are satisfied on the head SHA.
- **VAL-14a.** Until the evidence manifest is built (M1b, 22), the draft pull request carries a cut of this template and nothing else (D-33): item 1; of item 2 the integrity flags with their listing (VAL-3), and the enforcement mode, the risk and the tier where the run holds them; of item 4 the results of integrity and verify; of item 7 the run id and the factory version. The flags and the two results come from stages that M1a builds. The rest of the template arrives with the manifest and with the stages that fill it. The outcome is text from an issue and a path is text from an agent, so the cut passes through the encoder as the whole template does (GH-5a, GH-5c).

### 11.7 Later layers

- **Author tests (M3).** A test-author session writes acceptance tests from the criteria before implementation; the host confirms they fail, captures them as a commit, and records their paths as locked for the run, each an exact path (VAL-5e; `locked_paths_sha256` in the marker, the list in the pull request body).
- **Mutation testing (M3).** `cargo mutants --in-diff` runs in CI on the pull request diff; surviving mutants are listed in the manifest. A full run is scheduled weekly and files issues.
- **Holdout scenarios (M6).** End-to-end scenarios kept in a separate private repository that no worker can read, run by the control plane against the built artifact. Only a behavioural description of a failure is returned. Evidence that this generalises is thin, so only classes that cover service behaviour require it (13.6).

---

## 12. Pull requests and merging

### 12.1 Pull request lifecycle

Draft at first publish → updated on every round → marked ready after all automated gates pass on the head SHA → review requested (4.2) → merged or reset.

### 12.2 Human review

- **PR-1.** The factory acts on submitted reviews, not on individual review comments. "Changes requested" by an authorised actor starts a human round: the review body and its line comments (from authorised actors only) become gate feedback.
- **PR-2.** An approval counts only if it is from an authorised actor and on the current head SHA. Approvals from Apps and bots never count in the factory's own check, whatever GitHub counts. Any push to the work branch invalidates earlier approvals.
- **PR-3.** If a ready pull request has had no review for 3 days *(tunable)*, the factory nudges the approver once on the dashboard issue and then stops.

### 12.3 Merge

Merge strategy per repository: `human` (the human merges; the factory observes and finishes the run when it sees the merge, 7.7), `factory_serial` (default from M2), or `native_queue` (not scheduled: merge queues are unavailable on personal-account repositories).

Under `human` the factory still runs the test-merge of step 2 on every tick while the run is in `human_review` or `merge`: a conflict starts a base update (12.4). Before base update exists (M2), `sf run` reports the conflict in the status comment and stops; the operator cancels the run and approves the issue again.

For `factory_serial`, the merge coordinator handles one pull request at a time per repository:

1. **Eligibility**, in every mode: an approval valid under PR-2, or a class decision under 13.6; the three factory checks and the required CI checks satisfied on the head SHA; the head SHA is one the factory pushed; the base is the default branch; no `factory:hold`; no stop (13.7). In `factory-enforced` mode the three factory gates are read from the journal (after a rebuild: from check runs created by the factory App).
2. **Test-merge** the head into the current default-branch head in bare `host.git` (`git merge-tree --write-tree`, with no in-tree attributes applied). If it conflicts, or if policy `merge.update_when_behind = "always"` and the branch is behind, do a base update (12.4). A test-merge whose tree fails a test of WS-12 or WS-13, with the tree of the default-branch head as the base tree, counts as one that conflicts, here and under `human`: the default branch may have gained the other path of a pair, or the other entries of a layout, since the candidate was captured. Otherwise leave the branch untouched, so the approval stays on the SHA the human saw.
3. **Re-check** approvals, hold and stop immediately before the call, then merge:
   - `enforced` mode: `PUT /repos/{o}/{r}/pulls/{n}/merge-async` with `sha`, `merge_method: "squash"`, `merge_action: "direct_merge"`, and the host-generated `commit_title` and `commit_message` (5.6); never `bypass_rules`. A 202 returns a request id: journal it and poll the request status together with the pull request's merged state. Merged with the journalled head SHA is success. A 200 means already merged: confirm the merged head SHA. A `failed` status, or a 400 or 422 from the call, re-runs step 1 and is not a Deterministic failure under 8.6; twice in a row escalates with `infrastructure`. A 409 is handled as in 8.5.
   - `factory-enforced` mode: the synchronous merge endpoint with `sha` and the same title and message, so the window between check and merge is one request. A 405 (not mergeable) or 409 (the head no longer matches `sha`) re-runs step 1 at once.
4. **Finish.** If GitHub has not closed the issue within one tick (the repository's auto-close setting may be off), the factory closes it as completed. It writes the final status comment and deletes the branch.

With the default `merge.update_when_behind = "on_conflict"`, a textually clean merge can still break the default branch. This is accepted for small pull requests. After each factory merge the reconciler reads the VAL-8 checks on the merge commit on every tick until all have concluded or `ci.timeout_secs` has passed. A failing conclusion, or a check still missing at the timeout, is a red run: it pauses merges in that repository, files a `state:triage` issue linking the merged pull request, and counts as a tripwire (15.3). The pause ends when the VAL-8 checks are satisfied on the current default-branch head, or when the operator runs `sf resume`. Runs waiting in `merge` keep their approval.

- **PR-4.** The factory never merges a pull request whose head it did not push.
- **PR-5.** Auto-merge without human approval exists only at autonomy level A3 (13.6).
- **PR-6.** `native_queue` (not scheduled) would require the factory to report its checks on merge-group commits (`gh-readonly-queue/<default>/*`), copying verdicts only when every member's head SHA is one it pushed. Its permissions and polling are specified with that milestone (probe P-19).

### 12.4 Base update

A base update brings the default branch into the work branch without rewriting it.

1. The host fetches the new default-branch head into `host.git` (WS-10) and creates an update commit there with parents (previous published head, new default-branch head). The host makes the tests of WS-12 and WS-13 on the merge result, with the tree of the new default-branch head as the base tree. Where the merge was clean and a test rejects a path, the update is handled as one that conflicted. If the test-merge was clean and no test rejected a path, its tree is the merge result and the commit is the new candidate. If it conflicted, its tree is the merge result including conflict markers; this commit is a work-in-progress base **W**: it is journalled, never pushed and never a candidate.
2. In the conflict case the host re-creates the workspace from W, writes the conflicting paths and the paths that a test of step 1 rejected to the handoff, and runs an implementer round (merge-conflict round +1). The resulting candidate, and every later candidate until one is published, has W's two parents (9.4).
3. `base_sha` and `policy_sha` in the marker become the new default-branch head (16.3). Integrity and reviewer diffs use the new `base_sha`; every stage from `integrity` on uses the policy, prompts and repository instructions of the new `policy_sha`. If that policy is invalid, the base update waits as in 16.3.
4. In the clean case the workspace is re-created from the candidate (WS-6) and the run re-enters at `integrity`; in the conflict case the pipeline continues from `implement`. All later stages run on the new head, including `human_review`. A re-approval caused by a base update does not count against the human round cap.
5. If the push of an update commit is rejected because the range carries default-branch changes under `.github/workflows/` (GH-11, probe P-20), the failure is not retried. The run does an automatic reset (7.6) if one is still available, and otherwise escalates with `policy-denial`, naming the workflow files that changed on the default branch.

Whether a base-merge push dismisses approvals under GitHub's stale-review rules when the pull request diff is unchanged is probe P-12; until answered, the factory assumes it does.

---

## 13. Security

### 13.1 Threat model

Attacker-controlled input: issue titles, bodies and comments; pull request text and review comments; repository content including dependencies, build scripts and proc macros; any web page an agent fetches; every file in a workspace, including under `.git`. The design assumes a worker can be fully subverted and asks what it can then reach.

| Threat | Control |
|---|---|
| Injection steers the agent to exfiltrate secrets | The worker holds no GitHub credential (INV-1); the environment is scrubbed; egress is default-deny (13.4). The one secret a worker can hold is the model credential: none in `gateway` mode; in `subscription` mode a token that can only make model requests, and whose literal value is searched for in every candidate and every text written to GitHub (13.5). |
| Injection makes the agent write a malicious change | Integrity flags on sensitive paths, dependencies and hidden diffs; independent reviewer; human approval by default; CI without secrets. Residual risk: a plausible backdoor passing a tired reviewer. Small pull requests and the review-queue limit are the mitigation. |
| Workspace content attacks the host (hooks, nested repositories, symlinks, FIFOs, attributes, crafted names) | INV-2 and INV-3: the host does not open workspace paths; git on a workspace runs sandboxed; objects are verified at ingest; checkout, staging, export and diffs ignore in-tree attributes (9.4, WS-11, VAL-5a). |
| Agent weakens the checks that gate it | Verify and review run on a clean export (INV-11), which is made anew for each stage execution (WS-14); protected and locked paths, read from a diff that detects no rename (VAL-5a); App without Workflows permission; checks bound to the factory App; policy and instructions read from the default branch (INV-10); workflow audit (VAL-6a). |
| A candidate carries what runs on a later checkout: a directory with the layout of a git repository, whose configuration git reads when it is started there | Capture rejects every path that a run changes below such a directory (WS-13). One that a human commits, or that a base tree holds, is left to a git setting on the operator's machines, which nothing checks (WS-15, 23.3). |
| Agent text forges factory state on GitHub (markers, closing keywords, mentions, skip tokens) | GH-5, GH-5a; host-written commit bodies and squash messages. |
| Someone other than the operator triggers or steers work | SEC-1 for every human input; SEC-1b; SEC-2 (only the operator's issues); no issue runs without its own approval (INV-4). |
| Text edited between approval and use | 5.3 rule 6; snapshot from the same read as the check; 7.7; PLAN-2. |
| Confused deputy: the factory approves or merges its own work | INV-5; PR-2; PR-4; App never a bypass actor. |
| Malicious dependency or build script | Sandbox for all builds; a new dependency and a changed source flagged (VAL-5g); `cargo deny` in verify, and in CI on the factory repository, whose verify list leaves it out (BOOT-6). |
| Agent-written code runs in CI with open network | CI has no secrets and a read-only token (VAL-6a). On a private repository its content can still leave through CI; the template's no-network build step is the control, and repositories that keep their own CI job accept the risk. |
| Runaway cost | Turn, time and usage limits enforced by the control plane; circuit breakers; kill switches (13.7). In `subscription` mode the worst case is bounded by the plan's limits, not by a bill, while usage credits are off (13.5). In `gateway` mode usage is metered by the control plane. In `direct_key` mode only the provider-side spend limit binds. |
| Data leaves through allowed channels (pull request text, branch content, the model endpoint) | The repositories are public, so the only thing of value in a sandbox is the model credential (13.5). |
| Compromised control plane | `enforced` mode: GitHub rules still require a human approval. In `factory-enforced` mode, and for A3 classes, this risk is not mitigated. |

The OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications is the review checklist for changes to this section.

### 13.2 Trust and triggering

- **SEC-1.** Human inputs that change factory behaviour are: `state:ready`, `factory:hold`, `factory:restart`, `factory:plan-approved`, `factory:stop`, `tier:*`, `risk:*`, `priority:*`, `type:ci` and `type:spec-delta`. Except as PLAN-6 provides, each is honoured only if the most recent `labeled` event for that exact label, with no later `unlabeled` event, has an actor of type User whose repository permission is write, maintain or admin when checked through the permission endpoint. `author_association` is not used. A tier, risk or type label that fails the check is read as its most restrictive value (`risk:high`, plan gate required, not `type:ci`). Issue types and fields are honoured only through their label fallbacks until probe P-17 shows their changes are attributable. Removal is covered too: an `unlabeled` event for a `tier:*`, `risk:*` or `type:*` label by anyone who is not an authorised actor is ignored; the value of the last authorised `labeled` event stays in force and the factory re-applies the label once. `state:triage`, `state:needs-human` and closing an issue are deliberately honoured from any human, because they can only stop work (7.7).
- **SEC-1a.** (Removed. The factory never applies `state:ready`; see PLAN-6 for labels on issues it files.)
- **SEC-1b.** Factory-set labels are never read back as authority: the journal and check runs are the source.
- **SEC-2.** The factory works only on issues written by the operator: the issue's author and every editor of its title and body must be authorised actors, or the factory App for an issue it filed. Any other issue is never dispatched, whoever labels it and at any sandbox level. To act on someone else's report, the operator restates it in an issue of their own. The editors of the body are those its edit history names, one for every edit, a revision whose text was deleted included; the issue's `editor` field names only the last of them. The editors of the title are the actors of its rename events. The factory App is recognised by the numeric id of its bot account (`databaseId` in GraphQL, `id` in REST) and not by its login, which GraphQL gives without the `[bot]` that REST appends (P-8).
- **SEC-3.** The snapshot contains the title, the body, and comments by authorised actors. Other comments are not shown to agents. A comment that is minimised on GitHub is not in the snapshot either, whoever minimised it and for whatever reason, and it leaves no line there (D-35): the page collapses such a comment, and SEC-4 keeps from an agent what the page hides. The claim reads `isMinimized` for each comment in its GraphQL read. A comment that its author minimised came back `true`, with its body whole, for each of the six reasons that GitHub offers (P-32).
- **SEC-4.** Before use, the text that a snapshot is made of is sanitised (D-35). The page is here what GitHub renders of a text for a reader, the HTML that probe P-32 read. The aim is that an agent reads no text which the page hides from the approver. SEC-4 pursues it for two kinds of hidden text, HTML comments and characters that nobody sees, and names below what it leaves. Where the host's reading and the page's cannot agree on one of the two, the agent loses text that the page shows and reads none that it hides. This is no boundary: the task block still introduces the snapshot as untrusted data (10.4), and safety comes from what a worker cannot reach (1.3). In this item a comment is a comment on the issue, and an HTML comment is always called so.
  - **What is sanitised.** From the body and from each comment the host removes the HTML comments and then the characters named below, in that order, in one pass that is not repeated. From the title it removes the characters alone: GitHub shows a `<!--` in a title as text (P-32). The HTML comments go first because the page reads the text as it was written: `<!`, a zero-width space and `-- x -->` are text on the page, and so they are for an agent. Nothing else changes. The text that is left is not normalised or trimmed, and a line ending in it stays as it came.
  - **Where an HTML comment can be.** The host reads the text as CommonMark, with the table extension of `pulldown-cmark` on (19.2), and takes from the parser where the raw HTML lies: the HTML blocks and the inline HTML. The raw HTML of a block is the text of its lines as the parser gives it. The marker of a block quote before such a line, or the indentation of a list item, is no part of it. A `<!--` anywhere else is text and stays: in a code span, in a code block, after a backslash, and inside a paragraph where nothing closes it. The page showed each of these (P-32). The table extension is on because GitHub splits a table row into cells before it reads a code span, so that a `<!-- x -->` which follows a vertical bar between two backticks is an HTML comment on the page.
  - **What an HTML comment is.** Inside raw HTML it is what the HTML standard's parser makes a comment, since the page hides what that parser takes for one. An HTML comment runs from `<!--` through the next `-->` or `--!>`, and `<!-->` and `<!--->` are each a whole one. The parser makes a comment of three more forms, and the host removes each through the next `>`: a `<!` that no `--` follows, which covers a declaration and a CDATA section; a `<?`; and a `</` before anything but an ASCII letter. `</>` goes as it stands. Only the HTML comment is removed, so text after its end on the same line stays. In an HTML block the host finds these forms by the characters alone. In inline HTML the parser hands over each construct by itself: a tag is kept whole with its attributes, and a construct that begins with `<!` or `<?` is read as above.
  - **An HTML comment that nothing closes.** A `<!--` in an HTML block that neither `-->` nor `--!>` follows within the block stays open after it, as it does on the page. Everything from there on is removed, Markdown text included, through the next `-->` or `--!>` in raw HTML, and to the end of the text where none follows. What lies between two pieces of raw HTML while an HTML comment is open goes with it, whole: Markdown text, and the marker or the indentation before a later line of the same block. While an HTML comment is open the host looks for the two by the characters in every piece of raw HTML, a tag of inline HTML included: the page closed one at a `-->` inside an attribute value. A `-->` in a code span or in a paragraph closes nothing, because the page writes it with a character reference. One of the other three forms that no `>` closes ends with its HTML block: on the page the next tag that GitHub writes closes it. The host reads an HTML block by the characters, so a `<!--` inside an attribute value of a block opens an HTML comment for the host and none on the page. The agent then loses text that the page shows, which is the direction of the aim. Where nothing in the block closes it, the warning below is given.
  - **Which characters.** Every code point that has the Unicode property Default_Ignorable_Code_Point, by the tables of `icu_properties` at the pin of VAL-5d (19.2), and no other. The property holds the zero-width space, the joiners, the word joiner and the byte order mark; every character that sets the direction of text; the soft hyphen; the variation selectors; and the tag characters, which can spell a whole text that nobody sees. The set of GH-5c was not taken. It holds the line feed and the tab, and the spaces outside ASCII, which have a width. A control character stays: the page showed each one that was tried as U+FFFD or left it as white space (P-32). A list of code points was not taken, for the reasons of D-33. No joiner or variation selector is kept for being part of an emoji or of a word, so an emoji of several people reaches an agent as its parts, and a heart without its selector. An exception would keep a channel that can carry bytes nobody sees. The zero-width spaces that the encoder put into an issue which the factory filed (PLAN-5) are removed too, and the agent reads the planner's text as it was.
  - **What stays.** Some text that the page does not show as text is no HTML comment and stays in a snapshot: a link reference definition; the title and the address of a link; the alternative text and the title of an image; an attribute of a tag of raw HTML, whether the page keeps it or drops it; and a table cell beyond the columns of its table, which the page drops (P-32). Removing a definition would break the links that use it.
  - **The caps.** There are three *(tunable)*: 32 KiB for the body, 8 KiB for one comment, and 64 KiB for the title, the body and the comments together. A size is counted in bytes of UTF-8 after the removal, 1 KiB is 1,024 bytes, and a text is within its cap when it is no larger. The caps are numbers of the specification, as those of AG-2b are, and not keys of policy or of operator configuration. No text is cut at a cap: half of a text can be half of a rule.
    - *The body.* An issue whose body is over its cap is not claimed (8.2). From M3 that is a failure of the readiness lint, which says so on the issue (6.4). Before M3 nothing enforces the lint (22), so the claim itself ends with no run, and `sf run` says why.
    - *A comment.* A comment over its cap is left out whole. A line that the host writes stands in its place and gives the author, the time and the reason.
    - *The total.* The title and the body are in every snapshot. The comments that SEC-3 admits get what is left of the 64 KiB after them, the newest first. The host walks back from the newest of them. A comment over its own cap is left out as above and takes nothing from what is left. The first comment that does not fit into what is left ends the walk, and it and every older one are left out as one count, not a line each. The snapshot so holds an unbroken run of the newest comments, and gives them in the order in which they were written. The lines and the count are the host's and count toward no cap, so only the number of comments bounds the lines. Going on past a comment that does not fit, as the walk of AG-2b does, was not taken: a conversation with a hole in the middle reads worse than one that starts late.
  - **What a snapshot holds.** The title, the body and a list of comments in the order written, with the count of earlier comments that were left out. Each comment has its author's login, the time it was created, and either its text or the reason it was left out. `snapshot_sha256` (5.4, 13.8) is taken over all of this, as sanitised, since it says what an agent read. `title_body_sha256` is taken over the title and the body as read from GitHub, since 7.5.1 asks it whether the operator's text changed, and an edit inside an HTML comment is a change. The exact bytes that each is taken over are fixed by the change that builds the journal in M1a, and written here then. How the task block lays a snapshot out stays with 10.4.
  - **What is reported.** Removed HTML comments and removed characters draw no warning, since the page hides them as well. The journal records the number of bytes that each of the two removals took, for the title, for the body and for each comment. An HTML comment that nothing closes within its HTML block draws a warning, whether a later piece of raw HTML closes it or it runs to the end of the text. `sf issue lint` gives the warning (6.4), and `sf run` gives it at the claim, each for the body and for every comment of the snapshot. The issue stays ready. A comment that was left out for its size draws a warning from `sf run`, at the claim and at a resume that takes a new snapshot (7.5.1), and the journal records it. From M1b the human-readable part of the status comment gives the number of comments left out (5.4).
  - **Where the code lies.** Sanitisation is a pure function of `factory-core` (19.1): the texts in, the snapshot and what was removed out. One function finds the HTML comments, for a snapshot and for a file of the instruction set (AG-2b). M1a builds both, with the texts of probe P-32 as tests (19.4, 22).
- **SEC-5.** Pull requests from forks are never processed.

### 13.3 Sandbox

```rust
trait Sandbox {
    fn capabilities(&self) -> SandboxCaps;   // fs_isolation, process_isolation, egress_filtering, vm_boundary
    async fn create(&self, run: RunId, policy: &SandboxPolicy) -> Result<Box<dyn SandboxInstance>>;
    async fn reap(&self, record: &SandboxRecord) -> Result<()>;      // after a daemon restart
}

trait SandboxInstance {
    fn record(&self) -> SandboxRecord;                               // journalled before the first exec
    fn command(&self, spec: &ExecSpec) -> Result<Command>;
    async fn kill_all(&self) -> Result<()>;
    async fn destroy(self: Box<Self>) -> Result<()>;
}
```

One instance exists per stage execution, created from that stage's policy (mounts, read-only or read-write, network) and destroyed when it ends.

| Level | Requires | Backend | Used for |
|---|---|---|---|
| `none` | — | Passthrough | The fake agent only |
| `process` | `fs_isolation`, `process_isolation`, `egress_filtering` | bubblewrap with PID, IPC, network and user namespaces and a seccomp filter (SEC-6e), and with the factory's first process as PID 1 of the sandbox (SEC-10a); a worker creates no user namespace of its own (SEC-6b) | Attended `sf run` (M1); `sf sandbox exec` (CLI-6); development |
| `hardened` | plus `vm_boundary` | One KVM microVM per stage execution (Firecracker or Cloud Hypervisor, decision D-5). The workspace or export is attached as a block device or virtio-fs share, and the per-run proxy is the VM's only network peer. | `sf daemon` (from M2); required for any auto-merge |

- **SEC-6.** Filesystem policy, deny by default:

  | Access | Paths |
  |---|---|
  | Read-write | The workspace (implementer) or the candidate export (verify); `exec/<seq>/home/`, except the configuration in it that AG-4 names; the temporary directory, which a worker sees as `/tmp` (SEC-6c); at level `process` the socket of the egress proxy, which a worker connects to (SEC-10b); the stage's target directory; `quarantine/`, which also holds the capture index (capture only) |
  | Read-only | The resolved toolchain; the directory of each check tool that the policy names (WS-7a); the agent CLI; the `sf` binary, which is the first process (SEC-10a); the host's `git` installation; `host.git/objects` and the host-written git configuration (capture only); the directory of the host-fetched advisory database, below a directory of the instance's own that stays writable (implementer and verify, VAL-1a); the role's handoff view; the workspace (planner, capture); the candidate export (reviewer); the host-written configuration in the config directory (AG-4); a backend-defined OS baseline enumerated in code and covered by a snapshot test, which holds no `/etc/claude-code` (SEC-6d) |
  | Denied | Everything else, including the host home directory, SSH agent, container sockets, credential files, the mirror and the rest of the data directory |

- **SEC-6a.** A worker cannot see, signal, trace or message a process outside its sandbox: at level `process` through PID, IPC and user namespaces with a private `/proc`; at level `hardened` through the VM boundary. INV-1 keeps every GitHub credential out of child processes regardless. The only secret in a worker's environment is the model credential (13.5).
- **SEC-6b.** A worker cannot create a user namespace, at either level (D-28). At level `process` the host's kernel is all that stands between a worker and the host. A process that creates a user namespace holds capabilities inside it, and with them it reaches kernel code that an unprivileged process does not. At level `hardened` the namespace would be one of the guest's kernel. The rule holds there too, so that a check behaves alike in every worker and a fault in the guest's kernel stays harder to reach. The bubblewrap backend is started with `--disable-userns`, and with `--unshare-user` by name: bubblewrap refuses the option where the user namespace was asked for with `--unshare-all` alone (P-36). Under the option a second `bwrap` was refused on the server with bubblewrap 0.12.0 (P-6). That and `unshare --user` were refused in the development VM with the same version, and on the hosted runners of DEV-5 with the 0.9.0 that apt installs there, on the runner and in the network namespace of DEV-6 (P-36). Each observation is of stand-in commands and not of the backend. The seccomp filter refuses the same calls as a second layer (SEC-6e), and it was not tried (P-37). How the guest refuses it is left to the change that builds the guest image (M2). The agent CLI needs no such namespace: with its own sandbox off (AG-8), its Bash tool ran under that option (P-6). A build under it is first observed by the exit check of M0 (22). A check that creates a sandbox or a container of its own therefore does not run in a worker. On the factory repository those are the sandbox tests of 19.4 (DEV-7, DEV-11). A target repository whose entry point needs one does not pass step 2 of 20.1.
- **SEC-6c.** The temporary directory of SEC-6 (P-36). It is one directory for each stage execution, the one that `TMPDIR` names (AG-11). The host creates it with mode 0700. As the worker sees its path, every directory on the way to it belongs to the worker's user or to root, and none can be written by the group or by others without the sticky bit. That is the condition on which the agent CLI places its socket for injected messages there; otherwise it tries a directory of its own under `/tmp`, and without one it runs without the socket and says so in its debug log alone. On Linux the CLI also keeps the output of a subagent's task under `TMPDIR`, where on macOS it kept both under `/tmp` (P-26), and a session with a subagent completed where `/tmp` could not be written at all. Observed on Claude Code 2.1.326, with a stand-in for the sandbox or with none. The sandbox shows that directory at `/tmp`, and `TMPDIR` holds `/tmp` (D-40). A tool that follows `TMPDIR` and a build tool that ignores it then write to the same place, and a worker has no other `/tmp`. On the host the directory is `exec/<seq>/tmp/` (9.1): it is on disk, and it is removed with the stage execution. Above `/tmp` there is only the root of the sandbox, which bubblewrap creates, so the condition above rests on that one directory. A memory-backed `/tmp` beside the directory was not taken, since what a tool wrote there would count against the host's memory. A sandbox without a writable `/tmp` was not taken either: a build tool that ignores `TMPDIR` would fail there. The CLI was not run with `TMPDIR` set to `/tmp` (P-37).
- **SEC-6d.** The OS baseline of SEC-6 shows no `/etc/claude-code`, whatever the host holds there (P-36). On Linux the agent CLI looks in that directory for managed settings, a managed MCP file, `CLAUDE.md`, rules, skills, agents, commands and output styles, under the flags of AG-3a too. A `managed-settings.json` planted there took effect in a session started with those flags, under a stand-in that showed the directory, and did not under one that mounted an empty directory over it. The other files were not planted. Observed on Claude Code 2.1.326.
- **SEC-6e.** The seccomp filter of the bubblewrap backend (D-40). It allows every system call but those of the table below. A worker holds no capability and cannot gain one in a user namespace of its own (SEC-6b), so the kernel already refuses it much of what the table names. The filter is a further layer for the day one of those two fails. It also takes from a worker kernel interfaces that an unprivileged process reaches and that no build and no agent session is known to need.
  - **Where the list comes from.** It is the filter that Flatpak hands to bubblewrap, as Flatpak's source stood on 2026-11-26, with the three calls that Flatpak leaves open in a development run and with the rows that the table marks as the factory's (`docs/research/19-bubblewrap-backend-sources.md`). An allow-list was not taken. The candidate was the default profile of Moby, which allows 359 calls by name and refuses the rest. It refuses a call that the kernel gains later, which this list does not (23.3). Its cost is two long lists to keep, one for each architecture, and a build that fails on a call the list lacks, in a way that is hard to read. Level `process` is for attended runs (SEC-7). The daemon runs at level `hardened` (D-7), where a VM stands between a worker and the host's kernel.
  - **The table.** A name that an architecture does not have is left out there.

    | Refused | With | Why |
    |---|---|---|
    | `unshare`, `setns`; `clone` whose flags hold a bit of `0x7E020000` | `EPERM` | No namespace of a worker's own (SEC-6b). Flatpak refuses `clone` for a user namespace alone. The mask is that of Moby's profile, every flag of `clone` that creates a namespace, and is the factory's |
    | `clone3` | `ENOSYS` | Its flags lie in a structure, which a filter cannot read. Flatpak's source gives the error code so that a program falls back to `clone` |
    | `mount`, `umount`, `umount2`, `pivot_root`, `chroot` | `EPERM` | The mount tree and the root of the sandbox |
    | `open_tree`, `move_mount`, `fsopen`, `fsconfig`, `fsmount`, `fspick`, `mount_setattr` | `ENOSYS` | The same, through the newer calls |
    | `add_key`, `keyctl`, `request_key` | `EPERM` | The kernel keyring |
    | `syslog`, `acct`, `quotactl`, `uselib`, `modify_ldt` | `EPERM` | The kernel log, process accounting, quotas, and two calls that Flatpak's source calls old or a source of leaks |
    | `move_pages`, `mbind`, `get_mempolicy`, `set_mempolicy`, `migrate_pages` | `EPERM` | The placement of memory |
    | `perf_event_open`, `ptrace`; `personality` with an argument other than `PER_LINUX` | `EPERM` | Profiling, tracing and another execution domain. These are the three that Flatpak leaves open in a development run |
    | `process_vm_readv`, `process_vm_writev` | `EPERM` | The memory of another process, which `ptrace` reaches too. The factory's |
    | `bpf`, `userfaultfd` | `EPERM` | The factory's. Moby's profile allows neither without a capability |
    | `io_uring_setup`, `io_uring_enter`, `io_uring_register` | `ENOSYS` | The factory's. Moby's profile names none of the three |
    | `ioctl` with the request `TIOCSTI` or `TIOCLINUX`, compared in its low 32 bits | `EPERM` | Input put into a terminal (CVE-2017-5226, CVE-2023-28100). The backend also starts bubblewrap with `--new-session`, which leaves a worker no controlling terminal |
    | `socket` with a family other than `AF_UNSPEC`, `AF_UNIX`, `AF_INET`, `AF_INET6` and `AF_NETLINK` | `EAFNOSUPPORT` | Flatpak's list of families, without the two that it allows on a flag |
    | On x86-64, every call of the x32 ABI: a call number from `0x40000000` | `ENOSYS` | Such a call arrives under the architecture of x86-64 with another number, so no row above matches it. The factory's |
    | Every call of another architecture | The process is killed | The 32-bit calls of either architecture have other numbers too. `seccompiler` puts this check at the head of every program |

  - **What a refusal is.** An error, and never a signal: the call returns with the error code of its row and the program goes on. `ENOSYS` stands where a program is expected to have another way and to take it when the kernel says that it lacks the call. Flatpak's source says that of `clone3`; of `io_uring` the factory assumes it. The call of another architecture is the one exception.
  - **How it is built.** `factory-sandbox` holds the table as data and compiles it with `seccompiler` when it creates an instance (19.1, 19.2). That crate gives a program one action for all of its rules, so the backend compiles one program for each error code, which makes three. It keys a rule by one exact call number, so the row for the x32 ABI is a fourth program, of a few instructions, that the backend writes itself for x86-64. bubblewrap takes the programs with `--add-seccomp-fd` and applies them as its last step before it starts the first process (SEC-10a). They hold for that process and for everything it starts. The snapshot test of 19.4 holds every name with its condition and its error code, for each architecture.
  - **What it leaves.** `prctl` and `seccomp` stay allowed, so a worker can add a filter of its own, which can only refuse more. With `ptrace` refused, a debugger and `strace` do not work in a worker. No argument is looked at but those that the table names. This is the filter of the bubblewrap backend. What the guest of level `hardened` runs under is left to the change that builds the guest image (M2).
  - **What nobody observed.** No program has run under this filter. Whether a cold build or an agent session makes a call that it refuses, and what each then does, is probe P-37. Where one does, the table changes with the probe's record.
