## 23. Open items

### 23.1 Decisions

Decided by the operator on 2026-11-18 (D-18 and D-20 on 2026-11-19; the rule of D-16 about the server confirmed on 2026-11-20; D-21 to D-27, D-29 to D-31, D-33 and D-34 on 2026-11-21; D-28, D-32, D-35, D-37 and D-38 on 2026-11-22; D-39 on 2026-11-25; D-40 to D-43 on 2026-11-26):

| # | Decision |
|---|---|
| D-1 | The factory's repository is public on the operator's personal account, so GitHub enforces the ruleset (`enforced` mode). |
| D-2 | The model credential is the operator's own Claude Max subscription. There is no API-key fallback: when plan limits are reached the factory pauses. |
| D-3 | The host is a Linux server on the operator's own hardware with KVM. macOS is not a supported host. |
| D-7 | The daemon always runs at sandbox level `hardened`. (An earlier draft accepted `process` for the daemon; with KVM available that is no longer needed.) |
| D-10 | Target repositories are Rust first; language-specific parts sit behind the `Ecosystem` interface so others can follow (9.5). |
| D-11 | The goal is lights-out operation, earned per class of work (13.6). The operator approves every issue, including each sub-issue of a feature. |
| D-12 | The factory stops at merge. Releases and deployments stay human actions. |
| D-13 | The factory works only on issues the operator wrote (SEC-2). |
| D-16 | M0 and M1 are built by Claude Code sessions on the operator's Mac, one pull request per issue, each merged by the operator (BOOT-1). Every step on the server is the operator's to run; a session never connects to it by itself. The server was reached only from the operator's home when this was decided. The operator now also reaches it over a VPN (4.4), and on 2026-11-20 confirmed that the rule stays. Linux-only code is developed in a local VM (DEV-3). Every check runs before each commit through one command (DEV-1). |
| D-17 | The factory's repository, code and documents alike, is licensed `MIT OR Apache-2.0`, copyright Marc Tremblay. Every crate inherits the licence from the workspace, and the structural test (19.1) checks it. Source files carry no per-file licence header. |
| D-18 | `SPEC.md` stays one file through M0 and M1 and keeps its ids (`INV-`, `GH-`, `SEC-` and so on); it is not split into the living-spec layout of 6.3. Issues in that period cite those ids, with `SPEC.md` as the spec file path. The spec is protected through the repository's policy (BOOT-4). |
| D-20 | CI for the factory repository runs on GitHub-hosted runners only, with one job for x86-64 Linux and one for arm64 Linux; there is no macOS job and no self-hosted runner. Nothing that needs KVM or the Claude Code login runs in CI. Once the workflow exists, a ruleset on `main` requires both jobs and has no bypass actor; the ruleset of 20.3 replaces it at M1b (DEV-5 to DEV-8). D-41 changed the last point: the ruleset stays, beside that of 20.3. |
| D-21 | The conventions of the factory repository (20.4). `GLOSSARY.md` at its root is the one home of the terms; section 2 points at it. The file is protected with the spec (BOOT-4) and its citations are checked (DEV-2). `AGENTS.md` is a map: it states a rule itself only where a session that read nothing else would do harm without it. Through M1a it is written for the interactive sessions of D-16; what a worker receives of it (AG-2a) is decided when the repository's policy is written. None of the labels of 5.2 exists on the factory repository before its onboarding: they are created at step 5 of 20.1, on the operator's instruction, and a session creates none of them before that. Their absence guards nothing by itself: the REST API creates a label that is added by name (P-25). The repository has no `docs/adr/` through M1; this table is its decision record. |
| D-22 | The limits of M1 (section 14). M1 enforces the session limits only, which are the turns, the wall clock and the `implement` spend, and records the rest (BUD-7). Spend stays in API-equivalent dollars as the agent CLI counts them (BUD-6). The values are 200 turns, 90 minutes and $20 for an `implement` session. The dollars are at the list prices of `claude-opus-5-5`, the model the operator runs the factory's implementation on. The values rest on a measurement of the operator's own interactive sessions, most of them on `claude-fable-5-2`, the model the operator writes the specification with, and priced again for the other model (`docs/research/15-session-sizes.md`). They stay tunable. Reaching a limit ends `sf run` with a report, and running it again is the operator's grant (BUD-7). A session that reports usage credits in use is stopped, and credits that are switched on but unused draw a warning (BUD-8). No limit is set in units of the plan; the utilisation is recorded (BUD-9). Decided the same day, after the first answer: the $20 stands; an agent session may use subagents, whose calls count as turns and whose spend the CLI's own cap bounds until probe P-26 shows what the stream gives (AG-20, BUD-10); a restart by `sf run` after a limit tells the fresh session which limit was reached and does not count toward Poison (BUD-7); and the warning is drawn by any event that does not show credits refused, since the operator does not switch credits on to observe another state (BUD-8). What the daemon does while usage credits are switched on, and every other limit of section 14, waits for M2 (D-14). Probe P-26 observed the stream the same day and the rules for subagents stand. Two things were added on its evidence, and the operator was not asked about either: a subagent runs in the foreground (AG-11, BUD-10), and the CLI is told to forward a subagent's text (AG-1). |
| D-23 | The pre-commit hook of the factory repository, and how a Claude Code session is kept from skipping it. The hook is a committed file named by `core.hooksPath`, with no hook manager, and it runs `cargo xtask check` and nothing else (DEV-9). A `PreToolUse` hook that runs a task of `xtask` refuses the session a commit or a push that skips git's hooks, in place of a deny rule in `.claude/settings.json` (DEV-10). `cargo xtask check` is written now, with the checks that exist, and is not left to the rest of M0 (DEV-1). |
| D-24 | Which paths of the factory repository are protected as its gate code, and where they are listed (BOOT-4). All of `crates/**` and all of `xtask/**`, as whole trees and not as named crates or modules; `.githooks/**`; and `Cargo.toml` and `mise.toml`. A later file that configures a check of DEV-2, the development VM (DEV-3) or the release build joins them in the change that adds it. `Cargo.lock` is not listed. BOOT-4 states the list and the repository's policy carries it in `[paths] protected_extra`, as D-18 arranged for the specification. The built-in list of 11.3 does not change, and `.githooks/**` is not added to it. The structural test checks the policy against BOOT-4, and that part of it is written with the policy (DEV-2). A flag's listing names the paths and the pattern each matched (VAL-3), because on this repository the label no longer tells one protected change from another. The consequence is accepted: no class of work that changes code can merge at A3 on the factory repository, so it does not reach lights-out (13.6) while the list stands. Narrowing the list is a new decision, for when M4 scopes the classes and there is code to draw a line through. "Gate code" is not a term of `GLOSSARY.md`; BOOT-4 says by path what it covers. |
| D-25 | How a path pattern is anchored to the repository root (VAL-5b). Matching at any depth stays the default, so every pattern written before this decision keeps its meaning, and a pattern that begins with `/` matches from the root only. The reading of `.gitignore`, in which a `/` inside a pattern anchors it, was not taken: it would have made `.cargo/**` and `crates/**` root-only with no change to their text. No pattern of the built-in list of 11.3 is anchored, and policy cannot anchor one. The lists `generated` and `lockfiles` of policy are read the same way, so the anchor works in them. A pattern that matches too much is safe only where a match adds a flag, and VAL-5b says so for the lists that exempt or admit what they match. VAL-5 already read every path pattern at any depth, so the instruction directories (AG-2a) and a class predicate's allowlist (13.6) keep that reading and gain the anchor; the operator was not asked about either. The factory repository's own list stays unanchored, as BOOT-4 wrote it, and its `SPEC.md` pattern is looked at again with D-19. |
| D-26 | When the factory repository's policy is written and what its onboarding brings (BOOT-6), with three rules for every target repository that the question turned up (AG-21, GH-3a, VAL-3a). The repository is onboarded by hand from the checklist of 20.1, at the start of M1b and after probe P-28, in one pull request that a session writes and the operator merges. The policy is not written before that, and not by an early cut of `sf repo add`. The one real issue of M1a runs on the scratch repository from a development build, so the first run on the factory repository is the first issue of M1b (BOOT-1, 22). The policy holds what the pipeline of M1b reads and nothing for a later stage: `claude-opus-5-5` for `implement`, the verify commands as a list of its own and not `cargo xtask check`, the merge strategy `human`, the paths of BOOT-4, and no limits of its own. Its instruction set is `AGENTS.md` and `GLOSSARY.md`, and a worker reads `SPEC.md` from the workspace. The default instruction directory of AG-2a becomes `/docs/**`, anchored, and a pattern there may name a file. `AGENTS.md` gets no part written for a worker: the built-in role prompt says once, for every repository, what in such a file is not addressed to one (AG-21). That settles what D-21 left to this decision. `docs/agents/**` joins the protected paths of BOOT-4, which adds to the list of D-24, because the policy does not protect it as an instruction directory. The factory reads a repository's label set before its first label write and claims nothing where a label of 5.2 is missing, a name in another letter case counting as missing (GH-3a). D-21 stands on when the labels are created. `CODEOWNERS` holds an entry for every protected path, the patterns of `protected_extra` included. Each entry is translated by a rule taken from GitHub's documentation, and what case folding covers beyond it is given up. Probe P-28 observes the entries before they are relied on, and on the factory repository the structural test keeps the file in step with the policy (VAL-3a). The operator was not asked about four things: where the `CODEOWNERS` file lies; what a role prompt that a repository's policy rewrites has to keep of AG-21; that the reviewer's role prompt carries the statement of AG-21 too; and that the instruction directories, which 11.3 protects, have `CODEOWNERS` entries with the two lists. D-37 has since decided the first of the four, after probe P-28. |
| D-27 | How a check tool, a program of a repository's checks that is no part of its toolchain, reaches a worker (WS-7a). WS-7 put only the toolchain on the worker `PATH`, and SEC-6 let a worker read nothing that holds `cargo-nextest`, which the verify list of BOOT-6 names. A check tool is installed on the host and shown to the worker read-only, on the `PATH` after the toolchain, and from M2 the guest image holds it (SEC-9). It is not part of the OS baseline, which is one set for every repository, and it is not built in the sandbox by a setup command, which would cost a build on every stage execution and leave the binary writable by the candidate's build scripts. The policy names the check tools in `[tools]`, each at one exact version. `mise.toml` read as data was not taken: every target repository would need mise, and the host would have to read mise's tool names and backends. Operator configuration was not taken either, since the list belongs with the repository whose checks need it. There is one set and no list per role, so the implementer has what verify has. The operator installs each tool and `sf` installs none; operator configuration names one tools directory, and the host looks under it for `<name>/<version>/bin` without running the tool. A missing check tool blocks onboarding, is reported by `sf doctor`, and stops every claim on the repository, and the claim rule covers a missing toolchain or component as well, which WS-7 had covered at onboarding only (8.2). The factory repository's policy names every check tool that `cargo xtask check` runs, held to the pins of `mise.toml` by the structural test (BOOT-6, DEV-2). "Check tool" is a term of `GLOSSARY.md`. Whether a worker has to be able to run `cargo xtask check` whole was left open on purpose, to be decided before the first run of M1b (D-28): `cargo mutants --in-diff` needs a base to diff against, and the sandbox tests of 19.4 need a sandbox inside the worker's, and whether a worker may create a user namespace is decided nowhere. D-28 has since decided it, and the user namespace with it (SEC-6b). The operator was not asked about six things: that a name begins with a letter and a version with a letter or a digit, which keeps `..` from being a version; that the sandbox shows a worker the directory of each named tool and not the tools directory whole; that the check is step 1 of the claim, before the run row, and that a tool missing later is a Deterministic failure, which is not retried (8.6); that the check tools join the `PATH` by one rule in every ecosystem, and that the `Ecosystem` interface offers, with its default commands, the check tools they need, at a version nobody has chosen (9.5); that the development VM holds the check tools for the run of M1a (BOOT-6); and that the term covers a program of any check of a repository, where the answer spoke of its setup and verify commands, since the factory repository names tools that neither list runs. |
| D-28 | What `cargo xtask check` does inside a worker's sandbox, which D-27 left open. `AGENTS.md` tells a session to run the command before each commit, a worker reads `AGENTS.md` (AG-2a), and D-27 gave a worker the check tools. Two steps of DEV-2 need more than the tools: the sandbox tests of 19.4 create a sandbox of their own, and `cargo mutants --in-diff` needs a base to diff against. A worker does not run the command whole: it runs every step but the sandbox tests. A worker cannot create a user namespace, at level `process` or at level `hardened` (SEC-6b). At `process` the host's kernel is the only wall between a worker and the host that holds every credential. At `hardened` the namespace would reach the guest's kernel alone, and allowing it would have let the sandbox tests of level `process` run in a worker from M2; those of level `hardened` could not run there either way, since a guest is given no KVM. One rule for both levels was taken, so that the command behaves alike in every worker and a fault in the guest's kernel stays harder to reach. The agent CLI needs no such namespace (P-6). The cost is that a change to `factory-sandbox` is first tested in CI, and that no target repository's checks can nest a sandbox or a container in a worker. The sandbox tests become two steps of the command, one for each level, and leave the plain nextest run (DEV-2a). The verify list of BOOT-6 holds `cargo nextest run --workspace --locked`, which would otherwise have run those tests in verify's sandbox and failed there. The list stays word for word a step of the command, and verify does not test the sandbox on the factory repository; CI does, at level `process`. How the tests are kept out of the plain run is left to the change that builds them. The command keeps one set of steps, and a step has a third outcome, not run (DEV-11). In a worker's sandbox the sandbox steps are reported as not run, each with its reason, the other steps run, and the command exits with 0 and names in its last line what was not run. Anywhere else a step that cannot run fails by name. Failing by name in a worker was not taken: the command, which a session is told must pass, could not pass there. A shorter form of the command for a worker was not taken, because it is a second set (DEV-1). The host says where the command runs, in the variable `SF_SANDBOX` (AG-11a). The variable is information and no control. Trying to create a namespace was not taken, since that cannot tell a worker from a hosted runner that has lost its user namespaces. A flag was not taken, since the implementer would have to be told of it for each repository. The mutation step runs in a worker: found there a surviving mutant costs time inside the session, and found in CI it costs a round. Its diff runs from a base commit to the work tree as it stands (DEV-2a). In a session and in CI the base is the merge base of `HEAD` and `origin/main`, of which the workflow fetches enough (DEV-6), and in CI on a push to `main` the diff is empty. In a worker the base is the commit that the variable `SF_BASE_SHA` names (AG-11a). A local branch left at the base was not taken: an agent that checks it out and commits moves it, and the step would then cover less without saying so. The built-in role prompt tells the implementer two things, for every repository: to run before it finishes a check that the repository instructions tell a session to run before a commit, and to leave as it is a check that could not run in the sandbox and say so in `notes` (AG-21). `AGENTS.md` gets no part for a worker, the policy no key and the factory repository no prompt of its own. This change is the specification alone, and `xtask` does not change with it. The mutation step is built in M0 with the rest of DEV-2, each sandbox step with its tests, the third outcome with the first step that needs it, and the variables in M1a (22). The operator was not asked about eight things: how the guest of level `hardened` refuses a user namespace, which is left to the change that builds the guest image; that a target repository whose entry point nests a sandbox does not pass step 2 of 20.1; that the two statements are in the implementer's prompt and not the planner's, which is given the repository instructions too and runs no check; that no worker runs the tests that need the Claude Code login (DEV-7); how the command knows a hosted runner, for the step at level `hardened`; that each sandbox step and the third outcome arrive with the sandbox tests, the step at level `hardened` in M2, where the answer put them in M0 (DEV-7); that a reset replaces the value of `SF_BASE_SHA` as a base update does (7.6); and that the exit check of M0 starts bubblewrap as SEC-6b says (22). |
| D-29 | What a path pattern may hold and which spellings of a path are one name (VAL-5c, VAL-5d), which D-25 left open. A pattern has three wildcards: `*` and `?` inside one component, never across a `/`, and `**` as a whole component, for none or more components and for at least one where it ends the pattern. So `**/*.snap` matches a root `a.snap`, and `crates/**` does not match a file named `crates`. There are no character classes, no braces and no escape: `[`, `]`, `{`, `}` and `\` are reserved, so that a later grammar can use them without changing a valid pattern. The grammar of `.gitignore` and that of `globset` were not taken: `CODEOWNERS` has no form for a class or a brace (VAL-3a), and nothing in this specification needs one. A pattern names files and never a directory. `migrations` matches a file of that name, the tree is written `migrations/**`, and a trailing `/` is unreadable; the reading of `.gitignore` was not taken, because in `generated` and `lockfiles` it would exempt whatever lies in a directory with a matching name. A bare name that is a directory of the tree draws a warning from `sf policy check` and at onboarding (CLI-5, 20.1). A pattern and a path are folded by Unicode's canonical caseless matching, which is full case folding with canonical normalisation, and are compared in NFC. The aim is what a case-insensitive file system takes for one name: APFS on the operator's Mac took `AGENTſ.md` for `AGENTS.md`, and git folds ASCII letters only (P-29). Folding ASCII alone, as git does, and compatibility folding, which is wider than any file system that was observed, were not taken. The code points that HFS+ ignores and the spellings of NTFS are not folded, and that gap is accepted. A pattern that cannot be read makes the policy invalid (16.3); none is skipped. `factory-core` carries its own matcher, and the Unicode tables come from ICU4X, `icu_casemap` and `icu_normalizer` at 2.4.0, which carry Unicode 18.0.0 (19.2). `globset` behind a wrapper was not taken: it matches `?` against a byte and folds ASCII only, so the wrapper would be larger than the matcher. `unicode-normalization` with `caseless` was not taken either: the two carry different Unicode versions, 18.0.0 and 16.0.0. "Path pattern" is a term of `GLOSSARY.md`. The operator was not asked about nine things: that a last `**` stands for at least one component and any other for none or more, though both examples that follow from it were shown; that a control character in a pattern stands for itself; that the fold is computed as toNFC(toCasefold(toNFD(x))), with the default mappings and no Turkic rule, which the examples showed; how a path that is not valid UTF-8 is matched; that the warning for a pattern which matches no path covers a pattern with a wildcard too; that an unreadable pattern in a class predicate's allowlist is an error of the operator configuration; that the matcher's work is bounded by the product of the lengths of pattern and path; which properties the tests of 19.4 check; and that the term joins the glossary. The scan of 9.4 keeps git's rule for `.git` and does not use this fold. What `CODEOWNERS` makes of `?`, of a `**` between two components, of a space, of a leading `#` and of a name without a wildcard joins probe P-28. |
| D-30 | How the rules of 11.3 are read that name a path outside the pattern lists (VAL-5e), which D-25 did not cover. They use the matcher and the fold of D-25 and D-29, and no rule compares a path in a second way. The workflow rule is anchored to the root and folded, as the pattern `/.github/workflows/**`. GitHub's documentation stores workflow files in that directory of a repository, the protected path `.github/**` already flags a nested one, and a `fail` there would cost a verify round and, in a `type:ci` issue, a patch for a human to commit. Any depth was not taken for that reason. The exact letter case that GitHub reads was not taken either: it would be a second way of matching, used by one rule. A build script is `build.rs` at any depth and any file that a manifest names in `package.build`, at the base or in the candidate, and a change of that key is flagged too. The files that a build script includes are not followed. The name alone was not taken, because a build script under another name would go unflagged together with the manifest change that names it. Nor was `build.rs` only where a manifest lies beside it, which spares a flag and no more. The dependency rule reads every `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock` of the tree. The root workspace alone was not taken: its members are written in manifests that the candidate can change, and a second workspace would go unread. A locked path is the exact path of a file of the `author_tests` commit, compared after the fold. A comparison byte for byte, patterns over the directories the test author wrote into, and leaving the form to M3 were not taken. Decided the same day, after the first answers: M1a builds the rules that match a path alone, which are the workflow rule and the name `build.rs`, and M1b builds the dependency rule and the reading of `package.build` (22); building all of them in M1a, and the reading of `package.build` alone with it, were not taken. "Exact path" and "locked path" are terms of `GLOSSARY.md`. The operator was not asked about thirteen things: that an exact path holds no wildcard and no reserved character; that the files of the computed instruction set are exact paths too; that a file or a symbolic link whose own path is `.github/workflows` is flagged and does not fail; that the manifests read for `package.build` are those the pattern `Cargo.toml` matches, read as data at the base and in the candidate; how a name there becomes a path, and that a value which is not a string names no file; that a change of the key is flagged whatever its values, `false` included; that a manifest or a lockfile the host cannot read is flagged when it is new or changed; that the list `lockfiles` of policy plays no part in the dependency rule, with VAL-5 now saying that its lockfile is the size budget's; that a locked path is spelled as the tree spells it and no directory is locked; that the locked-path rule reads the diff from the `author_tests` commit and not from `base_sha`; that another ecosystem names its manifests, lockfiles and build scripts in the same two forms (9.5); that the examples of VAL-5e join the test tables of 19.4; and that the two terms join the glossary. Left open: what a new dependency or a changed source is, field by field (D-32); a symbolic link whose path is the name of a protected directory, `.github` or `.cargo` for example, which matches no built-in pattern and so draws no flag (D-31); and the lockfile that the dependency-bump predicate of 13.6 admits, which the operator left with D-14. What GitHub makes of a workflow directory below the root, in another letter case or behind a symbolic link joins probe P-5. |
| D-31 | What the integrity check makes of a symbolic link (VAL-5f), which D-30 left open. It flags every path of the diff that is a symbolic link at the base or in the candidate: a link added, removed or pointed elsewhere, a link where a file was, and a file where a link was. A pattern that names a tree, `.cargo/**` for example, does not match a link whose own path is `.cargo` (VAL-5c), capture admits the link (9.4), and cargo reads its configuration through it (P-30). The bare name of each protected directory in the built-in list, and a capture that refuses a link at such a name, were not taken: neither covers a link one directory up, and policy would write each tree of `protected_extra` twice. A link whose path is `.github` or `.github/workflows` is flagged as any other and does not fail. On the scratch repository GitHub took nothing behind either link for a workflow (P-5), so the link is no workflow change. A `fail` was not taken; it would have spared the run a rejected push, should GitHub reject the App's push of such a link, which was not observed then; GitHub has since accepted that push (P-5). A link whose path is `target` is flagged and does not fail as an added path under `target/**` does: build output does not go there (9.3). The listing of a flag names a link's target beside its path, because the target is what the operator may have to protect; the path alone, with the target left to the diff, was not taken. A link that is in the base tree is not followed: a change to the file it points at is flagged only where a pattern protects that file. Onboarding lists every symbolic link of the default-branch head with its target, as an advisory item (20.1), and the operator adds a target to `protected_extra`. An integrity check that resolves the links of the base tree was not taken, since it would read relative targets, chains and loops in the gate; nor was a blocking item, since a `CLAUDE.md` that is a link to `AGENTS.md` is common. The lists that loosen a control stay as they are (VAL-5b): the host follows no link, so a link widens none of them, and a head with a flag does not merge at A3. M1a builds the rule (22); M1b was not taken. The operator was not asked about eight things: that the rule is one of its own, VAL-5f, and that a link draws the outcome of every other rule that matches its path as well; that the rule is the same in every ecosystem (9.5); that for a link the candidate no longer holds, the listing names the target at the base; that a `Cargo.toml` or a `Cargo.lock` that is a link counts as a file the host cannot read (VAL-5e); that a link which `generated` or `lockfiles` matches is exempt from the size budget with the lines of its own diff; that the examples of VAL-5f join the tests of 19.4; that what cargo and git do with a link is a probe of its own, P-30, observed on the operator's Mac and not on the server; and that no term joins `GLOSSARY.md`. A link that a human commits to the default branch after onboarding is reported by nothing, and that gap is accepted. Left open: whether GitHub rejects the App's push of a link at `.github` or `.github/workflows`, and what it makes of such a link on the default branch or for an event other than a push, which stay with P-5; and how the draft pull request of M1a writes a path or a target of the listing, since the outbound encoder is built in M2 (D-33). |
| D-32 | What the dependency rule of 11.3 flags in the files that VAL-5e names (VAL-5g), which D-30 left open. The Cargo reference was read first, and cargo 1.100.0 was run on what it leaves unsaid (P-34). The rule flags a change through which a build can take code from a package, or from a place, that the base did not name. Which version of a package comes from the same place is left to `cargo deny` and to the dependency-bump predicate (13.6), and where the host cannot tell, it flags. In a manifest the dependency tables are `dependencies`, `dev-dependencies` and `build-dependencies`, at the top level and under `target`, and `workspace.dependencies`, with the underscore spellings `dev_dependencies` and `build_dependencies`, which cargo read in a package of edition 2021 and the reference does not name. All count alike. A new dependency is an entry whose key is new in its table of that file. Every entry of an added manifest is therefore new, and so is one that moved to another table or was given another key; a rule that says which entry is the same one under another name was not taken. An entry of `workspace.dependencies` is new whether or not a member inherits it. A `path` dependency and a new crate of a workspace are flagged like any other. Exempting a path that stays inside the repository was not taken: it needs the path arithmetic of `package.build`, and a package that a `[patch]` by `path` put in the place of another has no `source` in the lockfile either, as a crate of the tree has none. The cost is a flag on a change that adds a crate or joins two crates of one repository. A changed source is a change to an entry in any key but `version`, `features`, `default-features` in either spelling, `optional` and `public`. The rule names the keys that do not draw the flag, so that an unknown key draws it. A list of the keys that name a source was not taken: written from the reference it would have missed `registry-index`, which cargo followed to a registry of its own. A changed version requirement, an added feature and a dependency that is no longer optional are not flagged. A feature can bring in a package that no manifest names, and the lockfile shows that package. Any change to `[patch]` or `[replace]` is flagged, in any manifest, a removal included. A lockfile is flagged for each pair of `name` and `source` that it holds and the base file does not. The `source` is compared whole, with the commit of a git package, and having none is a value. A package that no manifest names is therefore a new dependency, which the predicate of 13.6 assumed already, and another version of a pair that was there is not flagged. The entries of `[[patch.unused]]` count as packages. A checksum that changed under an unchanged name, version and source is flagged. A removed lockfile is flagged, and a removed manifest or dependency is not. A manifest that is TOML and has another shape where the rule reads it, and a lockfile in a format other than 3 or 4, are files the host cannot read (VAL-5e); cargo loaded no manifest of such a shape. The listing of a flag names each file under a reason in the host's own words, with a count, and the names of the packages go to the journal (VAL-3, GH-5c). Onboarding reports a tree that holds a manifest and no lockfile, as an advisory item (20.1). A blocking item was not taken, since a library may leave its lockfile out on purpose. The rule is a pure function of `factory-core` on the `toml` crate, no crate joins the stack (19.2), no term joins `GLOSSARY.md`, and M1b builds the rule (22). The operator was asked these points in two rounds on 2026-11-22, fourteen questions in all, and took each as it was put. A fifteenth came from the review of this change. The lockfile shows what a feature or a version requirement brings in only where the candidate holds the lockfile that its manifests ask for, and cargo holds a build to it under `--locked` alone: without that option it fetched and built a package that no diff named (P-34). The operator took the gap as it is, for a repository whose verify commands leave `--locked` out; those of 11.2 and of the factory repository carry it. An advisory item at onboarding for a cargo command without the option was not taken, since it needs a rule for which commands take the option. Flagging a change of `version`, `features`, `default-features` or `optional` that comes with no change to a lockfile was not taken either: it flags the many such changes that rightly leave the lockfile as it is. Eight things were settled in the writing and not asked: the ids VAL-5g and P-34; that a new pair of a lockfile is listed as a new dependency where its name is new and as a changed source otherwise; which shapes of a lockfile the host cannot read beside its format, a package without a `name` or a `version` for example, and that a table under `target` or under `patch` which is no table makes a manifest one of those files too; that a file which the host can read on one side of the diff alone is flagged as one it cannot read; that `[patch]` or `[replace]` added or removed as a whole table is a change to it; that the rule does not read `[features]`, `[profile]`, `[lints]` and the rest of `[workspace]`; how a line of the listing carries the count, which GH-5c now says; and the item of VAL-5g on what cargo takes from a configuration file and from the environment, which changes no rule. Left open: which `Cargo.lock` the dependency-bump predicate admits, which stays with D-14; and what P-34 did not run, which is another registry, a vendored source, the configuration file, a lockfile of an older format, and the server. |
| D-33 | How text that an agent chose reaches GitHub in M1 (GH-5a, GH-5c, VAL-14a), which D-31 left open. GH-5a has all such text pass through one outbound encoder, section 22 built the encoder in M2, and M1a already publishes a draft pull request whose flag listing names paths. M1a builds the encoder whole, and capture takes the forms of a reference from it for the commit subjects it rejects (9.4). The status comment, the check runs and the evidence manifest of M1b use it, and the M2 row no longer names it. M1b, with a pull request body in M1a that holds no such text, was not taken: a flag listing cannot leave out the paths. A part of the encoder in M1a was not taken either: it is a pure function, and the cases of probe P-15 are its tests. Until the evidence manifest of M1b, the draft pull request carries a cut of the template of 11.6: the outcome and `Closes #n`; the enforcement mode, the risk and the tier; the integrity flags with their listing; the results of integrity and verify; the run id and the factory version. A path in a listing, and the target of a symbolic link, takes one line inside the fence. A control character, a character that takes no width or sets the direction of text, a backslash and a double quote are written as escapes, between double quotes: `\n`, `\u{202E}`, and `\xFF` for a byte that is not UTF-8. A letter outside ASCII stays as it is. A capture that refuses such a path was not taken, since a diff can delete one that is in the base tree. git's own quoting was not taken: it writes a byte in octal, and by git's documentation of `core.quotePath` it escapes every byte above 0x80 with that setting on, a letter such as `é` among them, and none of them with it off. Which characters are escaped is said by Unicode properties: the general categories Cc, Cf, Zl and Zp, Zs other than the space, and Default_Ignorable_Code_Point, from `icu_properties` at the pin of 19.2, which the two crates of VAL-5d bring in already. A list of code points written into GH-5c was not taken: the first one missed the tag characters, the variation selectors and the spaces outside ASCII, and a list goes stale with each Unicode version. The encoder is a pure function of `factory-core`, beside the sanitisation of SEC-4, and `factory-github` calls it on every write. GH-5a wants one list of the forms of a reference, for the insertions and for the subjects that capture rejects, and `factory-git` may not depend on `factory-github` (19.1). Keeping the encoder there with the forms alone in core was not taken. No dependency between crates of the workspace changes. A listing names at most 50 paths for one flagged rule *(tunable)* and then the number of the rest, and the journal holds them all. On the factory repository BOOT-4 protects nearly all code, so a large change would list hundreds. The output of a check run passes through the encoder like every other surface. Probe P-15 wrote to none, and a probe before M1b was not taken: the rule holds whatever GitHub does with a mention there. The operator was not asked about twelve things: the ids GH-5c and VAL-14a; that a path which is empty or holds a space is written in quotes too; how a line is laid out, with the arrow before a link's target and the matched pattern in parentheses, and that a pattern is written in the same form; that hexadecimal digits are capitals without a leading zero; that the escape is applied before the encoder, so that a zero-width space in a listed path is the encoder's; that the form holds wherever the host writes a path to GitHub as a path, the summary of a check run and item 5 of 11.6 included; that the encoder's cap on length cuts a listing between lines only; that the 50 paths are the first in byte order; that the enforcement mode, the risk and the tier are in the body of M1a only where the run holds them; that a letter of another script that looks like a Latin one is not told apart; that the form of a path lives in `factory-core` with the encoder; and the tests of 19.4. |
| D-34 | What a reference is in the repository instructions and how the instruction set is built from them (AG-2b), a question that the work on D-26 turned up. AG-2a took `AGENTS.md` "and the files it references", within "a size cap", and said neither what a reference is nor what the cap is, and M1a is the first milestone in which the host builds the set (22). An instruction reference is a link destination or the whole text of a code span that names a file of the tree at the policy commit. The factory repository's `AGENTS.md` holds 35 different code spans and no link, and seven of them are the path of a file. Links alone were not taken: its set would have been `AGENTS.md` until the file was rewritten, and the pattern `/GLOSSARY.md` of BOOT-6 would have admitted nothing. A bare path in the prose was not taken, since nothing marks its end, and nothing in a code block counts. An instruction reference is always one file. A name that is a pattern or a directory, as `docs/research/NN-*.md` and `docs/adr/` are, is none and is not expanded: patterns belong to policy, and an expanded name would let a later file join the set with no change to a file of the set. A code span is a path from the repository root and a link destination is relative to the file that holds it, or from the root with a leading `/`. The second is how GitHub reads a relative link, by its documentation. GitHub makes no link of a code span, and the first is how the files are written: `docs/agents/domain.md` names `GLOSSARY.md` from the root in a code span. One reading from the root for both was not taken, because a link between two files of `docs/` would then name nothing or the wrong file. A path with `..` stays rejected, as AG-2a had it. There are two caps, 32 KiB for a file and 64 KiB for the set *(tunable)*, the numbers of SEC-4, counted in bytes of the blob as stored. A cap for the set alone, and one for a file alone, were not taken. A file that an instruction reference names and that is over a cap is left out whole, and the walk goes on, so a smaller file named later can still join; no file is cut. An `AGENTS.md` that is over the cap for a file makes the policy invalid (16.3) and blocks onboarding (20.1), and so does one that is missing or is a symbolic link: no session runs without repository instructions or on half of them. A set without `AGENTS.md`, with a warning, was not taken. The cost is that a merge which grows `AGENTS.md` past the cap pauses new dispatch on the repository. Nothing else is an error, and nothing about an instruction reference is a warning. A name that is no file is no instruction reference and is not reported, since a code span that names no file cannot be told from a command. An instruction reference whose file was left out is listed with the reason, in the journal whenever a run takes a policy commit and by `sf policy check` (CLI-5); `AGENTS.md` naming `SPEC.md` without the policy admitting it is the intended state. The files that `AGENTS.md` names are level 1 and those that a level 1 file names are level 2, where the walk ends; a file is in the set once. A name is looked up by its exact bytes and the admitting pattern folds (VAL-5d). An inline or a reference-style link counts, and an image or an autolink does not; a fragment and a query are cut off and percent-escapes decoded. HTML comments are stripped before the instruction references are read, and a comment is what CommonMark reads as one. The Markdown is read by `pulldown-cmark` 0.13.6, at an exact pin and without its default features, in `factory-core` (19.2). It resolves with three more crates, `bitflags`, `memchr` and `unicase`, and its licence is MIT. A scanner of the factory's own was not taken: a code span and a link are what CommonMark says they are. In a run of the crate on two samples (P-31), a name inside an HTML comment or a code block was reported as neither form, an image and an autolink were told apart from a link, and a destination came back with its fragment and its escapes, which the host therefore cuts and decodes itself. A `<!--` in a code span came back as a code span, and a comment block that nothing closed ran to the end of the text. `GLOSSARY.md` gains three terms: repository instructions, instruction set and instruction reference. The bare word stays with GH-5a, where a reference is a mention of an issue or a pull request. M1a builds the set, and the scratch repository gets an `AGENTS.md` for its run (BOOT-6). Eighteen things were not asked of the operator when this was written. The operator was asked about them on the same day and confirmed them, with one change: a comment block that nothing closes draws a warning and is not only listed. A list that is only information gives nobody a reason to read it, and a stray `<!--` at the start of a line takes the rest of a file away. An invalid policy was not taken for it, since the author may have meant the comment. The eighteen: the id AG-2b; that the run of the crate is a probe of its own, P-31; that `AGENTS.md` is the file of that exact name at the root, in that letter case; that a file which is not valid UTF-8 is left out, and that such an `AGENTS.md` makes the policy invalid, which is one more way for a merge to pause dispatch; that the caps are numbers of the specification and not keys of policy or of operator configuration; that an HTML comment is what CommonMark reads as one and not every `<!--` in the text, that a block is stripped whole, and that a comment which runs to the end of a file is listed, which is the point that changed; that an indented code block counts as a fenced one does; that a symbolic link is an instruction reference which is never admitted, and that a submodule is none; the order of the five tests of admission, that the first which fails is the reason given, and that a file which fails one of the first three is not read; the order in which a link destination is read, and that one of which nothing is left is none; that a `.` component is dropped and a leading `/` in a code span changes nothing; that nothing is followed from a file which was left out; that the journal records the set whenever a run takes a policy commit; that the body of an onboarding pull request lists the set; that a file is read as CommonMark without extensions, so not as GitHub's own dialect; that building the set is a pure function of `factory-core`, given the tree as a lookup that `factory-git` answers; the sentence added to 20.4; and the tests of 19.4. 20.1 made a missing `AGENTS.md` a blocking item in step 2 and had step 3 write a skeleton for one. The operator took the skeleton out, on the same day: a repository without an `AGENTS.md` is not onboarded, because a skeleton would make the policy valid while every session ran on next to no instructions. Left open: what SEC-4 removes from a snapshot, which says neither what a comment is nor which characters it means (D-35). D-35 has since changed what a comment is, for the instruction set as for a snapshot. |
| D-35 | What the sanitisation of a snapshot removes (SEC-3, SEC-4), which D-34 left open. SEC-4 removed "HTML comments and zero-width or bidirectional control characters" and capped the size, and said neither what a comment is, nor which characters those are, nor what happens at a cap. M1a is the first milestone that gives an implementer a snapshot (22). The aim is that an agent reads no text which GitHub's page hides from the approver, pursued for HTML comments and for characters that nobody sees, and that the agent loses text where the host's reading and the page's cannot agree. An HTML comment is found in two steps. CommonMark says where the raw HTML of a body or of a comment on the issue lies, read by `pulldown-cmark` with its table extension on. The HTML standard says what an HTML comment in it is: the text from `<!--` through the next `-->` or `--!>`, and the forms `<!`, `<?` and `</` before anything but an ASCII letter, each through the next `>`. A `<!--` in a code span, in a code block or in plain text stays. An HTML comment that nothing closes takes everything through the next close in raw HTML, or to the end of the text. The reading of AG-2b was not taken for a snapshot: an HTML block that begins with `<!--`, taken whole, and inline HTML from `<!--` to `-->`. In a probe of 71 texts (P-32) it left text that the page hid in 25, among them an HTML comment on a line inside a `<div>` block and everything after a `<!--` there that nothing closed, and it removed text that the page showed in 5, such as what follows a `-->` on its line. A reading by the characters alone was not taken, as in D-34: an issue of the factory repository that writes `<!-- factory:state` in a code span would lose the rest of its body. A sketch of the rule taken agreed with the page on 65 of the 71. In 5 it left text that the page did not show and that is no HTML comment, and in one it removed text that the page showed. AG-2b takes the same rule through the same function, which changes that part of D-34. The warning that the operator chose there stays, now for any HTML comment that nothing closes within its HTML block. Some text that the page does not show as text stays in a snapshot: a link reference definition, the title and the address of a link, the alternative text and the title of an image, and an attribute of a tag of raw HTML. Removing a definition would break the links that use it, and a snapshot is untrusted data to an agent in any case (10.4). The characters removed are every code point with the property Default_Ignorable_Code_Point, from `icu_properties` at the pin of 19.2, and no other. In that version these are 4,174 code points, 3,769 of them unassigned, and they hold every code point of Bidi_Control, Join_Control and Variation_Selector and the tag characters. The set of GH-5c was not taken, since it holds the line feed, the tab and the spaces that have a width. A control character stays, since the page showed the ones tried. No exception keeps a joiner or a variation selector inside an emoji, which would keep a channel that can carry bytes nobody sees. The HTML comments are removed first and the characters after them, in one pass: a zero-width space inside `<!--` made it text on the page. The title loses the characters alone, since the page showed a `<!--` in a title as text. A size is counted in bytes after the removal. The caps stay 32 KiB for the body, 8 KiB for a comment and 64 KiB for the total *(tunable)*, and nothing is cut at one. An issue whose body is over its cap is not claimed, from M1a on; 6.4 had the readiness lint fail it, and section 22 enforces the lint from M3 only. A comment over its cap is left out whole, with a line of the host's in its place. The title and the body are always in, and the comments fill what is left of the total from the newest back, as an unbroken run: the first that does not fit ends the walk, and the older ones are one count. Cutting a comment was not taken, and neither was refusing the issue for one: a comment can arrive in mid-run, and a pasted log should not stop a resume. A walk that goes on past a comment that does not fit, as that of AG-2b does, was not taken. A comment that is minimised on GitHub is not in the snapshot (SEC-3). `snapshot_sha256` is taken over the snapshot as sanitised, and `title_body_sha256` over the title and the body as read from GitHub; what a snapshot holds is said now, and the exact bytes are fixed when M1a builds the journal. Removed HTML comments and characters draw no warning, and the journal counts their bytes. An HTML comment that nothing closes draws a warning, and so does a comment that was left out for its size. M1a builds SEC-4 whole, as pure functions of `factory-core`, and SEC-3 with the permission check for each distinct comment author. A snapshot of title and body alone until M2 was not taken. The operator was asked each of these points in two rounds on 2026-11-22 and took every one as it was put. Eight things were settled in the writing and not asked. `<!-->` and `<!--->` are each a whole HTML comment and `</>` is removed as it stands, as the HTML standard has them and as the page showed. One of the three other forms that no `>` closes ends with its HTML block, as the page showed for each of the three. An HTML block is read by the characters and a tag of inline HTML is kept whole, so a `<!--` inside an attribute value of a block opens an HTML comment for the host alone; that was the one text of the 71 in which the rule removed what the page showed. The marker of a block quote or the indentation of a list item before a line of raw HTML is no part of the raw HTML, and it goes with an HTML comment only where that is open across it. From M3 the readiness lint reports a body over its cap on the issue, as 6.4 had it, and the claim ends by itself only before that. The warning is for an HTML comment that its own HTML block does not close, also where a later `-->` closes it, and not only for one that runs to the end; the operator was asked about one "that took text with it". A table cell beyond the columns of its table, which the page drops, stays in a snapshot with the other text that the page does not show; it was found after the operator answered. And the field `isMinimized` was observed on the scratch repository in place of the new probe that the operator was told of. Left open: what a run does when a later snapshot of it has a body over the cap, and what the daemon tells the operator of an issue that it does not claim for that reason before M3, which is D-36; the bytes of the two hashes, with M1a; who edited a comment, which nothing checks and probe P-8 left open; and a comment that another account minimised, which P-32 did not try. |
| D-37 | Where the `CODEOWNERS` entries lie, where they stand in the file and how they are checked (VAL-3a), which D-26 left open and probe P-28 turned up. The operator decided four points on 2026-11-22, on what the probe had observed. The entries go into the file that GitHub reads: a repository that has a `CODEOWNERS` file under `.github/`, at its root or under `docs/` keeps the one GitHub uses, and a repository without one gets `.github/CODEOWNERS`, which is the factory repository's case (BOOT-6). A fixed `.github/CODEOWNERS` was not taken, because a new file there silences a file that a repository already has in a later place, and the file at the root was not taken, because a file under `.github/` takes its place. The entries are the last lines of the file. A later line decides the owner of a path, and a line without an owner leaves it with none, so an entry of the repository's own that followed them could take a protected path from the operator. A file that holds nothing but these entries was not taken, since a target repository may have entries of its own, and neither was leaving the rule to M5. A `?` of a pattern is written as `?`. In the file it stands for one byte, so a path with a character outside ASCII in its place has no owner, and that gap is accepted with the gap in letter case and normal form of D-26. Writing `*` in its place was not taken: the gap in letter case is open to any path already, so closing this one alone protects nothing more. The errors endpoint is read as a second check, by `sf repo check` from M5 and by the session at the onboarding of the factory repository, and never in place of the comparison of text: it was silent on a `#` that cut an entry down to `**/` without an owner. The operator was not asked about five things: that a backslash is written before a space, a tab and a `#`, which is the only form observed that owns the one name; that a pattern with a line feed or a carriage return has no entry and is reported by the comparison; that the comparison allows no line among those that end the file but the entries of VAL-3a; that `sf repo check` reports every error the endpoint lists; and that the onboarding pull request of the factory repository is not merged while the endpoint lists an error for its head. |
| D-38 | How many GitHub Apps the factory has, how and when each is created, and which probes M0 owes (GH-11a, GH-11b, 23.2). 5.7 had the App created by `sf app create`, the roadmap put "GitHub App creation" in M0 without saying whether it meant the command or the act, and 19.4 named a test App that nothing else knew. There are two Apps: the factory's App, whose private key is on the server only, and the scratch App, installed on the scratch repository alone, which is also the test App of 19.4. One App was not taken: a key belongs to an App and not to an installation, so the key in the development VM could have written to the factory repository from M1b on (BOOT-2, DEV-4). The factory's App may be installed on the scratch repository as well, which is allowed and not required. M0's exit owes the probes that a rule of M0 or M1 depends on, and every other unanswered probe carries the mark of the milestone whose rules first read its answer (23.2). | D-40 | What the bubblewrap backend is where the sandbox and the proxy meet: the forwarder inside the sandbox, what the proxy speaks, the seccomp filter and the sandbox's `/tmp` (SEC-6c, SEC-6e, SEC-10a, SEC-10b). 13.3 and SEC-10 named a forwarder, a proxy and a filter and specified none of them, and M0's prototype of the sandbox and its prototype of the proxy meet exactly there (finding B2 of the review of draft 4). The forwarder is `sf` itself. bubblewrap starts a hidden subcommand of the binary as the first process of the sandbox. It listens on the loopback interface before the command exists, copies bytes to the proxy's Unix socket, starts the command as the leader of a group of its own and passes the signals of the stop sequence on to that group (SEC-10a). `socat` from the OS baseline was not taken: it would be a requirement on the server, in the development VM and on the hosted runners, something would still have to start it beside the command and wait for its port, and it relays no signal, while bubblewrap passes none on and a SIGTERM to `bwrap` ends the sandbox at once. The proxy reads one request from a connection. It allows a `CONNECT` to a listed name on port 443, which it tunnels unread, and it refuses everything else with a reason and a record in the denied-request log (SEC-10b). A check of the TLS server name was not taken, and neither was the forwarding of plain HTTP: the first narrows the abuse of a shared address and not that of the model host, and puts a parser for a worker's bytes into the control plane, and no listed host needs the second. The filter is a deny-list: the one that Flatpak hands to bubblewrap, with the calls that Flatpak leaves open in a development run and with rows of the factory's for `io_uring`, `bpf`, `userfaultfd`, the two `process_vm` calls, every namespace flag of `clone` and the x32 ABI (SEC-6e). The allow-list of Moby's default profile was not taken. It refuses a call that the kernel gains later, which a deny-list does not (23.3), and it costs two long lists to keep and builds that fail on a missing call in a way that is hard to read. Level `process` is for attended runs, and the daemon runs at `hardened` (D-7). `seccompiler` compiles the filter (19.2). `libseccomp` was not taken: it is a C library that every machine which builds `sf` would need, the release job included, and the server at run time. The first process lies in `factory-sandbox`, as the half of the bubblewrap backend that runs inside the namespace, and it needs no code of the proxy: `factory-daemon` hands it the path of the proxy's socket in the `SandboxPolicy` (19.1). `factory-proc` was not taken, which would then hold socket code that one backend uses, and neither was `factory-net`, which would have needed `factory-proc` and a change to the table of 19.1. The temporary directory of a stage execution is the sandbox's `/tmp`, and `TMPDIR` holds `/tmp`, so a tool that ignores the variable writes to the same place (SEC-6c). A memory-backed `/tmp` beside it and a sandbox without a writable `/tmp` were not taken. Nothing of this was run. What is said of bubblewrap was read in its manual and source at 0.9.0 and 0.12.0, the filter in Flatpak's source, the alternative in Moby's profile and the crate in its own source, all on 2026-11-26 (`docs/research/19-bubblewrap-backend-sources.md`). What a kernel does with it is the new probe P-37, owed by M0 and answered by M0's prototypes of the sandbox and the proxy. A prototype ahead of this decision was not taken: it would have been that work, done twice. The operator answered seven questions, each as recommended. Two reviewers then read the change, one against the rules for editing this specification and one against the ticket and the answers, and what they found is in the text. Two points differ from an answer. A request head that cannot be read gets the answer of `hyper`, a `400`, a `431` or none, where the answer gave every refusal a `403`: such a head never reaches the proxy's own code. And the filter is four programs on x86-64 and three on arm64, where the answer spoke of a second program for the x32 ABI. Not asked of the operator, of the first process: its name, `sf __sandbox-init`; that it is PID 1 of the sandbox, through `--as-pid-1`, and reaps; the port, 3128; that the supervisor signals it and not `bwrap`, by the pid of `--json-status-fd`; that the sandbox instance names to the stop sequence of `factory-proc` what it signals; that a SIGKILL to it ends the sandbox; the status it exits with; `--new-session`; that the sandbox shows the binary which the host runs; and that `factory-cli` reaches both hidden subcommands through `factory-daemon`. Not asked, of the proxy: one socket for each sandbox instance, at `exec/<seq>/proxy.sock` with mode 0600, and no credential; that no instance is created where the socket's path is too long; the cap on the request head and the two timeouts; the four reasons and their order; that a host which cannot be reached gets `502` and is no denial; what a record of the denied-request log holds; that a name is compared whole and that an IP address is never listed; that every entry has port 443; the lower-case spellings of the three variables and the value of `NO_PROXY`; and that the proxy takes the function which connects to a listed name as a parameter. Not asked, of the filter: the error codes; that a refusal is an error and never a signal, but for a call of another architecture; each row of the factory's; that `ptrace` is refused, so that no debugger works in a worker; that `prctl` and `seccomp` stay allowed; and that the backend compiles a program for each error code and writes the one for the x32 ABI itself. Not asked, of the rest: `exec/<seq>/tmp/` as the directory on the host; the lowest version of bubblewrap in 4.4, which `sf doctor` checks (CLI-1), a floor under a requirement that was there already; that the hosted runners answer part of P-37; the two rows of 19.4; the weak point of 23.3; the glossary term; and the ids SEC-6e, SEC-10a, SEC-10b and P-37. Left open: which pid and group AG-15 journals at level `process`, and how that is done before the command starts, for which bubblewrap has `--block-fd`; how the traits of 13.3 carry the process that the stop sequence signals; what the OS baseline holds, which stays with the code and its snapshot test (SEC-6); where a run keeps the denied-request log (M1a); what starts the command and which filter holds in the guest of level `hardened` (M2); and how a worker reaches the model gateway in `gateway` mode, which is not scheduled.
