Three points differ from an answer, and each came out of the review of this change. The first answer named the previous tree as what a pair is measured against, and WS-12 names the base tree: after a base update that conflicted, the previous tree holds what the merge put together. The second answer spoke of the paths of a round and of a layout in the new tree. WS-13 reads the paths in which a tree differs from the base tree, and a layout in either, so that a run cannot take a layout of the base tree apart and write its `config`. And that answer had every layout of a base tree listed as an advisory item, where one at the root is a blocking item and stops the claim: no run could change a path of such a repository. Not asked of the operator: that the host makes the two tests on the tree of every test-merge and of every base update, and that a rejection there is handled as a conflict (12.3, 12.4); that WS-12 compares whole paths, of entries of every kind, and not the names inside one directory; that a pair is admitted by its two paths in the base tree, whatever the run did to either; that a name which is not valid UTF-8 is folded as VAL-5d folds a path, at the cost of a rejection too many; that the layout is read from names alone, and neither from the content of `HEAD` nor from a mode; that the entries of two directories count as those of one where their paths have the same fold; that the root of a tree counts as any directory does; that a path which a run removes below a layout is rejected like one it adds or changes, and that a run may remove a layout whole and no part of it; that a file outside its directory which a layout of the base tree reads its configuration from is not covered, and that `protected_extra` is the operator's means for it; that the host reads the paths in which two trees differ from the trees, and not from the listing of the staging git; that onboarding lists the pairs and the layouts of a default branch as advisory items; that the feedback of a rejection names both paths of a pair; that the walk of a tree lies in `factory-git`; that the setting is an item of its own, WS-15, and that the provisioning of the server in 4.4 names it; the sentence that INV-11 gains; that a stage which starts again is a new stage execution with a new `<seq>`; that `verify` and `review` never share an export; that every git command of the host names its repository (WS-8); that the option against renames is given although `git diff-tree` detected none without it; the cases that WS-9 gains for the suite of M0, and the tests of 19.4; that the case of 19.4 for a file that moved stands in the Git row and not, as the review proposed, in the row of the path patterns, since it is a case of the diff; that a link under a new name is a row of VAL-5f; the row that the threat table of 13.1 gains and the item of 23.3; the term that `GLOSSARY.md` gains; and the ids WS-12 to WS-15 and P-40. P-40 ran git 2.56.1 on macOS and git 2.47.3 in a Debian container on the same machine. Nothing ran in the factory's sandbox or on the server, and the trees with two paths of one fold were made with `git mktree`. Two reviewers read the change, one against the rules for editing this specification and one against the ticket, the answers and the kept output, and what they found is in the text: the second found the base update and the merge of a branch that is behind, the file added to one of two directories that the base tree holds, the layout taken apart, the layout at the root of a base tree, and what a `config` reads from outside its directory, and that the record said of every layout what the script had run on some, after which the script ran again. Left open: what a program does that reads a repository without the `git` CLI, with the setting or without it; a directory that becomes a layout when something creates an entry that git does not track; a merge that is made between two test-merges, whose tree nothing tests (23.3); the spellings of HFS+ and NTFS, which VAL-5d does not fold; an issue whose tests need such a layout as a fixture, which a round cannot commit; and the export of `review`, which M2 builds. |

Still open:

| # | Decision | Assumed until decided |
|---|---|---|
| D-4 | Which second repository does the factory run on from M2? | To be named |
| D-5 | The microVM monitor: Firecracker or Cloud Hypervisor. | Decided by the M2 prototype (boot time, workspace attachment, guest image build) |
| D-6 | A reviewer from a different model family needs a second subscription and driver. | A different Claude model from the implementer |
| D-8 | Eval trials and budget required per promotion type (binary, prompts, policy defaults, model). | Regression set at one trial for a binary; full suite at three trials otherwise |
| D-9 | `merge.update_when_behind`: `on_conflict` keeps the approval on the reviewed commit but can let a semantic conflict reach the default branch; `always` re-validates every time at the cost of re-approval. | `on_conflict` |
| D-14 | The class ladder, promotion thresholds and audit sampling rate of 13.6, and the limit values of section 14 that D-22 did not set: the `plan` and `review` session spends, the issue limit and its grant, the daily limits and the eval budget. What the daemon does while usage credits are switched on but not in use (BUD-8). Which `Cargo.lock` the dependency-bump predicate of 13.6 admits (VAL-5e). | The listed defaults; for usage credits, the warning of BUD-8; for the lockfile, none, since no class exists before M4 |
| D-15 | A dispatch schedule that leaves plan capacity for the operator's own interactive use. | None: the factory may run at any hour |
| D-19 | The layout of the factory's own specification from M2: one file, or the living-spec layout of 6.3 with either the existing id prefixes or ids renamed to `R-<CAP>-<NNN>`. With it, whether the policy's `SPEC.md` pattern is anchored (BOOT-4). | As D-18, until decided when M3, which builds spec deltas, is scoped |
| D-36 | What a run does when a later snapshot of it has a body over the cap of SEC-4, which D-35 left open: at a resume (7.5.1), a reset (7.6) or an edit in mid-run (7.7). The claim does not take such an issue (8.2), and nothing says what a run that already exists does. A run that `sf run` resumes from the journal in M1 keeps the snapshot of its run row (8.2, CLI-3), so the question first arises in M2. With it: what the daemon tells the operator of an issue that it does not claim for that reason while the readiness lint is not enforced (22). Decided when M2 builds the resume. | The run goes to `needs-human` with the category `clarification`, as a lint failure does at a resume (7.5.1). Nothing is assumed for what the daemon reports |

### 23.2 Probes

Facts the design depends on that research could not confirm from primary sources. A probe is answered by whatever can observe it: a script, a live session, a workflow run, or the operator on the server. The answer is recorded in `docs/research/probes.md`, and the record says how it was observed. `sf` holds no probe suite (D-39): earlier drafts had `sf doctor --probe` answer each probe against a scratch repository, and many of the answers so far are no question about a repository. A probe that writes to GitHub or reads from it is run on the scratch repository, and never on the factory repository. All are M0 unless marked: M0's exit (22) owes those that a rule of M0 or M1 depends on, however each was answered, and every other probe carries the mark of the milestone whose rules first read its answer (D-38). A probe that needs an App identity is answered with the scratch App (GH-11a). An answer observed with the operator's token stands unless it is about what an identity may do or may read; such an answer is observed again with the scratch App's installation token, and its row says so. An answered probe keeps its row, which then also states the answer.

| # | Question | Design impact |
|---|---|---|
| P-1 | What does creating an existing ref return (409 or 422, and the message)? Answered on 2026-11-20 by calls on the scratch repository, with the operator's token and not an installation token: a 422 with the message `Reference already exists`, whatever SHA the call asks for, under API versions `2026-03-10` and `2022-11-28`. Every fault in the ref name or the object that was tried got a 422 as well, told apart by its message alone, and a branch name that collides with the ref gets the same message as an object that is not a commit. No 409 was seen. Of two creates sent at once, one succeeded and the other got this 422, in each of 40 pairs; neither the answer nor the ref says who created it. Observed again on 2026-11-25 with the scratch App's installation token (D-38): every case that was tried with the operator's token got the same status and message, and of ten pairs of creates sent at once, one request succeeded and the other got this 422. With a ruleset on `factory/**` that restricts creation, the call got a 422 with the message `Reference update failed`, for a ref that exists and for one that does not, and the read of the ref returned 200 for the one and 404 for the other. With a ruleset that restricts updates, or deletions and force pushes, the answers were those without a ruleset. No 409 was seen in 83 creates. An empty repository, a ruleset of an organisation and a classic branch protection were not tried. | 8.2, 8.5, 20.1 |
| P-2 | (M2) Does an approving review from a GitHub App, or from `github-actions[bot]`, count toward required approvals under a ruleset? | Onboarding warning; PR-2 already ignores them |
| P-3 | (Not scheduled) Can an App bot be an issue assignee? | Optional "assigned to the factory" signal |
| P-4 | How does an issue form render into the body? Answered on 2026-11-20 by four issues that the operator created on the scratch repository through the form of 6.2, read back through REST and GraphQL: one block per field in the form's order, each the line `### <label>`, a blank line and the field's text as submitted, with a blank line between blocks. An empty field holds `_No response_`; a field of spaces and line breaks holds those. Nothing is escaped, so a heading typed into a field cannot be told from the form's own, and a carriage return in a field stays in the body. A sketch of the parser of WI-1 read back every field of the two bodies made from ordinary text; in the third, written to look like the form's output, two names repeat (WI-1a); in the fourth, a fence left open in one field takes the next field's heading with it, on GitHub's page as in the sketch. Two lines typed by hand arrived with a line feed between them. | 6.2, WI-1 to WI-1b, 6.4 |
| P-5 | Is a push that changes `.github/workflows/` rejected for an App without the Workflows permission on every push path? Is one accepted that changes a directory of that name below the root (`sub/.github/workflows/`) or in another letter case at the root (`.GitHub/workflows/`), and does GitHub take a file there for a workflow? Does it read a workflow through a symbolic link whose path is `.github` or `.github/workflows`? The three questions that need no App were answered on 2026-11-21 on the scratch repository, with the operator's token and not an installation token. GitHub took a file for a workflow only at `.github/workflows/<name>` at the root, in that letter case: a workflow under `sub/.github/workflows`, under `.GitHub/workflows` or under `.github/Workflows` started no run on a push and was not registered. With `.github/workflows` a link to a directory that held a workflow, and with `.github` such a link, no run started on a push to another branch, on a push to the default branch or for a pull request, while a regular workflow beside each of them ran. A link that was itself a file of `.github/workflows` was registered as a workflow, and its run failed before any job. The App part was answered on 2026-11-25 with the scratch App's installation token. GitHub refused the App every push that adds or changes a file under `.github/workflows/` at the root: a workflow, a file that is none, an empty file, a file one directory further down and a link that is a file of the directory, to a new branch and to an existing one. It refused the addition of a workflow through the contents endpoint and the tree endpoint of the REST API, with a 403. It accepted a push that only removes a workflow or changes its mode, and a push that leaves the workflows as they are, to a branch that the App had created at a commit with a workflow in its tree. It accepted a push of a workflow under `sub/.github/workflows`, `.GitHub/workflows` or `.github/Workflows`, and a push that makes `.github/workflows` or `.github` a link, and none of those five started a run. A push of two commits was accepted where the first adds a workflow and the second removes it, and the App could then put no ref on the first. It could put a ref on a commit of the operator's that adds a workflow once that commit was the head of a branch, and merge such a branch, and the workflow ran. The events `schedule`, `workflow_dispatch` and `pull_request_target` were not observed. | GH-11; defence in depth for 11.3; the reach of the workflow rule (VAL-5e); what a link at those names draws (VAL-5f) |
| P-6 | Does the agent CLI's inner Bash sandbox work inside the outer bubblewrap sandbox and inside the microVM, or must it be disabled? Answered on 2026-11-20 for bubblewrap, on the server as the factory's user, with a stand-in for the factory's sandbox and Claude Code 2.1.311. It works where the stand-in lets a process create a user namespace, once `socat` is installed: the CLI's sandbox nested and confined the command further. Under `--disable-userns` each command failed, or ran unsandboxed when the model asked for that. Without `socat` the CLI warned on stderr and ran every command unsandboxed. It does not have to be disabled: it is off unless a settings file turns it on, and with it off the Bash tool ran in the CLI's own namespaces, under `--disable-userns` too. `sandbox.enabled: false` in the host's `--settings` file is the setting, and it turned the sandbox off when the config directory had turned it on. The microVM was not tried (M2), and AG-8 does not wait for it. | AG-8, SEC-6b |
| P-7 | Does sandboxed staging with a quarantine object directory and host-side ingest behave as specified on the hostile-tree suite, and what is the exact ignore-rule mechanism? Answered on 2026-11-19 by a prototype of the git commands on git 2.47.3 and 2.56.1, without the factory's sandbox: it does, once 9.4 names the commands and their order. The ignore rules are the workspace's `.gitignore` files at capture, read by `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard`. In-tree attributes have to be switched off at checkout, staging and export alike. | 9.4, WS-1, WS-9, WS-11 |
| P-8 | Who is reported as the editor of an issue title and body through the API? Is `lastEditedAt` set for API edits, and does a title change always produce a rename event? Answered on 2026-11-20 by edits of six issues on the scratch repository, through REST, GraphQL and the web interface, by the operator and by a workflow as `github-actions[bot]`; the factory's own App does not exist yet. The three paths report alike. A body edit sets `lastEditedAt` and `editor` and adds an entry to the edit history, which names the editor of every edit; `editor` names the last one only. A title edit moves none of these and produces a rename event that names its actor: one for each of 32 changes, those of case or of trailing spaces alone included. Sending or saving a value the issue already has leaves no trace. Every time is a whole second, and a body edit sent just before a `labeled` event, or at once with it, can carry that event's time. An App is a `Bot` with the login `github-actions` in GraphQL and `github-actions[bot]` in REST, under one numeric id. The reads were observed again on 2026-11-25 with the scratch App's installation token (D-38), on which SEC-1 and SEC-2 rest: on the six issues, and on one that the App filed, it read the same `editor`, `lastEditedAt`, edit history and events as the operator's token, in each of 21 pairs of responses, and it read the permission endpoint of SEC-1. The scratch App is named as the Actions App is: a `Bot` whose login has the suffix `[bot]` in REST and not in GraphQL, under one numeric id, which is not the App's id. | 5.3 rule 6, 7.5, 7.7, SEC-2 |
| P-9 | (Not scheduled) Do blocked-by dependencies work across repositories, and what is the per-issue limit? | 5.3; cross-repository work is out of scope for v1 regardless |
| P-10 | (M2) Do comment edits count toward the content-creation rate limit? | GH-16 budget |
| P-11 | (M2) Does `merge-async` behave as documented on a personal-account repository, including `merge_action` and the custom commit message? | 12.3 |
| P-12 | (M2) Does a base-merge commit pushed by the App dismiss approvals under "dismiss stale approvals" or "require approval of the most recent push" when the pull request diff is unchanged? | 12.4; decision D-9 |
| P-13 | Cold versus seeded build time and size for the factory repository. The operator records what `sf sandbox exec` reports in steps 5 and 6 of M0's exit check on the server (BOOT-3, CLI-6): the cold build, and the same build in a second clone on the target directory that the first left. | 9.3 |
| P-14 | (M2) Is `current_user_can_bypass` populated for installation tokens on the ruleset objects that contribute to the default branch's rules? Seen in passing on 2026-11-25 (P-1): on a ruleset of the scratch repository for `factory/**` with no bypass actor, the scratch App's token read `current_user_can_bypass: never` and no `bypass_actors` key. A ruleset that names a bypass actor, and one that contributes to the default branch's rules, were not read. | DEP-4 |
| P-15 | Which encodings suppress mentions, issue references and closing keywords in comments, pull request bodies and commit messages? Answered on 2026-11-21 by text written to the scratch repository in seven encodings, by the operator and by a workflow as `github-actions[bot]`, and read back as rendered HTML, timeline events and the operator's notifications; the factory's own App does not exist yet. In a comment, an issue body and a pull request body a backslash escape and a character reference suppress nothing: the mention notified, the references were recorded and the pull request closed its issue. A fenced block and a code span suppress all three in a comment, an issue body and a pull request body, as long as the text does not end the fence. A fence suppresses nothing in a commit message, where the commit closed its issue. A zero-width joiner or space after the `@`, the `#` or the hyphen of `GH-` suppresses all three on every surface. An issue URL is matched by its path, `owner/repo/issues/<n>`, with or without a scheme and a host, and a zero-width space inside the path stops it. A closing keyword never acted without a live reference directly after it. Text set by an edit acts as text written new. A mention and a `#<n>` in an issue title did nothing. In a commit message `&#<n>;` is a reference to issue `<n>`. No notification from a commit message was seen, and a push with the workflow's token left no reference events. | GH-5a, PLAN-5, 9.4 |
| P-16 | Can GitHub-hosted Linux runners run the sandbox tests (unprivileged user namespaces for bubblewrap, `/dev/kvm` for the microVM)? Answered on 2026-11-19 with stand-in commands, not the factory's sandbox: runners of both architectures run bubblewrap once a setup step lifts Ubuntu's AppArmor restriction, and on Ubuntu 24.04 also in a network namespace without egress, but not in a container under Docker's default confinement. `/dev/kvm` is present on the x86-64 runners only; booting a microVM there was not tried. | 19.4, DEV-5 to DEV-7 |
| P-17 | (M3) Do issue type and issue field changes expose an actor and a timestamp? | SEC-1; use of types and fields |
| P-18 | (M4) For A3: does a ruleset accept zero required approvals with required code-owner review, and does an unowned CODEOWNERS pattern lift the requirement? Is a pull request held to the `CODEOWNERS` file of its base branch, and does the required review read an entry as the page of a file does (P-28)? The alternative is a fourth factory-reported check `factory/approval`. | 13.6, VAL-3a |
| P-19 | (Not scheduled) Can an installation token list merge-queue refs and entries, and which permission does that need? | PR-6 |
| P-20 | (M2) Is the push of a base-update merge commit (12.4), or the ref update of a reset (7.6), rejected for an App without the Workflows permission when the default branch changed a file under `.github/workflows/` since the old `base_sha`? Seen in passing on 2026-11-25 (P-5): GitHub accepted the scratch App's merge, through `POST /merges`, of a branch on which the operator had pushed a workflow, and a ref of the App's at such a commit once it was the head of a branch; it refused the App a ref at a commit with a workflow that no branch has at its head. The push of a merge commit and the ref update of a reset were not tried. | 12.4, 7.6, GH-11 |
| P-21 | In non-bare `claude -p` with `--setting-sources user` and `--strict-mcp-config`, which of these still load from the working directory: `CLAUDE.md`, `CLAUDE.local.md`, skills, commands, subagents, plugins, hooks? Is there a flag or setting that disables each? Answered on 2026-11-19 by live sessions of Claude Code 2.1.311 on macOS, outside the factory's sandbox: none of them, and neither `AGENTS.md`, `.claude/rules/`, the `env` blocks of the two settings files, the permission rules of `.claude/settings.local.json`, nor anything found in a subdirectory or written mid-session. `--setting-sources user` alone does this, `.mcp.json` included. Second switches exist for instruction files, for skills and commands, for hooks and for `AGENTS.md`; none for subagents or plugins. The per-execution config directory is live configuration: what a session writes there takes effect in that session. | AG-3a, AG-4 |
| P-22 | Under subscription authentication: do `--max-turns` and `--max-budget-usd` work, what usage does the stream report, and what does the CLI emit when a plan limit is reached (event shape and reset time)? Which hosts does the CLI contact? Answered on 2026-11-19 by the same sessions, on a `claude setup-token` token, except for the plan limit, which was not reached on purpose. Both caps work and end the session with `error_max_turns` or `error_max_budget_usd` and exit code 1. The final `result` holds tokens and API-equivalent dollars per model, as running totals for the session; per-call output tokens are in `message_delta` events only. A session that completes emits one `rate_limit_event`, after its first response, with the window, its utilisation, `resetsAt` in Unix seconds and the account's usage-credit state; the event of a rejected request is known from the documentation only. `api.anthropic.com` is the one host a session needs; without `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC=1` the CLI also asks for a Datadog log host. | Section 14, 8.3, SEC-11, 13.5 |
| P-23 | (M4) Can a host-side pass-through supply the subscription credential so that the worker holds none: does the CLI accept a custom base URL under subscription authentication, and is that within the plan's terms? | 13.5; would remove the token from workers before auto-merge |
| P-24 | On the server, as the factory's user: do the bubblewrap tests of P-16 pass, does the kernel accept a seccomp filter, and can the user open `/dev/kvm`? The server's other facts were observed on 2026-11-20 (4.4). These three were not: `bubblewrap` was not installed and the account that ran the script is not in group `kvm`. Answered on 2026-11-20 with the stand-in commands of P-16, not the factory's sandbox: as the factory's user, a member of group `kvm`, all five tests pass with bubblewrap 0.12.0 and no setup step, a sandbox given a seccomp filter runs under it, and `/dev/kvm` opens and answers `KVM_GET_API_VERSION` and `KVM_CREATE_VM`. No guest was booted. | 4.4, 13.3; the exit check of M0 (22) |
| P-25 | Does adding a label that does not exist fail, or create the label? Answered on 2026-11-21 by calls on the scratch repository, with the operator's token, which has admin permission there, and not an installation token: the REST API creates the label, grey and without a description, and applies it. Each of the four calls that take label names did so (add labels, replace the label set, update an issue, create an issue), under API versions `2026-03-10` and `2022-11-28`, and each answered with its ordinary success. `gh issue edit --add-label` refuses with `'<name>' not found`. In the one request that tried it, to the add-label endpoint, a name that differs from an existing label only in letter case applied the existing label and created nothing. Observed again on 2026-11-25 with the scratch App's installation token (D-38), which holds the Issues permission and no admin permission: each of the four calls created the label and applied it, under both API versions, and each applied the existing label when it was sent a name in another letter case, as did a read of a label by its name. With that token the App also listed the labels, created one with a colour and a description, changed it, removed one from an issue and deleted labels. | GH-1, GH-3a, 20.1, D-21 |
| P-26 | (M1a) What a session limit meets on the stream of a headless session. With stream-JSON input, does the CLI's `--max-turns` count start over with each message while its budget total runs on, as the documentation says? What does the stream show of a subagent's model calls: the lines, their token counts, and whether `--max-turns` and `--max-budget-usd` count them? Which fields of a `rate_limit_event` show usage credits switched on, and in use? The first two were answered on 2026-11-21 by live sessions of Claude Code 2.1.323 on macOS, on a `claude setup-token` token and outside the factory's sandbox. The turn count starts over with each message and the budget total runs on over the session; neither cap ends the process. A subagent's calls are `assistant` lines with a `parent_tool_use_id`, whose input and cache counts are real and whose output count is a placeholder, with no `message_delta`. `total_cost_usd` and `modelUsage` include them. The budget cap counts them; the turn cap does not, and a subagent runs on when the main loop reaches it. Left to itself the CLI runs a subagent in the background, which gives one message two results and can let a budget cap pass with no `result` and an exit code of 0; with `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_BACKGROUND_TASKS=1` every subagent ran in the foreground. A foreground subagent's text-only calls are in the stream only with `--forward-subagent-text`. The third is not provoked: it is recorded when a session meets it (BUD-8). | BUD-6, BUD-8, BUD-10, AG-1, AG-11, AG-14 |
| P-27 | Which command lines make git skip its hooks, what does git hand a hook, and does a `PreToolUse` hook of Claude Code refuse such a command in a live session? Answered on 2026-11-21 on git 2.56.1 and Claude Code 2.1.323 on macOS, by a script in throwaway repositories and by hand in the session that wrote the hook. On a commit, `--no-verify`, `--no-verif`, `--no-veri` and `-n`, alone or among other short options, skip the hook; a shorter abbreviation is refused as ambiguous, the last of `--verify` and `--no-verify` wins, and a word that is the value of another option, `-n` after `-m` or `--` after `--mess`, is no flag and does not end the options. `git push --no-verify` skips the pre-push hook, and `-n` there is a dry run. A `core.hooksPath` given through `-c`, `--config-env` or the `GIT_CONFIG_*` variables skips every hook. A hook that is not executable is ignored, with a hint, and the commit goes through. A hook runs at the top of the work tree with `GIT_INDEX_FILE` set, and in a linked worktree `GIT_DIR` too. A relative `core.hooksPath` holds in a linked worktree. The `PreToolUse` hook refused each skip it was given, alone and in a compound command, and let a commit through whose message names the flags. With `"if": "Bash(git *)"` it was not run for `time git …`, `env … git …` or git named by its path; with a filter of its own it refused those. A command inside `bash -c` went through both, and a flag that comes out of a variable skips the hook as one written out does. | DEV-9, DEV-10 |
| P-28 | (M1b) How does GitHub read a `CODEOWNERS` pattern? Does an entry written by the rule of VAL-3a own what its pattern protects: `**/crates/**` a file under `crates/` at the root and under a nested `crates/`, `**/Cargo.toml` the manifest at the root and one in a crate, an entry with a leading `/` the root alone? Is a path that differs from an entry only in letter case owned? Does the file read `?` as one character within a component and `a/**/b` as VAL-5c does, how is an entry written whose pattern holds a space or begins with `#`, and does an entry for a name without a wildcard, such as `**/Cargo.toml`, also own what lies below a directory of that name (D-29)? By GitHub's documentation, read on 2026-11-21, the paths are case-sensitive and the file follows most of the rules of `.gitignore`, without negation and without character ranges. Its sample file shows `**/logs` and `apps/` matching in any directory and `/docs/` at the root only, and has no pattern that ends in `/**` and no bare file name. No API names the owner of a path. Answered on 2026-11-22 on the scratch repository, with 67 variants of the file passed over its default branch one at a time, in 68 commits, each read through the endpoint that lists the file's errors (`GET /repos/{o}/{r}/codeowners/errors`) and through the owner that the page of a file takes from an endpoint of the web interface, on 64 or 65 test files. In each form that was asked about, an entry of VAL-3a owned the files that its pattern matches by VAL-5c: under `crates/` at the root and below it, the manifest at the root and in a crate, the root alone for a leading `/`, and `a/**/b` with none or more components between. It owned more where its last component matches the name of a directory: every file below that directory, so `**/Cargo.toml` owned what lies in a directory named `Cargo.toml`. It owned no path in another letter case or normal form. `?` stood for one byte and so not for `é`. A space ended the pattern and a `#` began a comment wherever it stood, so that `**/#hash.md` became the entry `**/` without an owner, which matched every file, left it without an owner and drew no error; with a backslash before it a space, a tab or a `#` was part of the pattern, which then owned the one name. A pattern copied without the prefix was read from the root alone where it holds a `/` between two components and does not begin with `**/`. A later line decided, and a line without an owner left its files with none. GitHub used the file under `.github/`, then the one at the root, then the one under `docs/`, and found `.GitHub/CODEOWNERS` and `codeowners` too. The errors endpoint named a character class, an unknown owner and a word that is no owner, and nothing for a `#` in a pattern or an entry without an owner; it gave a 404 where no file exists. Not observed: which file a pull request is held to, and whether a required code-owner review reads an entry as the page does (P-18); and whether an installation token may read the errors endpoint (P-33). | VAL-3a, VAL-5c, 20.1, BOOT-6 |
| P-29 | Which spellings of a name does a case-insensitive file system take for one name, which does git, and how does the crate `globset` read a pattern? Answered on 2026-11-21 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, git 2.56.1), by scripts in throwaway directories. APFS took two names for one in each of 21 pairs exactly where they are a canonical caseless match by the Unicode Standard: full case folding (`straße` and `STRASSE`, the long s, the Kelvin sign, the ligature `ﬁ`) and canonical normalisation, and neither compatibility forms nor the Turkic `i`, a zero-width code point or a trailing dot. git folds the letters A to Z in a case-insensitive pathspec and compares every other byte as it is. It refuses a path component that is a spelling of `.git`: in any ASCII letter case always, with a code point that HFS+ ignores under `core.protectHFS`, and in a spelling of NTFS under `core.protectNTFS`; it accepted the same spellings of other names. `globset` 0.4.21 matches `?` against one byte, lets `[!x]` match a `/`, and folds ASCII letters only. A sketch of the matcher of VAL-5c and VAL-5d on ICU4X 2.4.0 gave the expected answer for each of 55 pairs and 32 patterns. A case-insensitive file system other than APFS (ext4 with `casefold`, NTFS, HFS+) was not observed. | VAL-5c, VAL-5d, 9.4, 19.2 |
| P-30 | Does cargo read its configuration through a symbolic link, and how does a diff show a symbolic link? Answered on 2026-11-21 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, cargo 1.100.0, git 2.56.1), by a script in a throwaway directory. With `.cargo` a link to a directory that holds a `config.toml`, and with `.cargo/config.toml` a link to such a file, cargo ran an alias defined there; with no `.cargo`, and with a link to a path that does not exist, it did not, and it gave no warning for that link, nor did a build. `git diff-tree -r --raw --no-renames` lists a link as one entry with mode `120000` on the side that holds it: status `A` for a new link, `M` for one pointed elsewhere, `T` for a file that became a link or a link that became a file, and `D` for a removed one. A tracked directory that became a link is the link's `A` and a `D` for each file that was in it, and a link that became a directory the reverse. A change to the file that a link points at is listed under that file's path alone. Linux, and clippy, rustup and `cargo deny` on a link, were not observed. | VAL-5f, 20.1 |
| P-31 | What does `pulldown-cmark` report for a code span, a link and an HTML comment? Answered on 2026-11-21 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, cargo 1.100.0), by a throwaway package on `pulldown-cmark` 0.13.6 without its default features, on two sample texts and on the factory repository's `AGENTS.md`. A code span came back with its text, also where a run of two backticks held a backtick and where it stood in a table cell. An inline link and the three kinds of reference-style link came back as links, each with its destination as written: with a fragment, a query and percent-escapes, with `..`, and with a leading `/`. An image and an autolink were told apart from a link, and a bare path in the prose was reported as nothing. Nothing inside a fenced or an indented code block was reported as either form, and nothing inside an HTML comment, inline or as a block. A comment block ran through the whole line that held its `-->`, and to the end of the text where nothing closed it. A `<!--` in a code span came back as a code span, and one in the middle of a paragraph that nothing closed as text. In `AGENTS.md` it found 44 code spans, 35 of them different, and no link. Without its default features the crate resolved with `bitflags`, `memchr` and `unicase` alone. Linux, the tables and other extensions of GitHub's dialect, and a text that is not valid UTF-8 were not tried. | AG-2b, 19.2 |
| P-32 | What does GitHub's page hide of an issue body and of a comment, and does a CommonMark reading of an HTML comment hide the same? Answered on 2026-11-22: 71 sample texts were rendered by GitHub's Markdown API in the context of the scratch repository, 23 of them were also read back as the `bodyHTML` of four issues there, and 20 of those as that of a comment, written with the operator's token, all 71 were read back as files of a branch there, and all were read by a throwaway package on `pulldown-cmark` 0.13.6. The API, the issues and the files agreed on every text. The page showed a `<!--` in a code span, in a code block, after a backslash and inside a paragraph that nothing closed, as CommonMark reads them. It hid more than an HTML block that begins with `<!--`: a comment on a line inside a `<div>`, a `<pre>` or a `<span>` block; everything after a `<!--` in such a block that nothing closed, through the next `-->` in raw HTML or to the end; and `<?`, `<!` and `</` before a space, each through the next `>`. It showed the text after a `-->` on its line, and it closed a comment at `--!>`. A `<!-- x -->` after a vertical bar inside a code span of a table cell was hidden, and the crate reported it as inline HTML only with its table extension on. A link reference definition was hidden, a link title and the alternative text of an image were in attributes alone, and a table cell beyond the columns was dropped. A `<!--` in a title was shown as text. ESC, backspace, DEL and U+0085 came back as U+FFFD, and a zero-width space, a direction override, a tag character, a variation selector and a soft hyphen came back as they were. A zero-width space inside `<!--` made it text. The reading of AG-2b before D-35 kept text that the page hid in 25 of the 71 texts, and the rule of SEC-4, as a sketch, in 5, none of them a comment. A comment that its author minimised came back with `isMinimized` true and its body whole, for each of six reasons. An App's token, the page in a browser, a comment that another account minimised and Linux were not tried. | SEC-3, SEC-4, AG-2b |
| P-33 | (M5) May an installation token read `GET /repos/{o}/{r}/codeowners/errors`, and under which permission of the App? The operator's token read it (P-28). If the App's token may not, `sf repo check` reads it with the operator's own credential, as it reads the bypass list (DEP-4). | VAL-3a, 20.1 |
| P-34 | What does cargo read as a dependency in a manifest, and what does a lockfile hold for a package? Answered on 2026-11-22 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, cargo 1.100.0, git 2.56.1), by a script in a throwaway directory, after the Cargo reference was read. cargo read a dependency from `dev_dependencies` and `build_dependencies`, at the top level and under `target`, in a package of edition 2021, and refused those tables in one of edition 2024; the reference names neither. It followed the key `registry-index` of a dependency, which the reference does not name either, to a registry of its own. An entry of `workspace.dependencies` that no member inherits was not resolved. A dependency that a manifest holds as optional is in the lockfile whether or not a feature turns it on, and a feature added to an entry brought a package into the lockfile that no manifest names. A renamed dependency is in the lockfile under the name of `package`. A lockfile of `version = 4` holds one `[[package]]` table for each package: a registry package with a `source` and a `checksum`, a git package with a `source` that ends in `#` and the commit and with no `checksum`, and a package of the tree, or one that a `[patch]` by `path` put in the place of another, with neither. `[replace]` leaves two tables, and an unused patch one under `[[patch.unused]]`. A `[patch]` or a `[replace]` in a member that is not the workspace root was ignored with a warning. With no manifest changed, cargo built the version and the git commit that an edited lockfile named. It dropped, or under `--locked` refused, a package that no manifest asked for and a package from another place than its manifest asked for, and it refused a changed checksum. Where a manifest asked for more than its lockfile held, it stopped under `--locked` and without it fetched and built the missing package. It did not open a `Cargo.lock` in a member's directory. A manifest whose dependency table or entry has another shape did not load. Not run: another registry, a vendored source, the configuration file, a lockfile of an older format, and anything on the server. | VAL-5e, VAL-5g, 20.1; decision D-32 |
| P-35 | Does GitHub hold a release workflow as DEV-12 needs, on a public repository of a personal account? Does an environment that admits tags `v*` alone and names a required reviewer hold a job of a workflow that a tag push started until that reviewer approves, and may the reviewer be the account that pushed the tag? Does a ruleset that restricts the creation, the update and the deletion of tags `v*`, with the repository-admin role as its only bypass actor, refuse such a tag to an App that holds Contents: write, and take it from the operator? Does `gh attestation verify` with `--signer-workflow`, `--source-ref` and `--deny-self-hosted-runners` accept the archive of such a run and refuse one that a workflow built from another ref? To be answered on the scratch repository before the release workflow is merged, the question of the ruleset with the scratch App's installation token. | DEV-12, DEV-13, VAL-7 |
| P-36 | Three facts about Linux that M0 builds on. Does `bwrap --disable-userns` do on the hosted runners of DEV-5, with the bubblewrap that apt installs there and inside the network namespace of DEV-6, what it did on the server (P-6)? In a Lima VM as DEV-3 describes the development VM: do the five tests of P-16 and that case pass, does a session of the agent CLI complete under the stand-in of P-6, and does `/dev/kvm` answer `KVM_CREATE_VM`? Where does the agent CLI read and write on Linux outside what SEC-6 gives it: its temporary files, its socket and the managed settings (P-26, P-21)? Answered on 2026-11-25 by workflow runs on the scratch repository and in a Lima VM on the operator's Mac, with stand-in commands and Claude Code 2.1.326. The option does the same with bubblewrap 0.9.0 on both runner labels, in the network namespace too, in each of 8 jobs; it needs `--unshare-user` by name. The VM passes the tests and the case with bubblewrap 0.12.0 and no setup step, the fifteen sessions of P-6 gave there what they gave on the server, and `/dev/kvm` answers `KVM_CREATE_VM` and `KVM_CREATE_VCPU` for a member of group `kvm`; no microVM was booted in it. On Linux the CLI keeps a subagent's task output and its socket under `TMPDIR`, the socket only where every directory on the way belongs to the user or to root and none can be written by the group or by others without the sticky bit, and a session with a subagent completed with `/tmp` read-only. The CLI looks for managed settings under `/etc/claude-code/`: a settings file planted there took effect under `--setting-sources user`, and not once the stand-in hid the directory. With the login the CLI also fetches the account's managed settings, which were empty, and its policy limits, which were not read. | SEC-6, SEC-6b, SEC-6c, SEC-6d, AG-3a, DEV-3, 19.4 |
| P-37 | Does the bubblewrap backend of D-40 do on a kernel what its sources say? Nothing of SEC-6e, SEC-10a and SEC-10b was run: bubblewrap was read in its manual and source at 0.9.0 and 0.12.0, and the filter in Flatpak's source (`docs/research/19-bubblewrap-backend-sources.md`). The questions are answered in the development VM, with bubblewrap 0.12.0. The first three need neither the network nor the operator's login and are answered on the hosted runners of DEV-5 too, with 0.9.0. The first process: does a program that bubblewrap starts with `--as-pid-1` in a new network namespace bind `127.0.0.1`, and does a connection to that port, passed on to a Unix socket which the sandbox shows, reach a listener on the host? Signals: does a SIGTERM sent to the pid of `--json-status-fd` reach that program, and through it the group of the command it started, while `bwrap` lives on, and does a SIGKILL to it leave no process of the sandbox? The filter: does bubblewrap take one program for each error code through `--add-seccomp-fd`, and does each row of the table of SEC-6e do what it says, the x32 row on x86-64 included? Under the filter: do a cold build of the factory repository and one session of the agent CLI run, and which refused call, if any, does each make? The proxy: does `cargo` fetch from the registry, and does a session of the agent CLI complete, through the variables of SEC-10b where no name resolves inside the sandbox? The temporary directory: does the agent CLI place its socket and a subagent's task output under `/tmp` where `TMPDIR` holds `/tmp` and the directory has mode 0700 (SEC-6c)? Answered by M0's prototypes of the sandbox and of the proxy (22). A session of the agent CLI needs the operator's login, and starting it is the operator's step. | SEC-6b, SEC-6c, SEC-6e, SEC-10a, SEC-10b, AG-12; the exit check of M0 (22) |
| P-38 | Three facts that the check command and the ruleset of DEV-8 rest on. What does `cargo deny` do without the advisory database and without a network, and where does it look for the database? What does `cargo mutants` do on macOS with code that the build leaves out? Does a ruleset's bypass cover every rule of that ruleset, and a rule of a second one? Answered on 2026-11-26, on the operator's Mac and on the scratch repository. `cargo deny` 0.22.1 refuses `check --disable-fetch` as an unknown argument, and with `--offline` it fetches nothing. Without that option it clones the database from `github.com` with `git`, or fetches into its clone, and exits with 1 where it cannot. With `--offline` and no database it exits with 1, and `check bans licenses sources` passes. The database is `advisory-dbs/advisory-db-3157b0e258782691` under `CARGO_HOME`, or under a `db-path` that may name a variable, and a clone that `git clone --depth=1` made under that name was taken. With the root `advisory-dbs` on a file system mounted read-only the run fails at the lock file `db.lock`, and with the database mounted read-only below a writable root it passes. A last fetch older than `maximum-db-staleness` fails the run, and a fetch that fails renews that time unless the time of `FETCH_HEAD` is put back. The 0.18.5 that the Mac holds took the same options and then failed to parse the database of the day. `cargo mutants` 27.1.0 reported the five mutants of a function under `cfg(target_os = "linux")` as missed and exited with 2. On the scratch repository, with the operator's token, a pull request of the bypass actor's merged with no approval and neither required check, under a ruleset that names the repository admin with the mode `pull_request`; a direct update of the branch was refused. With a second ruleset that names no bypass actor the merge was refused, with 405 over REST and with an error of type `UNPROCESSABLE` over GraphQL, until that ruleset's check was green. `GET /rules/branches/{branch}` listed each rule once for each ruleset. Not observed: any of the `cargo` cases on Linux, and an account without a bypass. GitHub's documentation of rulesets was not read: the two pages asked for returned 404. | DEV-2a, DEV-3, DEV-8, DEV-14, VAL-1a, DEP-5 |
| P-39 | Where does rustup put a toolchain, what records its components, and does a toolchain's directory work without rustup? Answered on 2026-11-26 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, rustup 1.29.3), with a rustup home of its own in a scratch directory. For the channels `1.100.0`, `1.100` and `stable` rustup made the directories `1.100.0-aarch64-apple-darwin`, `1.100-aarch64-apple-darwin` and `stable-aarch64-apple-darwin` below the directory `toolchains` of its home, and each held rustc 1.100.0. The file `lib/rustlib/components` of a toolchain held one line for each installed component: `cargo`, `rustc` and `rust-std` with the Mac's triple after the name, `clippy` and `rustfmt` as `clippy-preview` and `rustfmt-preview` with it, a second target as `rust-std-x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, and `rust-src` with no triple. Removing a component or a target took its line away with its files. With the `bin` of the toolchain first on a cleared `PATH` and no rustup, a crate was built, tested, linted and checked by rustfmt in each of four cases: in place, from a copy under another path, from that copy with no write permission on any entry, and through a symbolic link. A `rust-toolchain.toml` beside the crate that named another version changed nothing, and `cargo +1.97.1` was refused as an unknown command. On the toolchain of the minimal profile `cargo clippy` and `cargo fmt` were unknown commands, and a check for a target without its `rust-std` failed. Run in a directory whose `rust-toolchain.toml` named a toolchain that its home lacked, `rustup --version` downloaded and installed it. The six pages of the rustup book that were read name the file's five keys and the grammar of a channel, and say neither where a toolchain lies nor that the `components` file exists. Not observed: Linux, a sandbox, a dated channel, another version of rustup or of the toolchain. | WS-7, 9.5, 16.4 |
| P-40 | Four facts about git that capture, the export and the host's diff rest on. Which directories of a checkout does git take for a repository when it is started in them, what does it run from their `config`, and what does `safe.bareRepository = explicit` change? What does a diff of two trees show for a file that moved? What do the export commands of 9.4 leave when they run a second time on a directory that was written to? What does git make of a tree with two paths that the fold of VAL-5d takes for one, and what does a file system that is not case-sensitive hold of it? Answered on 2026-11-26 by scripts in a scratch directory, on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, APFS that is not case-sensitive, git 2.56.1) and in a Debian 13.8 container on it (git 2.47.3). git took a directory under a name other than `.git` for a repository where it held `HEAD` with `objects` and `refs`, also where those two were symbolic links or regular files of mode `100755`, and where it held `HEAD` with a file `commondir`; on the Mac also under the names `head`, `Objects` and `REFS`. Started there or two directories below, in the work tree or in a clone, it ran an alias of that directory's `config`, and in every layout but the one with `commondir` its fsmonitor on `git status`. It did the same where the `config` was a symbolic link to a file outside the directory or named such a file in `include.path`. At the root of a checkout it took the `.git` instead, and in a copy of that tree without its `.git` it took the root and ran the alias. With `safe.bareRepository = explicit` in the global configuration, in the system configuration or on the command line it refused each layout that it had taken, also where the directory's own `config` set the value back; set in the surrounding repository alone, the setting did nothing. Under it an ordinary repository, a linked worktree, the inside of a `.git` and a bare repository as a remote by path worked as before, and git started inside a bare repository needed `--git-dir` or `GIT_DIR`. `git diff-tree` detected no rename without an option, also with `diff.renames` set, and `git diff` detected them; with detection a moved `clippy.toml`, a moved workflow and a moved symbolic link were one entry each, and without it a deletion and an addition. The export commands, run again on a directory that had been written to, left the changed files and exited with 1, and with `-f` they left a planted file. `fsck --strict` and `index-pack --strict` accepted seven trees with two paths of one fold. On Linux the export held both paths of each. On the Mac it held one file of each pair of files, merged two directories into one with exit status 0, failed on a symbolic link beside a directory, and a clone was dirty from the start; the entries of a layout that lay in two directories, `fix` and `FIX`, became one directory there, whose alias git ran. The factory's sandbox, the server, and a program that reads a repository without the `git` CLI were not run. | WS-8, WS-9, WS-12, WS-13, WS-14, WS-15, VAL-5a, VAL-5d, VAL-5f, 12.4, 19.4, 20.1 |

### 23.3 Known weak points

- Human review is the main defence against a plausible but wrong change, and review quality falls as volume rises. The review-queue limit and the size budget are blunt tools.
- There is one human, so the person who approves the issue is also the person who approves the code, and their own pull requests bypass the ruleset of 20.3. On the factory repository a second ruleset still holds those to CI (DEV-8).
- Reviewer agents are lenient by default and unevaluated until M3.
- The agent CLI's protocol and flags change often. The pinned version range and the contract test contain this but do not remove it.
- In `subscription` mode a token valid for a year sits in the environment of a process that may be subverted, and the CLI runs without bare mode. The mitigations (model-requests-only scope, egress allowlist, literal-token scan, lock-down contract test) reduce this but do not remove it. Bare mode is announced to become the default for `-p`, which may force a change to this mode.
- The plan's limits are shared with the operator's own interactive use and assume ordinary individual usage; a busy daemon may exhaust them and stall both.
- Lights-out rests on class predicates and automated evidence. For user interfaces and service behaviour this specification does not yet define that evidence, so those kinds of work stay reviewed until it does.
- The seccomp filter of level `process` is a deny-list (SEC-6e). A system call that the kernel gains later is allowed until someone adds it to the table, and at that level the host's kernel is all that stands between a worker and the host (SEC-6b).
- A directory with the layout of a git repository runs the commands of its `config` wherever git is started in it. Capture rejects one that a run brings in, and every change below one that the base tree holds (WS-13). One that a human commits is left to a git setting on the operator's machines that nothing checks (WS-15). So is a file outside the directory that a layout of the base tree reads its configuration from, until the operator protects it, and a directory that becomes a layout only when something creates an entry that git does not track. A pair of paths with one fold, or a layout, that a merge puts together from a branch and a default branch that moved is seen by the test-merge of 12.3 and by nothing after it: a merge that the operator makes between two of them is not tested. No program that reads a repository without the `git` CLI was tried, with that setting or without it (P-40).
- Every verify is a cold build. On a large repository this may dominate cycle time until a safe seeded cache exists.
- Most published throughput and quality figures for such systems are self-reported. No target in this specification is derived from them.

## Appendix A. Mapping to prior art

| This spec | OpenAI Symphony | StrongDM Attractor |
|---|---|---|
| Poll / dispatch / reconcile tick (8.3) | Same loop | — |
| `state:*` labels (5.2) | Tracker states Todo / In Progress / Human Review / Merging / Rework / Done | — |
| Status comment (5.4) | Workpad comment | — |
| Typed stages with result contract (7.3, 10.5) | — | Graph nodes with outcomes |
| Plan gate, human review (7.4, 12.2) | Human Review state | Human gate nodes |
| Holdout scenarios (11.7) | — | Scenarios as holdout tests |
| `.factory/policy.toml` (16) | `WORKFLOW.md` | DOT pipeline file |

Symphony's Elixir reference now includes a minimal GitHub Issues adapter (open/closed only, no dependencies); it is prior art for the adapter boundary, not for a label-driven workflow. Fabro (MIT, Rust, Attractor lineage) is worth reading as implementation prior art before M1.

## Appendix B. Research and review

The reports in `docs/research/` hold the evidence and source URLs behind this specification:

- `01-state-of-the-art`, `02-orchestration-architecture`, `03-specs-and-validation`, `04-github-integration`, `05-rust-implementation`, `06-security-governance-ops`: the six research angles.
- `07-gaps`: contradictions between the reports and how they were resolved. Where this specification departs from one of its resolutions, 23.1 or 13.6 records why.
- `08-fact-check`: 66 load-bearing claims checked against primary sources (56 confirmed, 9 partly, 1 wrong; corrections are applied here).
- `09-spec-review`: the 59 findings of the adversarial review of draft 1, each with the reviewer's proposed fix and the sceptic's verdict. This draft addresses each one, not always with the proposed fix.
- `10-spec-recheck`: the 47 problems a recheck found in draft 2 (unresolved findings and contradictions the rewrite introduced), addressed in draft 3.
- `14-server-facts`: what the operator's server is, observed on 2026-11-20 and set against the host requirements of 4.4. Reports 11 to 13 are on `research/*` branches and are cited from `probes.md`.
- `15-session-sizes`: how large the operator's interactive sessions on the factory repository were, in model calls, minutes and API-equivalent dollars, measured on 2026-11-21 for the session limits of section 14 (D-22).
- `16-spec-draft-4-review`: the 51 findings of a review of draft 4 as it stood on 2026-11-22, in four lenses (consistency, evidence, security, and whether M0 and M1 can be built from the text), each with the reviewer's proposed fix. No second reader judged them. B1, B5 and B15 are addressed and E4 in part (D-39), E5, E7 and E8 by probe P-36, B2 by D-40, B6, C1 and C2 by D-41 with probe P-38, B3 and B4 by D-42 with probe P-39, and E3, S2, S5 and S6 by D-43 with probe P-40; the others are not yet.
- `17-scratch-app`: the scratch App's id, slug and installation id and where its key lies, with the checklist of its creation set against what GitHub returned on 2026-11-25 (GH-11a, GH-11b).
- `18-release-tooling`: what the book of `cargo-dist` and the manual of `gh` say about the release workflow and the verification of an attestation, read on 2026-11-25 and not observed (DEV-12, DEV-13, D-39).
- `19-bubblewrap-backend-sources`: what the manual and the source of bubblewrap, Flatpak's seccomp filter, Moby's default seccomp profile and the crate `seccompiler` say on the points that the bubblewrap backend rests on, read on 2026-11-26 and not run (SEC-6e, SEC-10a, SEC-10b, D-40).
